FROM THE MOLECULE TO THE CELL I

Lucía Reed López

[PDF p. 2]

Lecture 1.2. Light and electron microscopy

History

The first magnification lenses were developed in the 11th century. The invention of the microscope wasn’t done by only one person, it received many contributions, but a name which stands out the most is the one of Robert Hooke, who in 1665 developed a microscope with 50X magnification.

Regular light microscope

Cells are very small and translucent, so to be able to see them we need a high magnification

and to create contrast.

Magnification : light microscopy can magnify samples UP TO 1500X, but no more. Therefore, it allows us to observe mitochondria and lysosomes (using the proper contrast), but not ribosomes.

  • Contrast : to produce said contrast, light microscopy uses differential staining such as Hematoxylin-Eosin (H & E) staining, commonly used in pathology labs to differentiate tumors in a tissue. But this method doesn’t allow us to visualize living cells. This can only be achieved by creating contrast using special optical systems, without staining, such as the phase contrast microscope.

Phase contrast microscope

Based on phase (wave) shifts when light passes through different objects. It alters light pathways and produces contrast, allowing us to observe many cell processes.

Fluorescence microscope

It allows us to differentiate many different structures in living cells, even antibodies (immunofluorescence). Excitation light is irradiated on the sample from the light source, passing through the excitation fiber and onto the dichroic mirror (which reflects blue light but not green light, so it can pass through) and the emission filter. Excitation light (blue, 450 landa) is irradiated and green light (520 landa) is emitted. Dichroic mirror: its function in microscopy is to reflect the excitation wavelength, but allow the passage of the emission wavelength.

Transmission electron microscope (TEM)

Consists not of light, but a beam of electrons passing through the sample. There has to be a vacuum so the electrons aren’t scattered by particles in the air. The result is observed through a computer, and samples are observed in very thin sections. Contrast is generated using uranium citrate and lead citrate. The differential absorption of these heavy metals gives us the contrast. Bright areas correspond to electron lucent parts of the sample, while darker areas are electron dense (the beam of electrons bounces off because they are repelled).

[PDF p. 3]

Images always appear in black and white. TEM allows us to see cellular organelles, such as the cilium. It can be used to diagnose dyskinesia, a condition in which the central pair of microtubules is missing. Proteins can’t interact and therefore function is impaired. It presents symptoms such as infertility, deafness, bronchitis and recurrent infections.

Scanning electron microscope (SEM)

It allows us to observe three dimensional structures. It involves a gold coating, and the electron beam scans the sample and creates a 3D image which is seen on a monitor. Once again, the image is in black and white.

Immunodetection

Antibodies can be labelled by either using fluorochromes (immunofluorescence), enzymes (immunohistochemistry) or gold particles (immunoelectron microscopy).

● Immunofluorescence, when used in cancer treatment, allows us to apply personalized medicine, by studying the markers of malignancy and immune response all in the same sample.

● Immunohistochemical procedures can be used to explore the best treatment for a particular breast

cancer.

[PDF p. 4]

TBL 1.1. Ultrastructure of the eukaryotic cells

Microscope structures

Glycogen is seen as dark accumulations found in the cytoplasm. Abundant in liver cells, called hepatocytes.

-Differences between lysosomes and peroxisomes Peroxisomes have a characteristic round shape and are homogeneous inside the membrane. The round black stuff inside the peroxisome is a crystalloid, which is a precipitation of enzymes that are involved in oxidation and the detoxification of cells. Lysosomes are more electron dense and have more irregular shapes. We can differentiate darker material inside, which corresponds to the substances that are being processed and digested.

-Differences between microvilli and cilia

Cilia are made up of microtubules which can be observed under the microscope, and are longer and less abundant. We can find cilia in cells like the ones in our respiratory tract, preventing particles and pathogens from entering our system. Inside cilia, we find microtubules in a particular disposition. Microvilli can be found in many cells, like intestinal cells called enterocytes, because their main function here is to assist absorption. Inside microvilli, we find actin supporting

its structure.

                      - **Desmosomes**, located between

the membranes of two

neighbouring cells. Its function is to attach these cells, allowing

them to communicate.

                      **Smooth endoplasmic reticulum (SER)** usually

presents as a big accumulation of round tubules, which can sometimes be more elongated depending

on where the cut was made.

[PDF p. 5]

  • Centrioles, when not in cell division, are forming microtubules and controlling the cytoskeleton

Lymphocytes

In this image, we can see a lymphocyte, with a thin ring of cytoplasm and a few mitochondria surrounding the nucleus. T and B lymphocytes are created inside the bone marrow. T lymphocytes mature in the thymus, while B lymphocytes

remain and mature in the bone marrow.

- T lymphocytes a) Cytotoxic T cells, which kill infected or damaged cells. Cytotoxic T cells express a

protein called T-CD8 on their membrane. b) Helper T cells, which orchestrate and regulate the immune response. Helper T cells

express T-CD4 . HIV virus attacks T-CD4 cells because it binds to that protein when infecting a cell, giving way to the appearance of AIDS.

Morphologically, we can differentiate T and B lymphocytes by using immunofluorescence. T-CD4 and T-CD8 are T lymphocyte markers. There are markers on B cells, most importantly, CD20 . But they can’t be differentiated when observing them under the microscope with differential staining.

- B lymphocytes and their differentiation into plasma cells B lymphocytes are produced in the bone marrow and mature there (maturing=express CD20). After they exit the bone marrow, they enter a lymph node. These B cells that exit are called naive B cells, because they haven’t yet been in contact with an antigen. But once in the lymph node, there is an encounter with different antigens. This way, they differentiate into plasma cells, B lymphocytes which change their morphology and function to be able to produce antibodies which correspond to the antigen they were in contact with. Many plasma cells, after differentiating, regress to the bone marrow.

[PDF p. 6]

Plasma cells need to produce lots of antibodies, and therefore when they mature, they develop a large RER which can allow them to produce lots of antibodies (proteins).

*Lymphatic system: Vascular system which drains the interstitial fluid between cells, and which carries white blood cells which carry out this filtration of the blood and fluids. These

white blood cells

Granulocytes Nucleophiles: 3 or 4 lobules Eosinophils: 2 lobules and big granules

[PDF p. 7]

Workshop 1.2. Normal and pathological cells under the microscope

LEUKOCYTES

Granulocytes

- Neutrophils have a size of 10-12nm. We normally find 3500-7000 cells/mm3. They make up 60-70% of leukocytes. They have a phagocytic role. As key morphological features, neutrophils have a multilobular nucleus (3-5 lobules) and contain granules, but these cannot be stained with either basic nor acid dye. - Eosinophils have a size of 10-14 nm. We can find between 150-400 eosinophils/mm3, and they make up around 1-3% of all leukocytes. Their function is to remove parasites and destroy antibody-antigen complexes. As key morphological features, they have a bipolar nucleus and granules which are stained with acidic dye .

- Basophils have a size of 8-10nm. There are normally 50-100 cells/mm3 in our blood and they make up 0.1-1% of leukocytes. As key morphological features, their nucleus usually has an “S” shape that is partially covered by granules which stain blue with basic dyes . They are involved in inflammatory reactions, similarly to mastocytes.

Agranulocytes

- Monocytes have a size of 12-15nm and we can find around 200-800 cells/mm3 in our blood. They make up 3-8% of leukocytes and, as a key morphological feature, they have a kidney shaped nucleus . They differentiate into macrophages when they infiltrate tissues. They also have a phagocytic role and act as antigen presenting

cells.

- Lymphocytes have a size of 7-8nm. There are 1500-2000 cells/mm3 in our bloodstream and they make up 20-25% of leukocytes. Their nucleus occupies almost all of the cell, and their main function is to participate in adaptive immunological responses. They cannot be distinguished morphologically, under the microscope. T lymphocytes are responsible for cell-mediated immunity. B lymphocytes participate in the humoral response, throughout the release of

antibodies.

[PDF p. 8]

ERYTHROCYTES

They have a size of 7um, and there are around 3.9-5.7 million cells/mm3 in our blood. They have a biconcave shape and lack a nucleus . The central part is thinner, explaining why they look pale in the centre when observed under light microscope. Their main function is to deliver oxygen to the cells via haemoglobin.

THROMBOCYTES/PLATELETS

Platelets are produced from very large bone marrow cells called megakaryocytes. As megakaryocytes develop into giant cells, they undergo a process of fragmentation. Platelets are made in our bone marrow. Their function is to form clots and prevent bleeding.

[PDF p. 9]

Lecture 2.2 & 2.3. Proteins I & II

Proteins have many functions, such as catalytic functions, structural, transport, defense,..

Proteins are made up of alpha-amino acids . There are 20 different types of amino acids, which give place to thousands of proteins.

Amino acids share common characteristics:

-They have a central carbon, known as alpha-carbon (sp3 configuration, therefore forms tetrahedral molecules) -Carboxyl group, or alpha-carboxyl group -Amino group -A hydrogen atom -An “R” group, a side chain, which differs between each aminoacid. The simplest amino acid is Glycine, which only has a hydrogen atom in its

side chain.

All amino acids, except for Glycine, are chiral. But all living beings (with exceptions) are made up of the L enantiomer, “L-amino acids”. They can be classified in different groups depending on their ability to interact with water and ionize. These are their structures found at physiological pH (around 7.4)

1. Nonpolar, hydrophobic

Glycine, Alanine, Proline, Valine, Leucine,

Isoleucine and Methionine

They do not like interacting with water, so they tend to be orientated inwards in the tridimensional structure of the protein.

2. Aromatic R groups

Phenylalanine, Tyrosine, Tryptophan

Tyrosine, because it contains an OH group, could react with an acid, like phosphoric acid, and form phosphotyrosine which could affect the tridimensional structure of the protein

and therefore its function.

The characteristic of these aminoacids is that they can absorb UV light, typically light with wavelengths around 275-280nm. The ability for proteins to absorb light comes from the presence of these three amino acids.

[PDF p. 10]

3. Polar, uncharged R groups

Serine, Threonine, Cysteine, Asparagine, Glutamine

These amino acids, due to their side chains, can form hydrogen bonds . In the case of cysteine, it can also form

disulfide bonds .

There are enzymes which phosphorylate serines, others, cysteines,… (serine-kinases, cysteine-kinases,…).

4. Positively charged R groups

Lysine, Arginine, Histidine

Can be found in their ionized form.

5. Negatively charged R groups

Aspartate and Glutamate

Ionization of amino acids

Amino acids can behave like acids or bases, depending on the pH. They can be in a Cation form, Zwitterion state (intermediate state, called isoelectric point, known as pI, a pH value at which the net charge of the molecule is 0, with the amino group positively charged and a negatively charged carboxylic group), or Anion form, when the environment has a very high pH and the amino acid is deprotonated.

How to calculate pI when the side chain is ionizable? -First, identify the Zwitterion, the species that carries net 0 charge -Identify the pka value that defines the acid strength of this zwitterion -identify the pka value that defines the base strength of this zwitterion -Lastly, take the average of these two pKa values, and you will get the pI of the respective

amino acid.

[PDF p. 11]

Protein conformation

Proteins have specific tridimensional structures. The particular conformation will give them their functionality. This folding state is held together by strengths and interactions, such as hydrophobic strengths, hydrogen bonds,… They are weak, but very abundant, which makes them important for the preservation of the structure. We can also find electrostatic interactions (strong interactions between permanently charged groups, such as salt bridges), Van der Waals forces (medium-range weak attraction) and disulfide bridges (covalent).

Many neurodegenerative diseases are caused by the misfolding of one protein. In the case of Alzheimer’s, it’s caused by the accumulation of a protein which forms aggregates, B-amyloid, as well as aggregates formed by the accumulation of TAU protein. In Parkinson’s disease we find the accumulation of alpha-synuclein in the neurons,…

There are 4 different levels of protein structure

1. Primary structure It consists of the amino acid sequence which conforms the protein. The amino acids are bound together through a peptidic bond (covalent bond) formed by C, O, N and H. It is a partial double bond, as there is resonance between C-O and C-N. Therefore, the protein can only rotate around the alpha-carbon (NOT in the peptidic bond).

After many peptidic bonds, we can differentiate the Amino terminal (N-terminal) and Carboxyl terminal (C-terminal). This reaction occurs by condensation and requires energy. This linear sequence starts to fold and form interactions in a regular form, giving way to the secondary structure. This structure will be determined by the organization around the peptide bond and the identity of the R groups.

2. Secondary structure The two main arrangements are the alpha-helix and the

beta-sheets.

Alpha-helix is held together by Hydrogen bonds. Typically, it’s formed by hydrophobic amino acids (for example, proline disrupts the helix).

[PDF p. 12]

  • Beta-sheets, where the protein backbone forms a zig-zag plain, and the R groups protuberate outwards from the sheet. We can find two types: parallel and antiparallel sheets, the last one being more stable. They are held together by hydrogen bonds. Keratin, found in nails, is formed by parallel beta-sheets.

Prion diseases are caused by misfolding of proteins, like the

case of the Cretzfiel-Jakob disease.

3. Tertiary structure It’s the spatial arrangement adopted by the atoms in a protein. All proteins have a tertiary structure, which is held together by those bonds mentioned

earlier.

Motifs are interactions between different segments of the protein. Each motif has a function in the protein. A domain is a region of the protein with a specific conformation which allows a protein to carry out a function. For example, Troponin C, which is a muscular calcium-binding protein involved in muscular contraction, has 2 domains.

 - The structure of **collagen** : Collagen is an important constituent

of connective tissue (tendons, cartilage, bones,…). Each collagen chain is rich in glycine and proline, which intertwine to form superhelical triple helices with very high tensile strength. Vitamin C is the cofactor which allows hydroxylation of proline, and this 4-hydroxyproline found in collagen chains offers more hydrogen bonds. That’s why vitC deficiency

causes scurvy.

                         - The structure of **myoglobin** : Myoglobin is an O2

storage point in the muscle. It needs a prosthetic group to be able to bind to oxygen, a heme group like iron. The prosthetic group needs to be located in a region that avoids iron oxidation; because the internal part of myoglobin is hydrophobic, the hemo group is burried inside the tertiary structure of the protein, and so it’s able to bind to oxygen.

4. Quaternary structure It’s formed by the interaction of different proteins/polipeptidic chains. A classic example is hemoglobin, a protein which transports oxygen in the blood. It needs to be able to bind to the O2 at one place but also release it in another. It has high affinity for oxygen at the lungs but low affinity for O2 in the blood vessels.

EXAM QUESTIONS

Protein conformation can be modified by altering ϕ and Ψ rotational angles

To induce a conformational change, it’d be necessary to change angles of C 𝛼 -C and C 𝛼 -N bonds

[PDF p. 13]

Lecture 2.4. Carbohydrates I: monosaccharides

- General characteristics

-Most abundant biomolecules in nature

-They can be very small (glyceraldehyde) or very big (amylopectin) -They are soluble in water and insoluble in non-polar solvents.

- Functions

-Energy storage (glycogen in animals, starch in plants) -One of our main sources of energy -Structural functions (cellulose, chitin, glycoproteins and glycolipids)

-Precursors for other molecules

-Regulators in cell signaling

- Classification

-monosaccharides

-oligosaccharides (2-20 monosaccharides) -polysaccharides

- Nomenclature and termination

-Termination in “-ose” (generally, doesn’t always apply) -Based on the number of carbon atoms: trioses, pentoses,… -Based on functional groups:

-Aldehydes (aldotriose, aldotetrose, aldohexose,..) -Ketones (ketotriose, ketopentose, ketohexose,…)

- Isomerism in monosaccharides

-Functional isomerism (aldehyde or ketone) -Stereoisomerism (will be found if the molecule contains anomeric carbons)

- Types of monosaccharides Monosaccharides are linear carbon chains with single bonds, which are bound to an alcohol group and a main functional group, either aldehyde or ketone. The ketone is always located in C2. Monosaccharides are reducing agents.

TRIOSES

-Aldehyde: glyceraldehyde (important for our metabolism. Has 1 asymmetric carbon). We can find two stereoisomers: D-glyceraldehyde and L-Glyceraldehyde, depending on if the OH group is found to the right (D) or left (L). The carbon which is further away from the main group is the one that determines the D or L nomenclature. In our metabolism, we can only process D-glyceraldehyde to obtain energy, because our enzymes are built to recognize the

D isomer.

These molecules are enantiomers between them, as they are a mirror image of each other.

-Ketone: dihydroxyacetone

[PDF p. 14]

TETROSES

-Aldehyde: D-Erythrose, D-Threose. L-Erythrose would have all the substituents on the opposite side, making it the mirror image of D-Erythrose

-Ketone: D-Erythrulose

PENTOSES

-Aldehyde: D-Ribose, very important as it is found very commonly in our body (ex: DNA). All its carbon atoms are found to the right. D-Xylose

-Ketone: D-Ribulose, D-Xylulose.

HEXOSES

-Aldehyde: D-Glucose (all of the OHs to the right, except C3), D-Galactose, D-Mannose

-Ketones: D-Fructose

- Monosaccharide cyclization In aqueous solution, monosaccharides with 5 or more carbons form cycles. In case of D-glucose, oxygen from C1 attacks C5, forming an hexagon through the hemiacetal bond (in the case of ketones, it would be an hemicteal bond). New stereoisomers now appear, based on the orientation of the alcohol around the carbon: beta anomer (up, same direction as CH2OH) or alpha anomer (down, opposite direction from CH2OH). Six-membered cyclic monosaccharides are known as pyranoses. Five-membered cyclic monosaccharides are known as furanoses.

Monosaccharides are reducing agents . Glucose oxidase oxidizes glucose in the presence of O2, releasing H2O2. The amount of glucose in blood can be measured by observing the amount of H2O2.

[PDF p. 15]

Lecture 2.5. Carbohydrates II: Disaccharides and Polysaccharides

DISACCHARIDES

Disaccharides are formed by 2 monosaccharides joined together by an O-glycosidic bond . This happens between the anomeric carbon of one monosaccharide and any OH group. The formation of the O-glycosidic bond happens by condensation . To break the bond, we would use hydrolysis. When the bond is formed, there is no mutarotation. It loses the capacity to switch between alpha and beta. It’s conformation is fixed.The anomeric carbon of the 2nd monosaccharide still mutarotates (in case of maltose for example). Disaccharides are named by common or systematic name: -Non-reducing monosaccharide (-yl) -Carbon atoms that participate in the O-glycosidic bond -Second monosaccharide (-ose)

-Maltose is formed by an alpha 1-4 bond between two glucose monomers, and is a reducing agent because

the OH from the anomeric carbon remains out of the

O-glycosidic bond.

                   - **Sucrose** is a disaccharide formed by **glucose** and

fructose, joined together by an alpha 1-2 linkage. Sucrose contains no free anomeric carbons, therefore, it is

a non-reducing sugar.

-Lactose is a reducing sugar formed by D-galactose and D-glucose, joined together by beta 1-4 bonds . It’s found in the milk of humans and other mammals, because the natural synthesis of lactose occurs only in mammary

tissue. Lactose intolerance occurs when an individual

lacks the enzyme ( lactase ) necessary for the hydrolysis of lactose. A more serious condition would be galactosemia, where a person cannot convert galactose into glucose to

metabolize it.

(In the case of sucrose, the molecule loses it’s reducing power, as it loses the

mutarotation in both

monosaccharides. Sucrose

is a non-reducing disaccharide. Lactose, on

the other hand, remains as a

reducing agent, because when it opens the aldehyde group reappears, and it’s a reducing group)

[PDF p. 16]

POLYSACCHARIDES

It’s the most frequent way in which carbohydrates are found. Because of their nature, they can be formed by an unlimited number of monomers. They carry out one of two functions: energy storage or structural functions .

Classification

They can be classified in many categories, such as homopolysaccharides or heteropolysaccharides, structural or reserve polysaccharides,…

Reserve polysaccharides The most important reserve polysaccharides are starch in plant cells and glycogen in animal cells. Both are found in the cytoplasm forming granules.

Glycogen is a branched polysaccharide made up of glucose monomers. The glucose is linked by alpha 1-4 bonds . But also, glycogen has alpha 1-6 linkers in branch points .

This gives place to many non-reducing ends, which is helpful when carrying out metabolism. The spatial structure of glycogen is spiral-like, which is helpful for the cell as it makes it easy to compact. This spiral shape occurs thanks to the alpha bond. “Glycogen is the main storage saccharide in

animals and bacteria ”.

Starch is the equivalent of glycogen in plants. It’s a combination of two polymers: amylose (a chain of glucose formed by alpha 1-4 bonds) and amylopectin (branched-chain polysaccharide, identical to glycogen, but with less ramifications). Humans possess enzymes that can degrade starch, such as alpha-amylases found in salivary glands.

- Structural polysaccharides

Cellulose is a linear homopolysaccharide formed by beta 1-4 bonds between glucose monomers, found in the plant cell wall. It has a structural function, as it gives shape to the cell. Thanks to the beta linkage, hydrogen bonds now form between adjacent monomers forming an insoluble fibrous structure, giving it it’s structural function. Our body isn’t able to make use of this glucose, as we lack the enzymes that carry out hydrolysis of this beta bond.

Chitin is present in the cell wall of fungi and the exoskeleton of arthropods. It’s a linear homopolysaccharide of N-acetyl-glucosamine monomers linked by beta 1-4 bonds,

same as cellulose.

[PDF p. 17]

Peptidoglycan is a linear heteropolysaccharide of N-acetylglucosamine (GlcNAc) and N-acetylmuramic acid (MurNAc) linked by beta 1→4 bonds . It’s the main component of the bacterial wall . Lysozyme, an enzyme present in our tears, breaks down the glycosidic bond between the components of peptidoglycan, and antibiotics such as Penicillin prevent these bonds from forming, thus protecting us from bacterial pathogens.

The extracellular matrix found surrounding our cells is rich in Glycosaminoglycans, heteropolysaccharides that, together with fibrous proteins, provide strength and resistance to tissues. They are linear polymers with beta 1→4 bonds of repeating disaccharides of a uronic acid + N-acetyl-glucosamine or N-acetyl-galactosamine. The negatively charged carboxylate and sulfate groups present in glycosaminoglycans form hydrogen bonds with water molecules creating a hydrated gel . This raises the viscosity of the medium and gives it an elastic consistency providing a flexible support for the

extracellular matrix.

Hyaluronic acid is a type of glycosaminoglycan, made up of disaccharides (glucuronic acid linked to N-acetylglucosamine via β1→3 bonds) joined by beta 1-4 bonds . It’s a characteristic element of connective tissue (cartilage, tendons,…), where it acts as a lubricant. Hyaluronidase is an enzyme secreted by pathogenic bacteria which carries out hydrolysis of the hyaluronic acid, making the tissue more susceptible to

infection.

Heparin is another type of glycosaminoglycan, although due to its alpha linkage, isn’t an extracellular structural polysaccharide. It is stored within the secretory granules of mast cells and is

released at sites of tissue

injury. It’s extensively used as an anticoagulant .

[PDF p. 18]

Lecture 2.6. Enzymes

An enzyme is a catalyst . They increase the rate of a reaction . Usually, enzymes are proteins, generally globular, although there are exceptions (such as ribosomes, which are made up of RNA). They are used as catalysts because of:

  • Their specificity: they catalyze a particular reaction with a particular product.

  • They work in mild reaction conditions. Compatible with conditions in cells

  • Higher catalytic power

  • Our body is capable of regulating the processes of the enzyme by changing its activity.

General properties of enzymes

-Generally globular proteins

-Varied sizes

-Monomeric or dimeric

-Just proteins or conjugated with other molecules -Two main zones: active site (where binding and catalysis happens) and regulatory site (enzymes may have one or many).

Enzyme (holoenzyme) = apoenzyme (proteic part) + cofactor (non proteic). Not all enzymes have cofactors, but many do, such as ions. It’s a necessary part of the enzyme, it’s needed for its catalytic activity. Without it, it doesn’t work.

Types of cofactors

  • Metal ions

Coenzymes (organic molecules) such as prosthetic groups or cosustrates. Many coenzymes are vitamins. That’s why we need to ingest them, although in small quantities, for the correct functioning of many of the enzymes in our body.

Enzyme nomenclature

  • Name of the substrate + -ase ( urease, lactase,…)

  • Substrate + enzymatic activity ending in -ase ( Triose phosphate isomerase, DNA polymerase,…)

  • Given name ( Trypsin, rubisco, papain,…)

Classification of enzymes

[PDF p. 19]

How do enzymes work?

For any reaction to happen, we need to reach the activation energy .

  • The activation energy determines the speed of the reaction. The activation energy tells us about the rate/speed of the reaction. How fast the reaction will transition towards the spontaneous direction.
  • The difference of free energy between substrates and products will determine the spontaneity of the reaction.

What enzymes do is reduce the activation energy needed for a specific reaction. Therefore, the rate will be much faster. They enhance reaction rates by lowering activation energy. A consequence of this is that enzymes do not affect the equilibrium. They do not change the direction of the reaction and its spontaneity.

Mechanisms of enzyme activity

1. Effect of proximity and

orientation : for a reaction to take

place, we need the substrates to

be able to interact with each

other. One thing that enzymes do is put together the substrates in a favourable position.

2. Decrease the activation energy:

There are many ways to do this, but we will focus on effect of tension and distortion: enzymes bind perfectly to the transition state of the substrate, modifying it to a way that pushes it closer to such transition state. The model considered closer to reality is the Koshland’s induced model . Both enzyme and substrate are modified in the process.

Example: hexokinase, when it binds to glucose, this event causes them both to change after the catalytic reaction. But this modification is reversible.

[PDF p. 20]

Enzyme regulation

Some enzymes exhibit increased or decreased activity.

Allosteric regulators are generally small chemicals that change the configuration of the protein, making it active or less active. They bind to an area which can be very far from the active site, although never the active

site. There are both allosteric inhibitors

and allosteric activators.

                    - **Reversible covalent modifications**, for example,

the phosphorylation of enzymes that posses an OH group. We will have a reaction where ATP donates a phosphate to the enzyme. This reaction is reversible, depending on the cell’s necessities. Kinase phosphorylates, and phosphatase produces the hydrolysis of the phospho group.

Activation by proteolysis : there are enzymes which are dangerous in the site where they are created, so our body produces enzymes that are inactive, and are activated when they’re in the place where needed. This happens by proteolysis: there is a cleavage of an area of the protein, making it an active enzyme. This reaction is

irreversible.

This happens for example with the protein-degrading enzymes produced in the pancreas: They are activated once they arrive in the small intestine.

[PDF p. 21]

Lecture 2.7. Lipids

Lipids are a heterogeneous group of biomolecules, with the common characteristic that they have a low solubility in water .

Biological functions in lipids

-storage functions (store high amount of energy)

-structural function

-Cofactors for enzymes -Act as signaling molecules, such as hormones -Antioxidants (like tocopherols such as VitE)

Classification

Typically, we find two main groups: those with fatty

acids and those without .

Lipids with fatty acids can be further classified into storage lipids and membrane lipids (look at chart). Biologically active lipids that don’t contain fatty acids are cholesterol, vitamin E,…

Fatty acids

Fatty acids are long carbon atom chains (hydrophobic) joined to a carboxylic head (polar end), containing between 4 to 36 C. Most of them have an even number of carbons, and they aren’t usually branched.

Depending on whether they have double bonds or not, we find saturated and unsaturated (mono or polyunsaturated/PUFA) fatty acids. The saturation of the carbon chain changes its direction and disposition in space: they can be found in a CIS configuration (same direction) or TRANS. The natural fatty acid is mostly found in a CIS configuration. Nomenclature: systematic (cis-9-octadecenoic acid) or common name (oleic acid). The delta numbering of the carbon skeleton describes the position of the first carbon of the alkene in relation to the carbonyl carbon (18:1 Δ9).The omega numbering describes the location of the first carbon of the alkene in relation to the terminal methyl. Examples of saturated fatty acids : palmitic acid, stearic acid,.. The higher the length, the higher the melting point and the lower the solubility in water, due to the hydrophobic nature

of the carbon chain.

Examples of unsaturated fatty acids : oleic acid (olive oil), linoleic acid, linolenic acid, arachidonic acid (precursor of leukotrienes, tromboxans,..).

[PDF p. 22]

Saturated fatty acids have a higher melting point because, due to their long straight carbon chains, they can pack together better and more easily, making it more difficult to separate

them.

Essential fatty acids are those that we cannot produce endogenously, but we need them in our diet: these are linoleic acid and linolenic acid (ALA) . ALA is an omega 3 unsaturated fatty acid. These types of fatty acids have been linked to protection against heart disease, that’s why they are considered healthy, although the mechanisms are unknown. “Trans-fats” : hydrogenation of fatty acids is a process used to produce many foods, making them taste better and giving better texture. This industrial process results in a partial hydrogenation and in the double bonds adopting a trans configuration.

Triacylglycerols (storage lipids)

Solid triacylglycerols are known as fats, while liquid ones are known as oils.

They are the primary form of storage lipids. A triacylglycerol is made up of Glycerol+3 fatty acid chains, linked together through esterification of the OH groups in glycerol. In a regular person,125,000 kcal are stored as fat and 2,000 kcal as glycogen. This is because we can store twice the amount of energy in 1g fat than in 1g of glycogen. This is due to the carbon atoms in fat being more reduced, and therefore, they undergo a longer oxidation that produces more energy. In addition to this, we can obtain energy from fats in a very fast way, which is more favorable for our body.

Biologically active lipids

- Steroid hormones

They are derivative from sterols such as cholesterol. Steroid hormones are synthesized from cholesterol in gonads and adrenal glands. Many steroid hormones are male and female

sex hormones.

- Arachidonic acid derivatives

Enzymatic oxidation of arachidonic acid yields prostaglandins (inflammation and fever), thromboxanes (formation of blood clots), leukotrienes (stimulates smooth muscle contraction in lungs). Pharmaceutical drugs such as paracetamol treat pain by blocking the production of arachidonic acid.

- Vitamin D

VitD regulates calcium metabolism . It’s a lipid that comes from 1,25-dihydroxycholecalciferol (calcitriol). A shortage of vitamin D

can result in osteomalacia or rickets.

[PDF p. 23]

- Vitamin A

Derived from Beta-carotene (a terpene), involved in the visual cycle .

- VItamin K

It’s a clotting cofactor with a similar structure to the previous vitamins.

  • Vitamin E

Acts as an antioxidant and it regulates the stress response in our body. It’s made up of the polymerization of 3 isoprenes, resulting in a terpene. The addition of the hydrophilic head results in an isoprenoid.

[PDF p. 24]

History

Lecture 2.8 & 2.9. Nucleotides and nucleic acids I and II

Friederich Miescher, in 1868, isolated from cells’ nuclei a substance that

contained phosphorous and that he called nuclein.

Frederick Griffith performed experiments in 1928 with virulent and nonvirulent streptococcus pneumoniae, with the difference that one was encapsulated and the other, not. He took the virulent, encapsulated bacteria and killed them by heat. When they were injected into mice, these did not die. But when he mixed the dead lethal bacteria and nonlethal alive bacteria, and injected it in mice, these died. He proved that there was a transforming factor which Avory, McLeod and McCarthy identified as DNA in 1944. They separated proteins and nucleic acids from the dead lethal bacteria, and mixed each fraction with nonlethal

bacteria. Those which had been mixed with nucleic acids and

injected in mice produced the death of these, demonstrating it was these nucleic acids which stored this genetic information. In 1952 Alfred Hershey and Martha Chase performed the definitive experiment, demonstrating that DNA is the genetic material in living organisms. They grew a culture of bacteriophages in the presence of S35 (incorporated into proteins) and others in the presence of P32 (incorporated into nucleic acids), for radioactive labelling. They observed that only when viruses were labeled with P32

were radioactive bacteria left behind.

Nucleic acids are linear polymers formed by nucleotides. They are made up of a phosphate group joined to a pentose (ribose and deoxyribose) by an ester bond, and a nitrogenous base joined by an N-glycosidic bond to the pentose.

Pentoses in nucleotides

The difference between ribose and deoxyribose is the presence of an OH group in C2.

Nucleobases

Nitrogenous bases are aromatic molecules . We find purines ( A and G ) and pyrimidines ( C,T,U ). The border of the rings is characterized by their hydrophilic borders, while the surfaces are flat and hydrophobic.

[PDF p. 25]

Nucleotides and polynucleotides

As the different components join, we go from base, to nucleoside (base+pentose) to nucleotide (base+pentose+phosphate group). Free nucleotides, such as ATP or ADP, cAMp (cell signaling) Polynucleotides . Depending on the number, there are oligonucleotides(less than 50) or polynucleotides. The backbone is formed by the bond between phosphate and pentose. They are

directional molecules with a 5’ end

and 3’ end, each end being linked by phosphodiester bridges. The chains read from left to right, from the 5’ end to the 3’ end.

Structural levels

-Primary structure (order of bases) -Secondary structure (3D conformation of the backbone) -Tertiary structure (referred to the supercoiling of the chain)

RNA secondary structure : RNA molecules, which are single stranded chains of nucleotides, can adopt very complex secondary structures. Their bases can pair with complementary DNA and RNA bases.

  • DNA secondary structure : the chain forms a double helix.
  • DNA tertiary structure: DNA is found in the form of chromatin, more compact and supercoiled around histones in the case of animals.

Nucleic acids stability

Under physiological conditions, the N-glycosidic bond can undergo spontaneous hydrolysis (every 24h) generating apurinic residues. But there is machinery in the cells which identifies these sites and attempts to repair it.

In other cases, the amino group in a base can be lost in a deamination process, resulting in the conversion, in the case of cytosine, into uracil. There is also machinery to avoid this

sort of mutation.

But why does DNA contain thymine and not uracil? A reason may be due to this deamination process. How if not would the cell tell apart the mutated uracil bases from the

[PDF p. 26]

“original” ones? The repair mechanism would be unable to restore the original base and the body would accumulate mutations. Another difference between DNA and RNA is the pentose that makes up the molecule. RNA has ribose and DNA, deoxyribose . This small difference has great consequences: under physiological conditions, a new esther bond can be formed by the attack of an oxygen atom (nucleophilic attack). This new bond breaks the chain. This breakage can only happen in RNA as DNA does not possess that OH group. This has a great impact on the half life of the molecule, making it millions of years for DNA while it’s minutes for RNA. In addition to all of this, DNA is negatively charged, making it less prone to nucleophilic attack and hydrolysis, and therefore more stable.

Nucleases

Nucleases are enzymes with phosphodiesterase activity, which hydrolyze the phosphodiester bond and digest nucleic acids. There are exonucleases and endonucleases. There is a specific group of endonucleases called restriction endonucleases . Bacteria have learned to restrict the possibility of attack from foreign DNA by means of these enzymes. They can identify specific regions within the DNA and generate blunt ends or protruding ends. These are interesting for genetic engineering.

Discovery of the secondary structure of DNA

Maurice Wilkins and Rosalind Franklin were studying the structure of DNA through X-ray diffraction, which helped to

determine its structure. The three fundamental evidences

that allowed the establishment of the secondary structure of

DNA were:

-X ray diffraction data, which suggested DNA had a double helical linear structure, and that both strands were oriented in an antiparallel fashion . DNA molecules exhibit 2 periodicities repeating along the length of the fiber, of 3.4 and 34 Armstrongs. -Studies on the chemical composition of DNA, which informed

about the relative abundance of each nucleobase.

-Tautomeric forms: nucleobases were most frequently found in their KETO and AMINO forms, info which was important for Watson and Crick when they proposed their theory on how

nucleobases interact.

[PDF p. 27]

Additionally, we have the studies made by Chargaff : 1.The nucleobase composition of DNA varies from one species to another, but remains the same within species. 2.DNA specimens from the same species but different tissues have the same base composition. 3.The DNA composition of an individual does not change with age. 4.A=T and C=G in any given species.

This additional info helped Watson and Crick, who published their proposal for the DNA

double helix in 1953 .

“DNA is a helical linear molecule made up of two strands which intertwine, and are each oriented in an antiparallel fashion. The nucleobases are oriented inwards and perpendicular to the central axis, interacting with each other through H bonds in a very specific manner” They proposed that this interaction involved the pairing of A and T, and C and G. They also added that the 3.4 periodicity corresponded to the separation between bases, and the 34A periodicity corresponded to each turn of the double helix. Major and minor grooves can be

observed on such strand. They also observed that both strands are not the same, but

complementary.

Curiously, there are residues/functional groups on the surface of the major grooves that inform which base is found where, allowing for example restriction enzymes to gain their specificity and identify where each base is located.

Structural variations in DNA

-It’s a very flexible molecule . This is important: the genome can measure up to 2m long, so it’s important that it can be rotated and supercoiled to fit into the nucleus of a cell. -The structure proposed by Watson and Crick is only one of many that DNA can adopt. Depending on the degree of humidity ad salinity in a solution, it can adopt different conformations, like the so called A form, a right hand double helix in which the nucleobases are not completely perpendicular to the axis, or the Z form, where it adopts a left handed double helix conformation and which is obtained in high salt concentrations.

Factors that influence the stability of DNA molecule

DNA is a very stable molecule. -The main force of it’s stability is the interaction of stacked nucleobases within the double helix, known as stacking interactions, like hydrophobic and van der waals forces, which

induce the helical conformations.

-The formation of H bonds between complementary bases stabilizes the double helix.

[PDF p. 28]

-But the electrostatic interactions between phosphate groups tend to destabilize the

double helix.

Denaturation and renaturation of DNA

DNA can undergo denaturation, a tensile dissociation of the two strands which doesn’t involve any chemical change (covalent bonds). This is important and necessary for almost all DNA processes (replication, transcription,..). In the cell, it’s induced enzymatically . There are enzymes called helicases which participate in the process of denaturation. They start to separate the two strands and form what is known as a replication bubble. Outside the cell, heating DNA in the lab can also induce denaturation, although it changes many of its physical properties such as the capacity to absorb UV light. Absorption occurs in nucleic acids thanks to the bases, which possess aromatic groups that absorb UV light .

Denatured DNA, in single stranded conformation, can absorb more UV light, a property known as hyperchromicity . This is because the electrons in this conformation can move more freely. This allows us to determine the DNA melting point. While increasing the temperature, we observe the UV light absorption, which increases as the DNA becomes denatured. A parameter can be derived from this property: the melting temperature (MT) which corresponds to the point where half of the molecule has been denatured. Two different DNA molecules will have different graphs and different MT. It has been observed that the melting temperature will be determined by the proportion of C and G bases (because they establish triple bonds). The more G and C in the molecule, the more energy will be necessary to denature it. In the cell, helicase will have to work harder in G and C regions. The molecule can be renatured also by reducing the temperature . This ability to denature and renature is the base of polymerase chain reaction (PCR) and many other new technologies and techniques.

[PDF p. 29]

TBL 2.1. The importance of pH in biology

Henderson-Hasselbach equation Knowing pH and pka values, you can know the ratio of conjugate base and conjugate acid

An example of this: (acetic acid/ acetate)

When the concentration of base and acid is the same, we get log(1)=0. Therefore, pH=pKa in every occasion.

At high pH, we have a low concentration of acid . At low pH, we have a high concentration of acid .

In conclusion, pH values have great importance in biology, because it will determine the amount of acid or conjugate base present in our system.

How does our body keep the pH in the right values?

Throughout a buffer solution, an acid-base equilibrium which shifts towards reactants or products depending on the concentration of each in relation to the constant.

[PDF p. 30]

Blood is a buffer solution. The most

important conjugate pair in the blood is H2CO3/HCO3-, the carbonate/bicarbonate

buffer .

The source of this carbonic acid is CO2.

But, can we control the pH in our blood? Yes! If we don’t release CO2 and retain it in our blood, it will react with water as it is in equilibrium, giving place to a higher concentration of H2CO3, which at the same time increases the concentration of protons and decreases pH. This can happen when someone stops breathing. If the body is in alkalosis, it will try to compensate this by provoking hypoventilation, to try and decrease gas exchange and therefore decrease the pH. If the body is in acidosis, it will try to compensate this by provoking hyperventilation, thus extracting CO2 from the blood and shifting the equilibrium, removing H3O+.

Case report

-normal ph range in humans: 7.31-7. -if ph goes above 7.7, the patient dies -high ph values correspond to alkalosis -low ph levels correspond to acidosis

pka / pk1 = ACID pH at which COOH becomes COO

pkb / pk2 = BASIC pH at which NH3+ becomes NH2

pk R = pH at which the R group gains or loses a proton

[PDF p. 31]

TBL 2.2. Enzyme kinetics and inhibition

Effect of temperature : increasing temperature will increase the enzyme-catalyzed reaction rate, but there is an optimum temperature. When we cross a line, the rate decreases because the protein is denaturalized.

Effect of pH : there is also an optimum pH, but if it’s too low or too high, the enzyme will change its conformation because it becomes protonated or deprotonated and the rate

decreases.

Effect of substrate concentration: it also affects the rate of the reaction as it favors the

formation of ES complex. But enzymes can be saturated with substrate and reach a point where adding more substrate won’t affect the rate of the reaction.

Michaelis-Menten model

First, the enzyme binds to the substrate, forming the ES complex. After this, catalysis can occur. There are two steps: binding and catalysis .

Michaelis menten equation: Km =Michaelis constant, which depends on the enzyme. High Km=low affinity. Low Km=high affinity. It represents the affinity of the enzyme for the

substrate.

Interestingly, an enzyme can work with more than one substrate, but it works at different rates depending on the affinity.

This equation (straight line) allows us to graphically calculate in a precise way, vmax (maximum rate) and Km, depending on where the line intersects with the y axis and

x axis.

[PDF p. 32]

The turnover number (catalytic constant ot Kcat ) measures the number of substrate molecules that an enzyme molecule can convert to product per unit of time . The higher the number, the better. It’s measured in s-1.

Catalytic efficiency

To calculate the specificity constant, we divide Kcat/Km . Efficiency will be higher if the affinity (low Km) and conversion speed are higher too.

Inhibitors

There are reversible and irreversible inhibitors. Penicillin is an irreversible inhibitor.

Irreversible = permanently inactivate the enzyme Reversible = can bind to and can dissociate from the enzyme. Often used as drugs to slow down enzyme activity.

Types of reversible inhibition

  • Competitive inhibition: mechanism of action where the inhibitor is able to bind only to the free enzyme. This only affects the Km, because it affects the capacity of the enzyme to bind to the substrate. But once it’s bound to the substrate, the inhibitor doesn’t do anything, it doesn’t affect the catalysis. A patient with methanol intoxication can be treated with ethanol, because it is a competitive inhibitor of methanol. Another example, statins, which are used to reduce cholesterol. It competes with cholesterol for the binding site.

                      **Acompetitive:** in this situation, the inhibitor

binds to the enzyme once the substrate is bound. There is a decrease in Km and in vmax, so the efficiency remains constant. The inhibitor only binds to the ES complex, not to the individual enzyme. Therefore it doesn’t affect the affinity, and Km apparently decreases because of the shift in equilibrium (increase in affinity), but it inhibits catalytic function and therefore, vmax.

[PDF p. 33]

  • Non competitive inhibition: Substrate and

inhibitor bind to different areas . The

enzyme binds independently to each. There is no change in Km, while there is a decrease in vmax . The affinity remains the same (as the binding site is independent for substrate and inhibitor), but Vmax decreases as catalytic function is affected.

[PDF p. 34]

TBL 2.3. Protein structure-function relationship

Pluricellular organisms need to distribute oxygen, but oxygen solubility in water is very low. We need something else. For this, we use proteins, but we don’t have any aminoacids that are able to bind to oxygen. On the other hand, transition metals do, although they are very

reactive and harmful for our cells.

Therefore, they appear bound to other organic molecules to reduce their reactivity. This is the case of hemoglobin, in which the heme group is bound to a protein. It has Fe2+ inside the heme group, which will be able to bind oxygen.

This is also the case of myoglobin, mainly found in muscles as an O2 storage molecule.

MYOGLOBIN

Myoglobin is a globular protein (90% of the molecules are alpha helices), with an ion bound in the middle. It has only one binding site for O2, and a very high affinity for it (exponential graph), which allows it to act as an O2 storage molecule.

Nevertheless, this tight-binding to O2 doesn’t allow myoglobin to act as an oxygen transporter, as it will not release it when in contact with the tissues (even when pO2 is decreased in said tissues). Lowering its affinity for O2 could make it a better transporter, but it would imply that not enough O2 is taken up in the lungs, and O2 delivery would still be insufficient. For a truly effective transport which can meet our body’s needs, affinity must vary with pO2, as occurs during hemoglobin O2 transport thanks to its multiple binding sites and cooperativity properties.

HEMOGLOBIN

Hemoglobin is the molecule which acts as the O2 carrier between the lungs and other tissues.It’s a globular tetramer with a quaternary structure (a dimer of dimers, 2 alpha chains and 2 beta chains), and therefore has different binding sites. It has 4 heme groups found in the inner part of the molecule. It has a homotropic regulation (the ligand is also the regulator).

An important characteristic of hemoglobin is that it contains a histidine residue, which binds the

Fe2+ ion to the protein and retains the heme group in position.

Fe2+ therefore appears bound to the proximal histidine residue, heme group and O2.

[PDF p. 35]

COOPERATIVITY makes hemoglobin a good O2 transporter. Due to this property, it has different affinities, depending on the amount of oxygen present in the environment. Myoglobin has only one binding site with a particular affinity; it doesn’t have this property. But hemoglobin has more than one binding site for

oxygen. Also, the binding sites aren’t independent, they are connected and interact between them. When the ligand is incorporated into one of the binding sites, it modifies the affinity of the other binding sites. This property (cooperativity) gives out a sigmoidal

curve.

Hemoglobin in blood

Oxygenated blood (arterial blood) has a high O2 saturation (hemoglobin is able to bind to almost 100% of O2 in the lungs). When in contact with tissues, it releases around 40% of the oxygen (when pO2 is 40 mmHg in tissues and 100 mmHg in blood). Deoxygenated blood (venous blood) has a much lower O2 saturation, and travels to the lungs to be reoxygenated thanks to the difference in pO2 (40 mmHg in blood, 104 mmHg in lungs).

The difference between arterial and venous blood colours is due to

the light absorption spectrum of hemoglobin.

Pulse oximeters measure oxygen saturation.

T and R stages of hemoglobin

- T stage stands for tense

- R stage stands for relaxed

Hemoglobin has different quaternary structures (T and R), each with different affinities for O2. In the relaxed state, subunits are more able to move and it is easier to incorporate oxygen (T stage affinity for O2 < R stage affinity). The main secondary structure of the chains in hemoglobin is alpha-helix.

[PDF p. 36]

- What happens when O2 binds to hemoglobin? When O2 binds, it pulls the Fe2+ ion with it, which will at the same time move the proximal histidine and therefore, the whole subunit. O2 changes the conformation of the subunit through the pulling of the proximal histidine. This simple movement changes everything: it changes the interaction with the other subunits . Some of the ionic bonds will be broken, and hemoglobin goes into the R state . So once one O2 molecule binds to hemoglobin, this will favour the binding of other oxygen molecules until we have 4 O2 bound to heme groups in hemoglobin.

Physiological factors that regulate the affinity for O2

● pH: a certain histidine interacts with an aspartate, because histidine (+) at physiological pH interacts with aspartate (-). They form ionic interactions, and the hemoglobin is found in the T stage. But pH can have an effect on this: Histidine can be protonated or deprotonated, depending on the pH.

“When pH goes down, the affinity of hemoglobin for O2 is lower”, because the lowering of pH causes more acid to form (histidine, +), therefore reinforcing the T stage. Ultimately, this favours oxygen release (as it is not in the R state) in tissues, as pH in tissues is lower due to the generation of CO2 during catabolism. In the lungs, the opposite effect occurs, and O2 binds to hemoglobin (pH in lungs > pH in blood). This

is known as the “Bohr effect”.

  • 2,3-Bisphosphoglycerate: it stabilizes the T state, and is therefore a negative heterotrophic regulator. “When 2,3-BPG is present the affinity of hemoglobin for oxygen is lower”. As 2,3-BPG reduces the affinity, it’s partly necessary for the correct transport of oxygen and its release into the tissues. If it weren’t present, hemoglobin’s affinity for oxygen wouldn’t allow it to work as an O2 carrier. BPG concentration can be regulated, for example, by the pressure of oxygen in the environment (altitude), which subsequently modifies the affinity of Hb for O2. https://aklectures.com/lecture/myoglobin-and-hemoglobin/high-altitude-and-2-3-bgp

[PDF p. 37]

How can O2 be transported from a mother to the fetus in an efficient way during pregnancy?

There is a specific hemoglobin in the fetus, fetal-hemoglobin, which is different to adult hemoglobin. The affinity of fetal-hemoglobin for oxygen is higher, and therefore, the oxygen transported by the mother’s hemoglobin will be transported to the fetus due to this higher affinity. This increased affinity is due to a gamma chain in fetal hemoglobin, which has a lower capacity to bind BPG, therefore making its affinity for oxygen higher.

Carbon monoxide (CO) poisoning. Clinical Case

“A child is found with reduced consciousness, hypoxic, cyanotic,… They measure carboxyhemoglobin and it is higher than 50%; normal levels are

around 2%”

This is a case of carbon monoxide poisoning (CO). It’s responsible for half of the deaths due to poisoning in the world. Molecularly, CO is very similar to O2, so it binds to hemoglobin. The affinity of hemoglobin for CO is 20,000 times higher than that for O2. To avoid CO binding due to this incredibly high affinity, we have a distal histidine (different from the proximal histidine). Thanks to this histidine, the affinity of hemoglobin for CO is reduced to 250 times higher, and allows us to maintain normal CO levels in the body.

Carboxyhemoglobin is normally found in levels of 2% in the general population, except in smokers ; they have higher levels of CO in circulation, which reduces the delivery of O2

to the tissues.

During CO poisoning, like in the case of this child, who was having half the capacity to bind to oxygen, O2 delivery becomes insufficient. Not only due to reduced binding sites, but also, the binding of CO induces the R state, and causes hemoglobin to bind more tightly to the other O2 residues; the curve is no longer sigmoidal. In conclusion, the capacity to bind to oxygen is lower (lesser binding sites) and the capacity to deliver oxygen is also lower. Only 10% of the oxygen that is taken up is

delivered to the tissues.

[PDF p. 38]

Lecture 3.1 and 3.2. Molecular structure of membranes I & II

Membranes are intelligent barriers that respond to extracellular stimuli if they have the

need to do so.

Functions of membranes

  • They limit the cell boundaries

  • Allow the transport of molecules

  • They allow compartmentalization of substances and processes within the cytoplasm. They separate energy-producing and energy-consuming processes and keep proteolytic enzymes away from important cellular proteins.

Common features of membranes

  • Membranes are made up of a bilipid layer, which can contain embedded proteins and saccharides bound to them. It is asymmetric.

  • They form spontaneously in aqueous solution

  • Fluid mosaic model proposed by Singer and Nicholson

Types of membrane lipids

They all share the property of being amphipathic, with a polar head and a hydrophobic tail . They differ in:

                                    - The **backbone**, which can

be Glycerol-3-phosphate or Sphingosine (long chain amino-alcohol).

                                    - The second difference

between the types of membrane lipids are the polar heads .

                                    They also differ in the **fatty**

acid chains which bind to them.

Therefore, we find: Glycerophospholipids, Sphingomyelins (sphingosine and phosphate) and Glycosphingolipids (not bound to a phosphate group, but to a mono or oligosaccharide). Lastly, in the membrane, we can find cholesterol . Phospholipids = contain phosphate; Glycolipids = contain saccharides instead

GLYCEROPHOSPHOLIPIDS

They are the primary

constituents of cell

membranes .

[PDF p. 39]

They are made up of two fatty acids which form ester linkage with the 1st and 2nd hydroxyl groups of L-glycerol-3-phosphate . The head group is charged at physiological pH. We can find different types of molecules bound to the phosphate group, like ethanolamine, choline, serine, inositol,…

Phosphatidylcholine, phosphatidylethanolamine and phosphatidylserine are the most abundant in plasma membranes.

We can find phosphatidylinositol, which is very important for the reactive properties of the membrane. Some carbons of the inositol can be phosphorylated, by kinases, to act as a recruiter for signaling molecules. It’s a precursor of molecules with an important role in intracellular signal transduction. It can be released from the membrane as a second messenger (IP3).

[PDF p. 40]

Lastly cardiolipins, which have a phosphatidic acid bound to a glycerol and another phosphatidic acid. We know they play an important role in the machinery of the respiratory processes carried out in the mitochondria. Very abundant in cardiac tissue .

Membrane phospholipids are sources of important second messengers after enzyme-mediated hydrolysis. There are a set of hydrolases which hydrolyze specific phospholipid bonds, like PLA1 (releases fatty acid bound at C1), PLA2 (f.a. in C2), PLC (in the case of inositol, it would release inositol-1,4,5-triphosphate /IP3, which is a calcium channel opener, and diacylglycerol/DAG which activates PKC), PLD,… They could, for example, trigger the release of arachidonic acid which is a precursor for eicosanoids.

There are glycerophospholipids containing ether groups, like plasmalogens, which are very abundant in

mitochondrial membranes.

Another lipid with ether function is the Platelets-Activating Factor, which contains choline and acetyl. It’s secreted by white blood cells and stimulates platelet aggregation.

SPHINGOLIPIDS

Instead of glycerol, we find sphingosine as the backbone, a long chain amino alcohol. A fatty acid is linked to sphingosine via an amide linkage (NOT an ester linkage). A polar head is connected to sphingosine by a glycosidic or phosphodiester bond (different head group substituents).

Types of sphingolipids:

  • Ceramides : closely related with apoptotic processes. When the amount of ceramides increases in membranes, it signals apoptosis .

Sphingomyelins : formed when phosphocholine joins the sphingosine.

Glucosylcerebroside, formed by the addition of glucose.

Lactosylceramide (a type of globoside ), formed by the linkage of di-,tri- or

tetrasaccharides.

Gangliosides, formed by the linkage of complex oligosaccharides.

[PDF p. 41]

Sphingomyelins, which are highly present in the

membranes of neurons. It’s abundant in the

myelin sheaths that surround the axons . It’s formed by sphingosine+amide-linked fatty acid+phosphocholine attached to the alcohol. Structurally, it is very similar to phosphatidylcholine.

Glycosphingolipids, where we can find more simple ones like cerebrosides (mainly present in the membranes of neurons, where the head group is a neutral monosaccharide such as glucose or galactose ) or more complex ones like gangliosides (head group composed by many sugars which are branched and bound together. They are charged heads, because they contain at least one acidic saccharide . They are usually found in the extracellular side of the membrane, because one of their main functions is to

participate in cell-to-cell recognition, and so, they are involved in organ transplant rejection).

Glycosphingolipids determine, in part, the type of blood group . The core antigen is present in all groups, but in addition, A groups contain A type antigens (N-acetyl-galactose) and B groups, the B antigen (galactose). AB groups express both antigens. Our blood type depends on the type of glycosylation RBCs undergo, which is determined at the level of our DNA. It encodes for specific enzymes which will add one sugar or another, giving rise to our blood type

Turnover of membranes

Membranes are constantly flipping the lipids that make up the bilipidic layer. Defects in the

turnover of membrane lipids leads to a number of diseases. If lipids which have been found to have errors are not degraded, it can alter the cell functioning.

SPHINGOLIPIDOSIS comes from the lack of a certain

enzyme, which is meant to degrade these membrane lipids. They accumulate inside of lysosomes and disrupt their function and morphology. Symptoms: mental retardation, blindness, liver failure,…

Examples: Gaucher’s disease, Niemann-Pick disease (lack of sphingomyelinase ), Tay-Sachs disease (shortage of hexosaminidase- A)… Now we know that many Parkinson’s diseases come from a reduced activity in these enzymes, a lack of glucocerebrosidase activity (exam question).

[PDF p. 42]

Sterols and Cholesterol

Its structure is Ciclopentaneperhydrophenantrane . Most of the molecule is hydrophobic, but it has a polar hydrophilic head group. It fills the gaps in lipidic membranes, so membranes with cholesterol are more rigid . The higher the cholesterol, the lower the fluidity. It’s a precursor of many molecules, such as Vitamin D.

Composition of membranes and asymmetry

The composition differs depending on organisms, tissues, organelles,… (tabla) The two leaflets have different membrane compositions

Membrane rafts

There are parts of the membranes with a very specific lipidic composition, microdomains, which contain higher amounts of cholesterol than surrounding regions and higher amounts of sphingolipids, which makes these regions much more rigid. These areas contain many receptors, such as insulin receptors. These raft areas mainly carry out endocytosis. There are raft portions of the membrane that are bound to caveolin, which favors invaginations and therefore allows receptor mediated endocytosis .

[PDF p. 43]

Lecture 3.3. The extracellular matrix

The cellular matrix is a three dimensional network of extracellular molecules, such as fibrous proteins and glycoconjugates, mostly synthesized by fibroblasts (fiber-producing cells). It’s function is to provide strength, stiffness, elasticity, and physical barrier in tissues. It largely determines the structure and function of our tissues. Connective tissue is especially rich in extracellular matrix. Variations in the types of molecules within the connective tissue account for the incredible diversity of tissues and organs in our body.

FIBROUS PROTEINS

Fibrous proteins are polypeptide chains composed of repeating elements, forming linear structures that arrange in long fibers or sheets. Several types of fibrous proteins, including α-keratin, collagen, elastin, fibronectin and laminin, are found within the extracellular matrix of different tissues. Many fibrous proteins bind to integrins, transmembrane receptors that mediate cell–cell and

cell–matrix interactions.

Matrix metalloproteases (MMPs) are enzymes that catalyze the cleavage of proteins found in the extracellular matrix. MMPs continuously remodel the extracellular matrix to control for cell movement and responses.

Alpha-keratin It’s a fibrous protein formed by 2 alpha helices which wrap, forming a left handed helical structure . It’s rich in hydrophobic amino acids. These fibrils formed by keratin fibers are what we find in hair. When together, the fibers form disulfide bonds between each other, making the structure more stable. The shape of the hair is determined by these bonds. If we want to break these bridges to, for example, curl your hair, you have to first reduce the S which are bound together to SH. Once this is done, you can reshape the hair, creating new bonds by reoxidizing the S. Naturally curly hair and naturally straight hair difere in the way these disulfide bonds are established.

Collagen Scurvy is a disease which became more common in the 15th century. A Dr, Dr LInd, carried out the first clinical trial and divided 12 sick sailors into 6 groups of 2. Daily, all patients received the same diet together with different kinds of treatment. After 6 days Dr Lind had to stop the trial, but amazingly two of the sailors had almost

recovered, the ones treated with citrics.

This is due to collagen : it’s an important component of connective tissue (bones, tendons, cartilage,…). Each collagen chain is a long left handed suprahelical structure with abundance of Glycine, Proline and Hydroxyproline (which stabilizes the structure of collagen and favors the interaction with other collagen fibers). 3 collagen chains intertwine and form a triple helix. There is an enzyme responsible for hydroxylation of proline, prolyl-hydroxylase . It’s an enzyme with a proteic part and a cofactor, a prosthetic group: ascorbic acid / Vitamin C, generally found in citrics. Its deficit causes teeth to fall out, skin rashes,…

[PDF p. 44]

Elastin

Elastin is the major protein rich in hydrophobic amino acids, such as alanine, valine, glycine and proline,

found in elastic fibers of the extracellular matrix. It

permits tissues to return to their original shape after they have been stretched . This is of special importance to blood vessels, which expand and contract repeatedly in response to changes in intravascular pressure.

Fibronectin

Fibronectin is a protein dimer, consisting of two nearly identical polypeptide chains linked by two C-terminal disulfide bonds. It helps position cells within the extracellular matrix. Some cells use the fibronectin matrix to migrate, which is particularly important during embryonic development and wound healing. Fibronectin is also necessary for cellular division .

Laminin

Laminin is a heterotrimeric protein (α, β, and γ subunits). Laminin forms sheet-like networks that serve as the “glue” between different tissues.

GLYCOCONJUGATES

Glycoconjugates are the other main molecular components of the extracellular matrix . Like fibrous proteins, they have structural properties and communicate cells with the extracellular environment. There are three main types of glycoconjugates: proteoglycans, glycoproteins and glycolipids

Proteoglycans

They are macromolecules composed of a protein core bond by glycosidic linkages to long chains of glycosaminoglycans . All glycosaminoglycans in our body, with the exception of hyaluronic acid and heparin, are found in proteoglycans. In contrast to fibrous proteins, which provide resistance to stretching forces, proteoglycans provide resistance to compressive forces . They also affect other cellular activities, such as growth and

adhesion .

There are several types of proteoglycans that differ in the glycosaminoglycan chains. Many are free in the extracellular matrix but some are integral membrane structures. Proteoglycans are the main components of connective tissues such as cartilage, where they bind to each other, as well as to fibrous proteins, mainly collagen. The proteoglycan in

cartilage is rich in keratan sulfate

and chondroitin sulfate . The

complementary functions of proteoglycan and collagen molecules give cartilage its resilience (cartilage can withstand a high compressive load and then reexpand to its previous dimensions when that load is relieved).

[PDF p. 45]

Glycoproteins

Glycoproteins are proteins covalently linked to carbohydrates . Normally, these carbohydrates are small, branched and structurally diverse, both in terms of monosaccharide types and glycosidic bonds. Oligosaccharide chains can bind to the protein by two different linkages:

  • O-glycosidic bond : the anomeric carbon of the first residue in the oligosaccharide, normally N-acetylgalactosamine, binds to the hydroxyl group of serine or threonine residues in the protein.
  • N-glycosidic bond : the anomeric carbon of the first residue in the oligosaccharide, normally N-acetylglucosamine, binds to the amide of an asparagine residue.

Glycosylation of proteins occurs after their synthesis in the endoplasmic reticulum and in transit through the Golgi apparatus. More than half of human proteins are glycoproteins. The oligosaccharide chain(s) in a protein exerts unique functions, such as affecting the folding of the protein, altering polarity and solubility and establishing a recognition site by other molecules, which allows the binding to enzymes, receptors,…

Lectins are proteins that bind carbohydrates with high specificity and affinity. Lectins serve in a wide variety of cell-cell recognition, signaling and adhesion processes . Some lectins bind to mannose-6-phosphate residues in newly synthesized proteins and transfer them to the lysosomes. Secretins, a family of plasma membrane lectins, are involved in the movement of leukocytes through the capillary wall, from blood to tissues at sites of infection or inflammation. Several pathogens attach to their host cells through their glycoproteins or their lectins (Influenza virus, Helicobacter pylori, Bordetella pertussis or HIV virus, among many others).

Erythropoietin is a protein that is used to increase the production of erythrocytes (erythropoiesis) in the bone marrow. It’s used as a treatment for anemia. It’s a naturally-synthesized protein in our system; kidneys are the main epo producing organs in our body. So if it’s injected, it’s difficult to recognize. But glycosylation beat Armstrong. Erythropoietin isn’t only proteic. It is made up of aminoacid chains and sugars (oligosaccharides). These are bound to the protein by covalent bonds (either O-glycosidic or N-glycosidic). The pattern of glycosylation in bacteria and humans is different. When samples from Armstrong were taken, recombinant EPO was identified due to glycosylation.

Glycolipids

Glycolipids are lipids derived from sphingosine conjugated to carbohydrates. Cerebrosides consist of a ceramide with a single sugar residue (glucose or galactose). In gangliosides the ceramide is bound to an oligosaccharide chain . Glycolipids are located in the plasma membrane, where they can have cell recognition

functions.

[PDF p. 46]

Some oligosaccharide motives of gangliosides, and of some glycoproteins, determine human ABO blood groups . Depending on the glycosyltransferases types present in an individual, gangliosides contain different oligosaccharides. Blood type A and B contain an additional N-acetylgalactosamine or galactose . The oligosaccharides of type A and B are immunogenic, whereas the oligosaccharide of type O is not. As a result, antibodies to A and to B antigens occur naturally in the blood of people whose red blood cells lack the corresponding antigen. These antibodies may cause intravascular destruction of red blood cells if given inadvertently during a blood transfusion.

[PDF p. 47]

Lecture 3.4. Microtransport through the membrane

Microtransport occurs whenever there isn’t a deformation of the plasma membrane. Examples: pass of ions, H2O and small molecules like glucose.

Two main types of micro transport: -Passive transport (no energy)

-simple diffusion

-facilitated diffusion

  • Active transport

-primary -secondary

PASSIVE TRANSPORT

No energy consumption. From high to low concentration

1. Simple diffusion Affected by size, polarity, liposolubility, gradient (concentration) and charge. Molecules which are transported by simple diffusion: gasses (O2), hydrophobic substances like benzene, small hydrophilic uncharged polar molecules like water, ethanol, glycerol,…

2. Facilitated diffusion

Transport facilitated by proteins. Types of proteins: -Carriers (for molecules such as glucose) -Membrane channels (ion channels, aquaporins,..)

This type of transport is regulated and selective (Na+, K+,… always in specific pairs and ways) Carrier proteins can be saturated, but membrane channels, as long as they’re open, can allow the pass of unlimited ions.

- Facilitated diffusion regulation: -Voltage gated -Ligand gated (extracellular ligand) -Ligand gated (intracellular ligand) -Mechanically gated (ex: cilia in the ear, when stimulated, open the channels and allow the stimuli to travel to the brain)

- Types of transport in facilitated diffusion: -Uniport (ex: glucose transporter) -Symport (ex: glucose and Na+ transport) -Antiport (ex: HCO3-/Cl-)

[PDF p. 48]

Passive transport through carriers

It occurs down electrochemical gradient. There is a binding to the solute which produces a conformational change. These kinds of transporters can get saturated. They are more efficient for when we have a low concentration of glucose, but as said, it can get saturated.

Passive transport through membrane channels. Transport of H20

H20 can be transported by simple diffusion, or by facilitated diffusion through proteins called aquaporins . Aquaporins are very abundant in the membrane of kidney cells, which filter 180L per day but are able to reabsorb 178L thanks to these aquaporins. This facilitated diffusion is much faster than simple diffusion.

Related to diabetes insipidus: it can be due to an alteration in AQP2 (aquaporin 2) or ADH. This person won’t have the right amount of bodily fluids. Symptoms: polyurea (lots of urine), increased thirst, dehydration and hypovolemia (reduce blood volume).

ACTIVE TRANSPORT

Happens against electrochemical gradient, so there will be an energy and ATP expenditure. The protein binds to the solute, causing a conformational change. It’s selective and regulated, and can also get saturated.

1. Direct active transport / primary active transport

Ex: Na+/K + pump: sodium ions bind to the transporter. ATP appears at the site and is degraded into ADP+P, releasing energy. This causes a conformational change in the protein. The 3 sodium ions are released outside the membrane, and the new structure of the protein has a high affinity for K+ ions, which now bind to the channel.

[PDF p. 49]

This causes the release of the P atom that had remained bound to

the protein, and this release changes the protein structure back to its original conformation, releasing two K+ into the cell. Both sodium and potassium ions move against concentration gradient, thanks to the constant expenditure of ATP . It’s an example of antiport .

Ex: ABC (ATP binding cassette): They incorporate molecules into the cell and release xenobiotics, drugs, toxic agents,… Cells which overexpress this transporter send out more drugs than usual, which results in resistance to chemotherapy (multidrug resistance proteins).

Ex: CFTR protein (Cystic fibrosis transmembrane conductance regulator), which regulates the expelance of Cl-. In a normal respiratory tract, this transporter expels Cl- and it accumulates outside the cell.

In a person with CYSTIC FIBROSIS, there is a mutation in CFTR, making these transporters unable to work. CFTR is unable to transport Cl- out of the cell, therefore H2O is drawn in by osmosis and the mucus layer becomes thicker. This means that cilia can’t move and function correctly, which results in infection . There is no known cure, and life expectancy is less than 40 years, but treatment has improved by preventing and controlling infections, removing mucus from the lungs and in extreme cases, lung transplant. New drug treatment has increased life expectancy.

2. Indirect active transport / secondary active transport

ATP is broken down but used indirectly. A 1st transporter breaks down ATP to create a gradient, and 2nd transporter makes use of this established gradient to move a substance against the previous gradient. A substance such as glucose is pumped from lower to higher concentration, against gradient, through a symport protein of glucose and sodium. Na+ does go in following it’s gradient and therefore, without ATP expenditure, because the gradient has been previously established by the Na+/ K+ pump. Sodium-potassium pump then expels sodium back to the exterior of the cell with the use of ATP, restarting the cycle.

[PDF p. 50]
