Lecture 3.5. Macrotransport through the membrane

Macrotransport involves transportation processes which involve modifications in the plasma membrane. It transports large molecules which can’t go in through micro transport. -Requires energy -Involves the plasma membrane, cytoskeletal proteins and organelles

Types of macrotransport

- Endocytosis Incorporation of external molecules

- Exocytosis Release of cell components

(Endocytic-exocytic cycle)

ENDOCYTOSIS

A. Pinocytosis

Liquid and small dissolved particles are incorporated into the cell. Seen under the microscope, we observe small vesicles under 0.1 um diameter with a clear content, incorporating liquid and small dissolved particles. There are different types of pinocytosis:

- Non-receptor mediated pinocytosis : it’s a continuous and non-selective process. Caveolin is one of the proteins which forms Caveolae, invaginations which favour endocytosis ( 0.05 micrometers, non receptor mediated pinocytosis vesicles)

- Macropinocytosis : also continuous and non-selective

- Receptor-mediated pinocytosis : it’s a specific and selective process. This type of pinocytosis forms vesicles with an electron dense coating, made of clathrin.

  1. Receptor binds to a ligand and initiates an invagination.
  2. Once the receptor is joined to the ligand, different proteins will join to the receptor and assemble: adaptins and clathrin. Together, they form the coating.
  3. Dynamin then joins and strangles the vesicle, detaching it from the membrane, with a coating which

surrounds the content.

  1. HSP70 (ATPase activity) releases adaptin+clathrin coating to be recycled.

[PDF p. 51]

  • Clathrin is the main component of the coating. It’s a protein formed by triskelions (3 heavy chain and 3 light chain peptides). These triskelions organize in a way that results in a structure that resembles a honeycomb. 36 triskelions are required to form this

clathrin structure.

  • Endosomes are organelles which receive vesicles from plasma membrane or Golgi apparatus.

    Early endosomes receive vesicles from the plasma membrane and have irregular shapes. They form very rapidly (1 min) and we can observe ATPases on the membrane which decrease pH by pumping H+. They’re necessary because enzymes such as hydrolases, which carry out digestion, work at very low pH, hence the need for this ATPase. These endosomes can attach to the cytoskeleton and are moved to rich areas of the cell where they

bind to other vesicles.

 - **Late endosomes** take 15 mins to form. Vesicles are incorporated coming

from the Golgi apparatus, which contain the enzymes that are going to allow digestion. Therefore, these late endosomes differentiate into secondary lysosomes.

  • Endocytosed molecules include: Hormones (such as glucagon and insulin), growth factors, lipoproteins,…

  • LDL/low density lipoprotein : its incorporation is carried out to maintain correct

levels of cholesterol in the blood.

  1. LDL is recognized by its receptor and surrounded by clathrin coating.
  2. After uncoating, the receptor is recycled and returns to the plasma membrane the vesicle fuses with an early endosome which is then transferred to the lysosome to be degraded.
  3. Lysosomes degrade cholesterol and release it to the cytosol

Abnormal levels of cholesterol can come from:

 - **Excessive intake** of cholesterol in the diet ( **hypercholesterolemia**, leads to

atherosclerosis) - FAMILIAL HYPERCHOLESTEROLEMIA, genetic condition caused by a mutation in LDL receptors: the cell has no capacity to form the clathrin coating and the vesicle can’t be incorporated. LDL accumulates in the exterior of the cell, which thickens arteries increasing arterial pressure. Familial hypercholesterolemia can cause xanthomas and atherosclerosis.

[PDF p. 52]

  • Epidermal growth factor receptor (EGFR) binds to EGF. It has two domains, with an enzymatic one that will trigger internal signaling in the cell. The receptor, once it has carried out its function, is targeted for degradation (ubiquitination) to stop the signalling process. Therefore it is not recycled, but remains in the early endosomes. Through pinching off/sequestering, EGFR-containing endosomes form multivesicular bodies which will then travel to lysosomes, where all the content is degraded.

B. Phagocytosis

Solid substances (large particles and microorganisms) are incorporated into the cell. Seen under the microscope, we observe vesicles with electron dense content. These vesicles are larger, around 0.25 um in diameter. Protozoa uses it as a regular way of eating, while higher organisms use it as a mechanism of defense. There are specialized cells/phagocytic cells such as macrophages, neutrophils, dendritic cells,.. which target aging cells or pathogens and microorganisms.

  1. First, target recognition and adhesion occurs, which happens by opsonization : a pathogen is marked for ingestion-destruction

by a phagocyte, and antibodies bind

to them.

  1. Secondly, target penetration takes place by the emission of pseudopods, to swallow the target.

  2. Lastly, the phagosome binds to the lysosome and the pathogen is degraded.

C. Transcytosis

Can happen by non-receptor (through endothelial cells in capillaries and the pass of substances into the blood) or receptor mediated endocytosis (maternal IgA to newborns).

[PDF p. 53]

EXOCYTOSIS

Exocytosed materials include disposable material (products of digestion and metabolism) and cell synthesized material (cell membrane components and extracellular matrix, enzymes, hormones,…). Anoikis leads to programmed cell death when a cell loses its capacity to adhere to other cells or ECM, and therefore, its capacity to communicate.

Types of exocytosis:

  • Constitutive : it’s a continuous process of exocytosis.

Regulated : triggered by an external signal. For example, the pancreatic acinar cells, which release digestive enzymes when necessary. It’s also the case of cells which release mucin in the lungs (globet cells) and endocrine cells, which carry out regulated exocytosis releasing insulin or glucagon depending on BG.

[PDF p. 54]

Lecture 3.6 & 3.7. Organization of the cytoskeleton I and II

The cytoskeleton is not rigid, it’s really active, made up of components that are in constant polymerization. We can find microtubules (made of tubulin, of 25 nanometers), microfilaments (made of actin, of 7 nanometers), and thick filaments (made of myosin, of 15 nanometers), and intermediate filaments (keratin and some others, of 8-12 nanometers of diameter).

MICROTUBULES

Microtubules are spread in the cytoplasm, and are made of alpha and beta dimers of tubulin. They form a hollow circle of 13 dimers of alpha and beta tubulin. The properties of this microtubules are:

- Polarity:

A microtubule has a +end and a -end. The concentration

of alpha-beta-tubulin heterodimers for the microtubule to start polymerizing through the -end is higher than the one required for the polymerization to occur in the +end. Therefore, the growth happens from the +end, which explains the polarity of microtubules.

- Dynamic instability: For polymerization to occur, the alpha-beta-tubulin dimer has to be bound to GTP . When it’s incorporated into the microtubule, beta-tubulin GTP dissociates into

GDP. When beta-tubulin is bound to GDP, it’s more

unstable than when bound to GTP.

When there is high GTP-tubulin concentration in the medium, these dimers are quickly added to the +end of the microtubule. But if this amount of GTP-tubulin decreases, the hydrolysis rate of GTP (which results in GDP) will be higher than the rate of incorporation of GTP-tubulin. Therefore the +end will become highly unstable and suffer depolymerization. In conclusion, the amount of GTP-tubulin present in the medium is critical to determine polymerization/depolymerization. This property is called dynamic instability.

Functions of microtubules:

  • Maintenance of cell shape and polarity

  • Cell division (spindle formation, chromatid segregation)

  • Transport of vesicles and organelles inside the cell

  • Cell movement

MAPs

The cell controls the process of polymerization and depolymerization. To control it, we have different proteins in the cell that modify the behaviour of microtubules. They are called microtubule associated proteins (MAPs) .

[PDF p. 55]

  • Non-motor MAPs: they are related to the stabilization and cross-linking of microtubules (structural functions). For example, MAP2 (cross-links microtubules in a parallel position) and Tau (it stabilizes microtubules and allows them to group in a parallel disposition). In Alzheimer’s disease, there is a hyperphosphorylation of Tau, leading it to form clusters in the neurons. These interfere with the normal mechanisms of vesicle transport in the neurons, which causes their death by apoptosis.
    • Motor MAPs: they are in charge of vesicle trafficking throughout an ATP-dependent transport. Two examples are kinases (move vesicles form the -end to the +end) and dyneins (move vesicles form the +end to the-end).

Agents that block the polymerization of microtubules

- Colchicine, which is used in cytogenetics. It is also a therapeutic agent, since it treats gout. It’s used as an anti-inflammatory drug because it blocks leukocyte recruitment (diaperesis) in the site of inflammation by avoiding their migration.

- Vinca alkaloids such as vincristine, vinblastine; used to treat hematologic tumors.

Agents that block depolymerization

- Taxol, used to treat solid tumors. It was first found in the Yow tree.

Complex organization of microtubules in centrioles and motile cilia

1. Centrioles: made of 9 triplets of microtubules forming a circle, with nothing in the middle (93+0 structure) . They form centrosomes (2 centrioles).

2. Motile cilia: found in the respiratory system, sperm,… The stem has a 92+2 structure surrounded by a

membrane.

The basal body has a 93+0 structure (same as centrioles), below the membrane. Microtubules are interconnected by two proteins: nexin (it binds the doublets of microtubules in the stem) and dynein (it is the one responsible for the movement of cilia).

Microtubule organization centres (MTOC)

Microtubules are formed in certain parts of the cell ( centrioles and basal body of cilia are the places where microtubules are originated). In these centres, there is another protein, gamma-tubulin, that is needed for the polymerization (growth) of

microtubules.

[PDF p. 56]

Immotile cilia

With a 92+0 structure . They cannot move. Their function is to be involved in sensory functions. It is one single cilium per cell, that has a lot of sensory proteins, transmitting the conditions from the outside (whether it is too fluid, too solid, or there is a lot of mucus,…). Some of these immotile cilia are found in the ear, nose, and so on (neurons with these cilia, that transmit changes of the noise wave, for example).

- PRIMARY CILIARY DYSKINESIA (PCD)

It is a genetic defect caused by genetic mutations of dynein (90% mutations). Therefore, cilia are disorganized. It is a rare autosomal recessive syndrome. We have cilia that cannot move, so the patient presents symptoms such as infections, inflammations,… Clinical manifestations: sinusitis, bronchitis, pneumonia, otitis, bronchiectasis, chronic infections. Situs inversus is a reversal location of some asymmetric organs in our bodies that 50% patients with PCD might suffer. If there is a patient with PCD that affects the respiratory and reproductive systems + situs inversus, it is said that he suffers Kartagener syndrome. Why do some people develop situs inversus? There is a hypothesis showing that the embryo cells in the middle line (normally having motile cilia), have imotal cilia in the patients suffering

the illness.

Normally, the motile cilia translate the liquid from one side of the cell to another. The cells on the left side sense that there is a flow and it is going to communicate to the cells on the right that they have to differentiate into an organ. If the cilia in the embryo are inmotile, this flow is going to stop, because the cilia cannot move, so there is no sense sent, and the formation of organs is going to be altered.

MICROFILAMENTS

They are made of actin monomers . They are polarized (because actin always incorporates in the +end or barbed; the -end is the pointed end) and dynamic (present dynamic instability ). ATP is needed for polymerization

and then it dissociates into ADP. When it

dissociates into ADP, the microfilament becomes less stable, so depending on the concentration of ATP-bound actin in the medium, there will be polymerization or depolymerization (same as microtubules).

There are actin-binding proteins that control the process of polymerization (microfilament stability and association). In the case of microvilli, we need to maintain the parallelism, and the proteins are vibrin and fimbrin (revisar en el libro de Calvo).

[PDF p. 57]

MYOSIN

It is a motor protein (it uses ATP to move actin). There are different types:

- Myosin 1 : if we put this in a petri dish with actin, actin is going to move towards myosin. It bends the head interacting with the actin filaments, and it moves them. Made up of 1 head and 1 tail.

- Myosin 2: it includes two molecules of myosin. It can associate with other myosin 2 molecules, forming a thick filament (15 nanometers) that we are going to find in muscles . Made up of 2 heads and 2 tails.

Actin-myosin interaction:

In muscles, we find the sarcomere, where we find cylinder filaments with actin walls, except for the central part (where we find the myosin filaments). Contraction is the interaction of actin and myosin. The smooth endoplasmic reticulum in muscle cells is known as sarcoplasmic reticulum and it stores calcium (because the levels of calcium in cytoplasm have to be low, and in the reticulum and outside the cell, high).

When the nerve impulse is transmitted, a chemical called acetylcholine is released, making calcium exit from the endoplasmic reticulum and appear in the cytosol. In the actin there are filaments of troponin and tropomyosin that block the myosin from binding

to actin active sites. When calcium is increased

in the cytosol, calcium binds to troponin, and troponin bound to calcium separates tropomyosin from the actin filament. Now myosin

is able to interact with actin. The breakdown of

ATP releases energy, which makes the myosin move the actin towards the centre of the plain. This process occurs repeatedly. Once the nerve impulse ends, the calcium is reabsorbed.

There are also actin-myosin interactions in cells that are not muscle cells. They do not have sarcomeres, so the mechanism is different. For example, we need cell migration in embryogenesis, metastasis and inflammation.

- DUCHENNE’S MUSCULAR DYSTROPHY

It is a genetic disorder (recessive X-linked: more frequent in males). There is a mutation in a protein called dystrophin (bound to actin in muscle cells), so there is a disorganization of the sarcomere. There is a progressive muscle deterioration. As a result, throughout the years, fat replaces the muscle . Some clinical symptoms are weakness, motor difficulties, breathing problems, fatigue, and cardiomyopathy (it also affects the heart). We can treat it by symptomatic treatment and physical therapy, or with corticosteroids to reduce inflammation and the process of infiltration of immune cells (it is going to prevent the degeneration of muscle cells).

[PDF p. 58]

People are trying to use gene therapy to replace the mutated gene through introducing a vector containing the Y type version of the gene in the muscle cells, although for now these are just trials. This is very difficult, because you have to replace all the cells of the body. We can also use cell therapy (only trials for now), which consists in taking a piece of skin and isolating the stem cells. Then, you culture these cells, and you replace the missing gene with a healthy gene using for example a viral vector in vitro. Then we differentiate these cells in vitro to form muscle cells by adding different components. So we take these modified cells, and introduce them in the body, that tend to go where they are happy (to the muscles), and

then we can have at least some of the muscle tissue made of these cells that are able to

contract.

INTERMEDIATE FILAMENTS

Their diameter is between 8 and 12 nanometers . They are polymers made of different filamentous proteins. They don’t show polarity (they grow in both ends), nor dynamic instability, which is why they are highly stable. They don’t require ATP for polymerization. They are responsible for the cytoskeleton maintenance, mechanical strength and resistance to mechanical stress (structural function, not motor). Keratin is an example of intermediate filaments in keratinocytes.

[PDF p. 59]

CELLULAR ENDOMEMBRANE SYSTEM: ENDOPLASMIC RETICULUM, GOLGI APPARATUS & LYSOSOMES

OBJECTIVES

  • To have a general vision of the macromolecular transport pathways in the eukaryotic cell.

  • To understand the structure and function of the endoplasmic reticulum, the Golgi apparatus and lysosomes.

  • To be capable of describing the protein biosynthetic pathways and the transport chains that take them to their correct cellular destination.

  • To recognise some representative cell types that show a special development of the different elements in the biosynthesis-secretion routes.

  • To be familiar with some examples of illnesses caused by alterations in the endomembrane

system compartments.

INTRODUCTION

Eukaryotic cells, unlike prokaryotic, have a series of internal membranes that delimit specific membranous compartments with a particular morphology and function. This compartment network requires a complex transport system that allows the movement of molecules from one compartment to another. These membranous compartments (also called organelles), are the endoplasmic reticulum with its rough and smooth components, the Golgi apparatus and the lysosomes. The functions of these organelles, except for the smooth endoplasmic reticulum (SER), are related to the synthesis, processing, transport and localisation of a major part of the cellular proteins. The SER, on the other hand, plays a key role in the synthesis of lipids, and therefore it is related to providing the rest of the compartments with membranous supplies.

ROUGH EDOPLASMIC RETICULUM

Structure and composition

The rough endoplasmic reticulum (RER) consists of an interconnecting network of membranous flattened saccules (or cisternae) and tubules with ribosomes attached to them (fig. 10-1). These saccules are connected with the nuclear envelope and with the SER. In electron microscopy cuts the saccules appear as “profiles” (and we call its interior “lumen”) which normally present a thickness of 20 to 30 nm, although sometimes they can appear dilated. (e.g. in plasma cells).

Claude, Porter and Fulham were the first, in 1945, to describe the RER using the electron microscope. Their work was later completed by Sjöstrand, Palade and Porter, who in addition, they related this organelle with protein synthesis.

The RER is present in all nucleated cells (except for spermatozoids) and is especially well developed in cells with a high protein synthesis rate. In an exocrine pancreatic cell, the RER can easily occupy 20% of the cell volume. cells with abundant RER present a characteristic called cytoplasmic basophilia, which consists in the affinity of basic dyes for certain regions of the cell cytoplasm. Cytoplasmic basophilia is seen in areas with abundant RNA from ribosomes, either found free in the cytosol or attached to the RER. Depending on the position of the basophilic component, three types of basophilia can be differentiated (fig. 10-2):

1

[PDF p. 60]

  • Diffuse basophilia, which implies that almost the whole cytoplasm is basophilic. We can normally see a non-basophilic region within the cell, which corresponds to the Golgi apparatus, and is normally called , Negative Golgi Region . An example of diffuse basophilia can be seen in plasma cells.

    • Basal basophilia: in this case, the basophilia is seen in the basal part of the cell. This is very common in secretory epithelial cells which have a large amount of RER in the basal cytoplasm. We find this basophilia in exocrine pancreatic epithelial cells and in the epithelial cells of the salivary glands.

    • Basophilia in clumps: the basophilic components appear in groups or clumps, which correspond to areas were the RER or the free ribosomes are accumulated

within the cell. This basophilia is typical of hepatocytes and neurons (the basophilic areas in neurons correspond to the clumps or Nissl bodies).

Functional studies performed in the 60s of the last century demonstrated that the RER was involved in the synthesis and distribution of proteins. Using radioactive amino acids and autoradiography techniques in pancreatic acinar cells, it was found that amino acids introduced into a cell were incorporated into proteins, primarily in the RER. Longer incubation periods showed that

the proteins had been transported to the Golgi apparatus, then to the secretory vesicles and finally secreted by the cell. Therefore, these experiments proved the existence a pathway for protein synthesis and secretion, which involved passing from the RER to the Golgi apparatus then to the cell membrane and finally to the exterior of the cell.

The RER membrane is a trilaminar structure

typical to that seen in cell membranes, and its thickness is of 5-6 nm, in other words, a little thinner than the plasma membrane. Its composition is slightly different from other cell membranes as it it contains a higher percentage of lipids and a series of proteins exclusive to this organelle. Amongst these proteins, it is important to mention those involved in the attachment of

ribosomes and the translocation of proteins towards the lumen. Moreover, the interior of the RER contains a series of proteins typical to this organelle, such as enzymes involved in the glycosylation of proteins, peptidases, disulphuric chains isomerases and chaperones.

RER protein translation

Cellular proteins can be completely translated by free ribosomes found in the cytosol or the translation may start in the cytosolic ribosomes and continue it in the RER. This difference is

related to the destination of these proteins. Those synthesized in the RER will stay in this organelle, or will be transported to the Golgi apparatus, the

2

[PDF p. 61]

lysosomes, the cell membrane or secreted to the extracellular space. On the other hand, those proteins translated by free cytosolic ribosomes can either remain in the cytosol or be posteriorly incorporated into the nucleus, mitochondria or peroxisomes. As the incorporation of these latter proteins into their target organelles takes place once the protein has been completely translated, this process is called post-translational importation. However, the ribosomes that join the RER membrane, translate the proteins at the same time as they are incorporated to the RER, and hence it is called co-translational

importation .

The proteins that are going to be translated in the RER contain a signalling peptide of 16-30 residues found in the N-terminus region, as a result of the translation of a group of signalling codons present in the mRNA that codifies these proteins. The existence of peptide signals in proteins, responsible for their incorporation in specific membranous compartments in the cell, was also proven in the case of the cell nucleus, mitochondria, chloroplasts and peroxisomes.

How does the translation and

simultaneous incorporation of proteins into the RER take place? Several sequential steps can be distinguished:

Step 1: the translation starts in the free ribosomes. When the signalling peptide appears at the N-terminus end of the protein in formation, this peptide is recognized by the SRP ( signal recognition particle ) and this indicates that the protein must be translated in the RER. The SRP is made up of an RNA molecule and six proteins, and has several protein domains (for binding to the signal peptide, for stopping translation, for joining the ribosome and for the coupling of GTP.

Step 2: the binding of the SRP to the ribosome stops the translation and leads to a conformational change in the SRP which can now be recognized and bind to its receptor in the RER membrane . The ribosome/SRP complex and its receptor then interact with a translocator, a protein complex that will allow the protein that is being synthetized to pass into the RER lumen.

Step 3: the translocator is opened the protein synthesis continues. Then, the signal

peptide remains anchored to the internal region of the translocator via protein interactions and hence will not pass into the RER. The protein being synthesized will work its way along the translocator and then penetrate the RER. The hydrolysis of GTP will take place resulting in conformational changes that will lead to the SRP release.

Step 4: a signal peptidase, bound to the inner surface of the membrane, cuts off the signal peptide from the protein that is being incorporated into the RER.

Step 5: translation continues and the protein will remain in the RER lumen.

Localization and orientation of the proteins in

the RER

This mechanism of incorporation that we have just explained is not common to all proteins translated in the RER. there are proteins that remain joined to the membrane of this organelle because they have hydrophobic signals that allow them to stay in the lipid bilayer (fig. 10-5). Indeed, some proteins have, in addition to the signal peptide at the N-terminus domain, a stop-transfer sequence in an internal region of the protein. This sequence of 20 to 25 amino acids is hydrophobic, which prevents the protein from continuing its translocation towards the RER lumen. In this case, the protein translation continues, and the sequence that comes after the stop-transfer sequence will remain facing the cytoplasmic side. Therefore, once the translation finishes, we will obtain a protein anchored to the RER membrane, with its N-terminus end facing the RER lumen and the carboxyl end (C-terminus) facing the cytoplasm. (see fig. 10-5).

On the other hand, there are proteins that are orientated within the membrane in other ways, with the N-terminus end facing the cytosol, or with both ends facing the same side or even there are

3

[PDF p. 62]

proteins that cross the RER membrane several times. In order to achieve these orientations

various strategies consisting of one or more sequences for initiating the translocation and stop-transfer sequences are needed.

Some proteins have the start transfer sequence located internally in the protein instead of at the N-terminus domain. In this case, the ribosome that translates it will only join the RER when this signal appears, which will determine that the Nterminus end will be facing the cytoplasmic side when its translocation through the RER membrane begins. The translation of the protein continues in such a way that the carboxyl end is placed facing the RER lumen. (fig 10-6). In this case, there is no removal of the signal sequence by the signal peptidase, thus leaving the protein embedded in

the membrane with the hydrophobic signal immersed in it. The internal sequences of the protein can be arranged in such a way that they control the translocation orientating the Nterminus end towards the cytosolic side or the luminal side of the RER, which will in turn allow a different conformation of the protein in the membrane. By changing the direction of the start and stop transfer sequences proteins can be orientated with both N and C-terminus ends

towards the same compartment (cytosolic or luminal side). The presence of several start and stop transfer sequences permits the existence of proteins that cross the RER membrane several times (fig. 10-7).

FIGURE 10-6. Process of synthesis and insertion in the RER membrane

of a single pass protein whose Nterminus is oriented towards the

cytoplasmic side. Source of the image: E. Maldonado and A. Calvo

4

[PDF p. 63]

Post-translational modifications

The proteins translated in the RER must achieve their final configuration so as to be perfectly functional. This configuration depends upon the correct folding of the protein as well as a series of post-translational modifications that take place inside the RER (glycosylation, fragmentation and formation of disulfide bonds). Among cellular proteins, only those that pass through the RER will be glycosylated, which it does not mean that all the proteins translated in the RER will undergo the glycosylation process (although most will). Glycosylation is a process that starts in the RER and ends in the Golgi apparatus. Both organelles contain enzymes capable of transferring sugars to the proteins or deleting some of them so as to form chains of specific oligosaccharides for each protein. . Glycosylation is an important process because some proteins need to be glycosylated in order to fold correctly in the RER. Furthermore, glycosylation prevents protein aggregation.

All the proteins that are glycosylated in the RER (fig.10-8) first undergo a a transference in block of an oligosaccharide formed by 14 residues: 3 of glucose, 9 of mannose and 2 of Nacetylglucosamine, catalysed by an oligosaccharide transferase. The oligosaccharide has been previously synthetized on the cytosolic

side of the RER membrane by means of the activity of several enzymes that build the sugar chain on a lipid in the membrane called dolicholdiphosphate. Once the 14 residue oligosaccharide has been made, a translocation of the carbohydrate chain takes place,, from the cytosolic side to the lumen RER. The transfer of the oligosaccharide to the protein will take place on an asparagine amino acid (Asn) that is found in a Asn-X-Ser/Thr sequence (where X can correspond to any amino acid; Ser: serine; Thr: threonine). This oligosaccharide will be posteriorly modified in the Golgi apparatus.

The correct formation of the three dimensional structure (or correct folding) of the proteins is a phenomenon of great importance.

The formation of disulfide bridges (S-S) takes part in the process of acquisition of a correct protein structure. In proteins located in the RER lumen, disulfide bonds are formed by the loss of two electrons from the SH groups found in cysteine amino acids. This protein modification takes place in the RER because the oxidative

5

[PDF p. 64]

environment favours the formation of S-S bonds,

unlike the reductive environment found in the

cytosol.

The folding of proteins in the RER is a complex process which is not completely understood. Chaperones and protein processing enzymes act as sensors, detecting misfolded proteins and sending them to the cytosol so that they can be degraded.

SMOOTH ENDOPLASMIC RETICULUM

The SER is formed by a system of membranous structures, mainly tubular, without attached ribosomes, and they differ from the RER in morphology, composition and function (fig. 1011).

Although there are morphological and funcitonal differences between both reticula, they are interconnected with each other, forming a structure or compartment with a continuous membrane. The characteristics that differentiate

the SER from the RER are:

  • It does not have ribosomes.

  • The main membranous elements are

fundamentally tubules (not saccules).

  • Its membrane composition it is not the same (different proteins and a greater percentage of lipids).

  • Its functions are mainly related to the synthesis of lipids and fat derivatives.

  • It is only abundant in specialized cells such as hepatocytes, steroid hormone secretory cells, muscular cells, etc.

The SER has numerous functions within the

cell, mainly related to the synthesis of lipids, which are necessary for the increase and replacement of the membranous elements of the different organelles and the plasma membrane.

Functions

LIPID AND FAT DERIVATIVES SYNTHESIS

In every cell the synthesis of phospholipids and cholesterol is very important for the renewal of cellular membranes. This is

done by the SER. Moreover, there are specialised cells in the organism in which the SER is very abundant; in these cells the synthesis of certain types of lipids and their derivatives takes place.

6

[PDF p. 65]

The lipid synthesis takes place in the cytosolic hemimembrane of the endoplasmic reticulum, therefore, the addition of lipid molecules would only take place on this side of the membrane. In order to redistribute the lipids in both hemimembranes and establish the asymmetry that characterizes them regarding their composition, the activity of enzymes called scramblases is required, which catalyse the energetically unfavourable passage of the lipid polar groups from one side of the membrane to the other (see chapter 3).

The lipids synthesized in the SER will be distributed by means of vesicular transport to the different membranous compartments (RER, Golgi apparatus, lysosomes) and to the plasma membrane. In order for them to be transported to mitochondria and peroxisomes cytosolic proteins that exchange phospholipids are required

Due to its role in lipid synthesis, the SER also contributes in a very important manner in the formation of lipoproteins, bile acids and steroid hormones. Enterocytes contain RER and SER that

take part in the formation of chylomicrons which are formed by triglycerides, phosphoglycerides, cholesterol and proteins. The liver also synthesizes lipoproteins (LDL, VLDL, HDL and VHDL, which are posteriorly released into the bloodstream) and bile acids, formed from cholesterol (and excreted into the bile canaliculi). The steroid hormone synthesis from cholesterol, takes place in specialized endocrine cells in several successive steps that require the action of mitochondrial and SER enzymes. In this way, hormones such as testosterone, oestradiol, cortisol and aldosterone etc. are synthetized.

DETOXIFICATION OF LIPOSOLUBLE

SUBTANCES

The SER plays an important role in the breakdown of toxic liposoluble substances, such as certain drugs, alcohol, pesticides and carcinogens, as well as toxic substances produced by the metabolism itself. All these substances are turned into hydro soluble products, which are more easily eliminated by the organism. In hepatocytes, when there are high levels of such toxic substances, the SER is highly developed and contains detoxifying enzymes (such as the ones of the cytochrome P450 family, which are anchored to the cytosolic side of the SER membrane. The activity of these enzymes include oxidation, hydrolysis and reduction of substances which have to be chemically modified to reduce their toxicity. Other cytoplasmic enzymes act by conjugating these substances to certain chemical groups (glucuronic, sulfate, acetyl, etc), facilitating their excretion.

PARTICIPATION IN THE GLYCOGEN

METABOLIM

In some cell types, the SER is frequently found close to glycogen stores, especially in hepatocytes. These cells play an important role in the storage of glucose as glycogen. On the cytosolic side of the SER membrane the glucose6-phosphatase is present, an enzyme that hydrolases glucose-6-phosphate, producing free glucose that can go to the bloodstream and then to the tissues when their glucose levels are low.

7

[PDF p. 66]

Ca [2+ ] STORAGE

As explained in chapter 4, the SER has Ca [2+ ]

pumps, Ca2+ ATPases that introduce Ca [2+ ] into the SER, against the concentration gradient with the purpose of maintaining low cytoplasmic levels of this ion. ;therefore the SER acts as an intracellular reservoir of Ca [2+] . The increase in the cytoplasmic Ca [2+ ] levels triggers signaling cascades that can have different consequences, depending on the cellular type (see chapter 13). Therefore, it acts as a second messenger in intracellular signaling. In skeletal muscle fibers, a special type of SER is found, called sarcoplasmic reticulum, which wraps the myofibrils. It constitutes a Ca2+ storage. These ions are released with the arrival of the nervous impulse and then muscle contraction is produced.

GOLGI APPARATUS

Structure and properties

The Golgi apparatus is formed by a group of membranous structures, mainly flattened saccules and stacked in a parallel fashion. Vesicles also appear around the saccules, especially near its ends, as well as tubular shaped membranous elements. All these elements are grouped together forming dictyosomes, whose number and distribution varies according to the cell type (e.g. hepatocytes can have up to 50 dictyosomes). The Golgi apparatus is highly developed in cells that produce glycoproteins and proteoglycans, such as the pancreatic acinar cells, goblet cells, hepatocytes, neurons, etc.

The Golgi apparatus exhibits polarity, which means there are two areas with very different morphology and composition, called Cis face (or entry face) and Trans face (or exit face) (fig. 10-14). Protein maturation takes place in the Golgi apparatus that occurs progressively from the Cis face to the Trans face. Hence protein maturation is polarised towards the trans face of the Golgi apparatus. For this,there is a clear functional compartmentalisation within the Golgi apparatus in such a way that it is possible to distinguish five compartments (fig 10-14)

  • Cis Golgi network (CGN)

  • Cis cisternae

  • Medial cisternae

  • Trans cisternae

  • Trans Golgi network (TGN)

The two end compartments (CGN and TGN) are not formed by cisternae but by an irregular network of tubules and vesicles (this explains why they are called ‘networks’). Each compartment has a specific group of enzymes responsible for the sequential maturation of the proteins. Therefore, in the CGN region and in the Cis area we can find enzymes that transfer phosphate groups and eliminate mannose residues, respectively. In the medial cisternae Nacetylglucosamine is added; in the Trans ones, galactose and sialic acid, whilst in the TGN area enzymes transfer sulphate groups to certain proteins.

Functions

In the Golgi apparatus the proteins and the lipids synthesized in the ER will continue their maturation. The main changes that they will undergo are N-glycosylation, O-glycosylation, fragmentation and sulphation.

GLYCOSYLATION

Regarding the proteins that have been previously glycosylated in the RER (where the N- glycosylation took place), in the Golgi apparatus they will lose some of their sugar radicals and have new ones added, giving rise to glycoproteins which contain N-oligosaccharides that can be

8

[PDF p. 67]

classified into 3 groups a) rich in mannose, b) complex and c) hybrids, (intermediate oligosaccharides, in between these two groups).

Apart from the modification of the oligosaccharides of the N-glycoproteins, the Golgi apparatus is also responsible for the O- glycosylation (which takes place exclusively in this organelle). In this type of glycosylation sugars are linked to an OH group of the following amino acids: serine, threonine and, in the case of collagen, hydroxylysine. The O- glycosylated proteins can be found forming mucins, which are abundant in cells that secrete mucinous

substances; collagen also has O-ligated sugars.

MARKING OF LYSOSOMAL ENZYMES

Enzymes of the acid hydrolase type, which are going to be responsible for the cellular digestion that is carried out by lysosomes, are classified by means of a special route that will direct them towards this organelle. Primary lysosomes are formed from clathrin-coated vesicles, that originate in the TGN area of the Golgi apparatus. The process begins in the CGN area, which contains enzymes that add phosphate groups to the

6 [th] carbon of the terminal mannose on the

hydrolase proteins, that will go to lysosomes. Mannose-6-phosphate (M6P) therefore constitutes a way of tagging those proteins that will go to the lysosome. All these proteins undergo N-glycosylation as well as the addition of a phosphate group which takes place as a result of the enzyme activity of N-acetylglucosamine phosphotransferase. This enzyme transfers a phosphate group from N-acetylglucosamine-UDP to the mannose found in lysosomal enzymes. The labelled lysosomal hydrolases are recognized by M6P receptors found in the TGN area. In this way, clathrin coated vesicles are formed which leave

the TGN area and fuse with the late endosome.

Any alteration in this classifying system will produce serious consequences for the cell, since these enzymes are not destined for the lysosome, but they join the secretory pathway and are released into the extracellular space.

MEMBRANE RECYCLING

The Golgi apparatus is a very dynamic compartment that must be in constant equilibrium,

9

[PDF p. 68]

as it continuously receiving contributions of membranous elements that come from

endocytosis, and in turn releases vesicles or the secretory pathway, whose membrane becomes part of the plasma membrane. It is therefore an organelle responsible for the correct distribution of the different membranous components of the cell.

PROPER DISTRIBUTION OF THE

DIFFERENT MACROMOLECULES

As we have already mentioned, the Golgi apparatus is a classifying compartment, from

which vesicles emerge with different cell destinations. The figure 10-15 shows the path that several macromolecules follow form the

beginning of their synthesis in the RER to their final destination. Proteins can be directed towards

the plasma membrane and stay there (if they are proteins anchored to the membrane), be released to the cell exterior, stay as Golgi resident proteins or be destined for the lysosomes.

10

[PDF p. 69]

1

STUDY MATERIAL FOR TBL 3.1.

SUMMARY AND TRANSLATION OF CHAPTER 6, FROM THE BOOK “BIOLOGÍA CELULAR BIOMÉDICA”, PLASMA MEMBRANE SPECIALIZATIONS AND CELL INTERACTIONS WITH THE ENVIRONMENT

Authors: Elena Bodegas, Alfonso Calvo

Translation: Pedro Montero Narva

LEARNING OBJECTIVES:

• To know the plasma membrane specializations in the different cellular domains (apical, lateral and

basal).

• To understand the molecular structure of the different plasma membrane specializations.

• To relate certain human pathologies with molecular and/or cellular alterations in the plasma

membrane specializations.

INTRODUCTION

Cells are usually connected to one another or to

proteins in the extracellular matrix (ECM) which

surround them. Cell interactions with its environment

are vital for functioning in a particular tissue. In fact,

most cells depend on these interactions for their

survival. Thus if they are unable to adhere to the

substrate or to other cells, they end up dying by a

process called “anoikis” (apoptosis caused by the lack

of adhesion). These types of cell-cell and cell-ECM

interactions are also key in communication and

intercellular signaling. The plasmatic membrane plays

an essential role in these processes, developing

different structures that facilitate adhesion, contact

with other cells or the interaction with the

surrounding medium. In this chapter we describe the

plasma membrane specializations and the main

extracellular molecules with which they interact.

PLASMA MEMBRANE SPECIALIZATIONS

The plasma membrane may have different

specializations (fig. 6-1) in either: a) the cells´ free

surface (whichever is not in contact with other cells),

such as microvilli, cilia and flagella (these last two

differentiations are explained in chapter 12), and b)

the contact surface with neighboring cells or the

ECM, which include interdigitations, basal infoldings

and cell-cell or cell-EMC junctions.

1

[PDF p. 70]

Figure 6.1. Representation of an idealized cell showing the

different plasma membrane differentiations studied in this

chapter.

The latter can be further classified by their function

into:

  • Anchoring junctions

  • Tight junctions

  • Communicating junctions

Microvilli

Microvilli are fingerlike projections in the apical

surface of epithelial cells that help increasing the

surface area of absorption (fig. 6-2, A) . These

structures are approximately 1 m in length and 0.08

m in width. Inside, they are stabilized by a bundle of

20-30 actin filaments, linked together by villin and

fimbrin . These membrane differentiations are

anchored to the terminal web, which contains

different filamentous proteins. The microvilli have a

highly developed glycocalyx, which can be seen under

the optic microscope using saccharide specific stains,

such as PAS (periodic acid Schiff) (fig. 6-2,B).

Figure 6.2. Structure of microvilli in enterocytes (Ent). EChE:

brush border; CC: Goblet cell. MB: Basal membrane. MV:

Microvilli. GC: Glycocalyx.

2

Microvilli can be scarce in some epithelial cells, but in

some others they are very abundant, as is the case

for epithelia of the intestine, proximal convoluted

tubules and choroid plexus.

In the intestinal epithelium, microvilli (fig. 6-2, C) play

an essential role in the absorption of nutrients, as

they increase the surface area of the membrane,

which contains many transporter glycoproteins.

Several enzymes, responsible for the final stages of

protein and sugar digestion, can be found on the

microvilli´ surface. These enzymes can be altered by

different causes, resulting in pathologies, either by

defect (such as lactose intolerance) or by excessive

absorption (such as hemochromatosis). Lactose

intolerance usually develops during childhood,

although its first symptoms may appear in adulthood.

In patients with this disease, among other alterations,

there is a secondary deficiency of lactases (enzymes

found in the membrane of the microvilli of

enterocytes). The function of these lactases is to split

the lactose ingested in foods into its two basic

components: glucose and galactose, which will later

be absorbed. When there is partial or complete lack

of this enzyme, lactose will not be digested, resulting

in diarrhea. Hemochromatosis is an autosomal

recessive disease characterized by an excessive iron

build-up in different organs. This disease is

characterized by gene mutations, which determines

the over-expression in the intestinal microvilli of the

DMT-1 transporter, whose function is to absorb iron.

Therefore, when an increase in this protein in the

epithelial surface occurs (as is the case in

hemochromatosis), there is an excessive iron

absorption. Because of its high concentration in the

patient´s body, iron accumulates in different organs

such as the liver heart (causing cardiomyopathy) and

hypophysis.

Microfolds: interdigitations and basal infoldings

2

[PDF p. 71]

Microfolds are narrow evaginations/invaginations of

the cellular membrane on its contact surface with

other cells or with the ECM. They can either be on

the lateral surfaces (interdigitations or lateral

infoldings), which allows to reinforce epithelial cell

junction (fig. 6-3, A) or on the epithelial cell´s basal

membrane, known as basal infoldings (fig. 6-3, B) .

These latter ones can be found in the distal

convoluted tubules of the kidney, where they are

deep and numerous. The cytoplasm within the basal

infoldings contains lots of mitochondria. This

arrangement relates to the osmotic regulation

performed by these cells via ionic transfer, which

requires a large energy source. The invaginations

increase the surface area of the basal membrane on

which the ion transporter proteins are located.

Figure 6.3. Microfoldings of the plasma membrane. A: Int:

Lateral infolding. D: Desmosome. N1: Nucleus of cell 1. N2:

Nucleus of cell 2. B: Basal infolding (PB). MT: Mitochondria.

Anchoring cell junctions

Cells adhere to one another and to the ECM through

specific membrane molecules called cell adhesion

molecules. These molecules are mostly

transmembrane proteins which, together with other

associated proteins, can be located in specific sites in

the plasma membrane. The transmembrane proteins

that participate in cell adhesion belong, mostly, to

four families: cadherins, integrins, selectins and

cellular adhesion molecules of the immunoglobulin

superfamily (IgSF-CAM, immunoglobulin superfamily

cell adhesion molecules). These transmembrane

3

proteins link to proteins of neighboring cells or the

EMC (in the case of integrins). Proteins of these

families can be monomeric (IgSF-CAM and selectins)

or appear in a dimeric form; and so, cadherins form

homodimers (dimers formed by the same type of

protein), while integrins form heterodimers (dimers

constituted by two different types of protein) (table

6-1) .

There is a large number of proteins associated to

these transmembrane proteins, usually located on the

cytoplasmic side of the membrane, participating in

the structuring and stabilization of the junction and

its anchoring to the cellular cytoskeleton. When an

adhesion protein from one cell binds to another of

the same type on a neighboring cell, we refer to this

as homophilic bonds, while if it binds to different

proteins (or other types of molecules), we refer to it

as heterophilic bonds . These junctions can also be

either of stable or temporary (v. table 6-1).

Cadherins

Cadherins constitute a family of proteins that contains

Ca2+ binding sites (fig. 6-4, A) . Cadherins form

homodimers and make Ca2+ dependent homophilic

bonds. More than 40 different proteins belong to the

cadherin family, among which we find E-cadherin

(found in epithelial cells) and N-cadherin (present in

nerve tissue cells, the lens of the eye, and skeletal

and cardiac muscle cells).

3

[PDF p. 72]

Figure 6.4. Structure of cadherins (A). Immunofluorescence

image of E-cadherin (in red) in epithelial cells in culture.

Cadherins can be grouped in specific cell locations to

form complex anchoring (or adhesion) structures.

These structures are typically found in epithelial cells,

although they may also be present in other tissues.

There are two fundamental classes of complex

anchoring structures which are cadherin dependent:

  1. Zonula adherens or adhesion belt.

  2. Macula adherens or desmosome.

We will now analyze the basic organization of each of

these structures and some of the pathologies that

arise when they get disorganized or undergo

alterations.

ZONULA ADHERENS OR ADHESION BELT

The zonula adherens is a 0.1-0.5 m wide cellular belt

with adhering function, normally found on the apical

surface of the lateral cell membranes, which perform

a vital role in the anchoring of epithelial cells (fig. 6-5,

A). Desmocollins and desmogleins (two types of

cadherins) are the transmembrane proteins that make

up these structures, whose cytoplasmic end joins to

an adhesion plate which contains desmoplakin,

plakophilin and plakoglobin (fig. 6-5, B). The

adhesion plate is bound to actin filaments by

intermediary proteins. The actin filaments extend

through the cytoplasm, forming an intracytoplasmic

anchoring network.

MACULA ADHERENS OR DESMOSOME

4

Macula adherens (also known as a desmosome) is

0.1-0.5 m point shaped junction usually located in

the lateral membranes of epithelial cells, providing

anchorage between them (fig. 6-5, C) . We can also

find desmosomes in intercalated disks that bind

adjacent cardiomyocytes, where they play a key role

in maintaining cardiac tissue structure. Desmosome

forming transmembrane proteins are also cadherins

( desmocollins and desmogleins ). In this sense,

desmosomes are structurally equivalent to adhesion

belts, but they have a pointed morphology (fig. 6-5,

D) . In this case, the desmosome adhesion plate is

directly attached to intermediate filaments, rather

than to actin filaments. In the skin, intermediate

filaments are keratin (tonofilaments), desmin in the

intercalated discs of cardiomyocytes and vimentin in

other tissues.

There are different types of desmogleins, which are

expressed differently depending on the tissue type.

The main desmogleins in the epidermis of the skin

(where desmosomes are abundant) are desmogleins 1

and 3. Two dermatological diseases related to

desmogleins are pemphigus vulgaris and

pemphigus foliaceus . These diseases are

characterized by the presence of blisters on the

cutaneous surface of patients. Pemphigus vulgaris

(PV) is characterized by blisters, on the surface of the

skin, which burst easily (fig. 6-6, A) . These lesions can

be found in the oral mucosa (fig 6-6, B) or extend as

far as the skin of the face, neck, extremities and torso.

Patients refer itchiness and pain in the affected areas,

and they commonly have infections due to lesion

ulceration. An anatomopathological exam of PV

shows separation between keratinocytes in the skin,

known as acantholysis (fig. 6-6, C) .

4

[PDF p. 73]

Figure 6.5. Structure of cell adhesion structures. A.

Representation of desmosomes, adhesion belts and

hemidesmosomes. Structure of an adhesion belt. C. Electron

microscopy image of a desmosome. D. Structure of a

desmosome.

These lesions are usually found above the basal layer

of the epidermis . Immunofluorescence biopsy

analysis reveals an IgG deposit on the keratinocyte

surface, due to the autoimmune nature of the

disease. Patient´s autoantibodies target desmoglein 3

(mainly expressed on the basal layer of skin and

mucosa), desmoglein 1 (found mostly in the

superficial layers of the skin and less commonly the

mucosae) or target both. Autoantibodies are

responsible for desmosome deconstruction in

keratinocytes (where they are very abundant) and for

blister appearance. This disease has a symptomatic

treatment that involves corticosteroids and

immunosuppressive drugs, as well as pain killers and

antibiotics to control infections. Pemphigus foliaceus

is also an autoimmune disease where an IgG deposit

is observed targeting desmoglein 1. Therefore, the

blisters will only be found in layers above the

epidermis and will burst easily. The prognosis for this

disease is better than that for PV, and its treatment

5

consists of the use of systemic or intralesional

corticosteroids.

Figure 6.6. Macroscopic images of blisters (A) and oral

lesions (B) in a patients with pemphigus vulgaris. C.

Histological image of acantholysis of the skin.

Integrins

Integrins are a family of proteins that participate in

the permanent or transient cell-EMC or cell-cell

adhesion. They are heterodimers made up by two

different subunits: α subunit and β subunit (fig. 6-7,

A). The -subunit has binding sites for divalent

cations (Ca2+. Mg2+). These molecules bind to both

ECM components and to intracellular cytoskeleton

proteins by means of interaction with intermediary

proteins that interact with actin filaments, but may

also bind to intermediate keratin filaments . The

extracellular domain of the -subunit can bind to

extracellular proteins ( laminin and fibronectin ).

Integrins play an important role in the migration

processes of embryonic and immune cells.

These proteins can be found as simple molecules or

in groups forming complex adhesion structures, which

constitute hemidesmosomes and focal adhesions .

Hemidesmosomes are stable adhesion structures

typically found in sessile cells, while focal adhesions

5

[PDF p. 74]

are more commonly found in cells in motion. Focal

adhesions are constantly undergoing a process of

formation and destruction.

HEMIDESMOSOMES

Hemidesmosomes (fig. 6-7, B) are structures that,

based on their morphology, would resemble half of a

desmosome located in the basal part of the cell.

These structures allow anchoring of the cell to the

extracellular basal membrane in which the cell rests.

However, the proteins that make up

hemidesmosomes are different to those found in

desmosomes, both the transmembrane anchoring

proteins (in this case integrins instead of cadherins)

and the adhesion plate proteins. The integrin that

constitutes hemidesmosomes is the  -6  -4-integrin,

and the proteins found in the plaque are known as

plakins . Another transmembrane protein found in

hemidesmosomes is BPGA2 (bullous pemphingoid

antigen-2). The plaque is connected to tonofilaments

in the cytoplasmic side (fig. 6-7, B) .

Hemidesmosomes increase the overall stability of

epithelial tissues and allow them to settle in the ECM

by binding of cytoskeletal tonofilaments to basal

membrane components.

Figure 6.7. A. Structure of integrins. B. Representation of a

hemidesmosome. C. Structure of focal adhesions.

6

FOCAL ADHESIONS

Migratory cells bind to the ECM via focal adhesions

(fig. 6-7, C), formed by clusters of integrins

interconnected with bundles of actin filaments by

means of other intermediary proteins. These

structures are very dynamic, in a process of constant

formation and destruction during the process of cell

migration, where they provide a connection at

specific points with the substrate. They are abundant

in lamellipodia (see chapter 12), which cells use to

travel through an inert substrate. Not only focal

adhesions have an adhesion function, they also

participate in intracellular signaling. Thus, integrin

interaction with the substrate in focal adhesions

results in phosphorylation of the focal adhesion

tyrosine kinase protein (FAK), a protein that

promotes the activation of proteins involved in

cellular migration and protects cells from anoikis.

Integrins act as signal transductors, keeping the cell

informed as to the conditions (mainly adhesion) of

the extracellular surroundings. FAK hyperactivation

has been associated with an increased metastatic

capacity of tumor cells.

Integrins also mediate cell-cell contacts. These

interactions are usually temporary and occur in the

adhesion processes of immune system cells. Within

these integrins we can highlight the so-called “LFA-1”,

or lymphocyte function-associated antigen-1, as well

as Mac-1 (its equivalent in macrophages). These

integrins find their receptor in ICAM and VCAM

molecules (see below) on the endothelial cell surface,

thus reinforcing the interaction produced by selectins

(see selectins, below) in the leukocyte extravasation

process.

Selectins

Selectins are Ca2+ dependent adhesion molecules,

with affinity for carbohydrates found in other cells

(fig. 6-8, A) . Rather than forming complex binding

6

[PDF p. 75]

structures, they are involved in molecular contacts

that are not visible under the microscope and are

generally temporary . They frequently participate in

the binding of leukocytes to endothelial cells,

facilitating their exit from the blood vessels to an

inflammatory site (fig. 6-8, B) .

Figure 6.8. A. Structure of selectins. B. Participation of

selections (and other adhesion molecules) in the

attachment of neutrophils to endothelial cells and

extravasation to the sites of infection.

Most leukocytes have L-selectins in their plasma

membrane (‘L’ for leukocyte), which interacts with its

receptor via heterophilic bonds in the plasma

membrane of endothelial cells that coat blood

vessels. Receptors for selectins are oligosaccharides

contained in glycoproteins (such as IgSF-CAM) or

plasma membrane glycolipids. Additionally,

endothelial cells themselves have two types of

selectins: P-selectin (‘P’ for ‘platelet’ where the

expression of this selectin is highly abundant) and E-

selectin (‘E’ for “endothelium’), which in turn interact

with oligosaccharides on the leukocyte surface.

Selectins are involved in inflammation and cancer . In

inflammatory processes, certain stimuli (such as

histamine and thrombin) stimulate the endothelial

cells to produce P-selectin on the cell surface. Some

cytokines (such as TNF-) also increase E- and P

selectin expression in the endothelium, thus favoring

the leukocyte binding. This then leads to a tighter

junction, mediated by IgSF-CAM integrins (see IgSF

7

CAM integrins below) and, finally, leukocyte migration

to the inflammatory sites.

IGSF-CAM

The adhesion molecules of the IgSF-CAM

immunoglobulin superfamily (fig. 6-9) possess bow

shaped domains, similar to those found in

immunoglobulins. They form heterophilic bonds

(interacting with integrins and selectins) or

homophilic bonds (binding to other IgSF-CAM)

(fig.6-9) . IgSF-CAMs have no Ca2+ binding sites and

regulate cell junctions, which are normally temporary .

This protein superfamily is involved in adhesions of

immune system cells. We can highlight the following

IgSF-CAMs:

  • Intercellular adhesion molecules (ICAM) and vascular

cell adhesion molecules (VCAM). They are present in

many cell types, especially in endothelial cells,

regulating interactions with blood cells, which have

integrins present on their membranes.

  • Nerve cell adhesion molecules (NCAM). These are

found in the neurons and are bound by homophilic

interaction to other NCAMs.

Figure 6.9. A. Structure of IgSF-CAM. B. Interaction

between two molecules of IgSF-CAM, or between IgSF-CAM

and selectins.

Occluding junctions (Zonula occludens or tight

junction)

Occluding junctions (or ‘tight junctions’) are belt

shaped contacts, 1 m in width, found almost

7

[PDF p. 76]

exclusively in epithelial cells (fig. 6-10) . This occlusive

belt is formed by a network of proteins that join the

adjacent cell membranes, leaving the intercellular

space occluded by a system of anastomotic crests of

varying complexity. This can be seen, particularly, in

the electron microscopy cryo-fracture images (see

fig. 6-10, C) .

Tight junctions perform two essential roles:

  1. They determine epithelial cells´ membrane polarity,

as they separate the apical domain from the

basolateral one, avoiding the free lipid and protein

diffusion between the two domains.

  1. They prevent substances from crossing between

epithelial cells (paracellular transport). However,

sometimes cells can modify their tight junctions to

allow the passage of certain substances, when those

substances are in such excess that transport through

the membrane is not sufficient to incorporate them.

This is the case of amino acids and sugars. When

they exceed the intestine´s ability to absorb them,

they can be incorporated by paracellular transport.

The closure ridges are formed by interlocking rows of

transmembrane proteins (Fig. 6-10, B), particularly

occludins and claudins, which bind directly to similar

proteins in the adjoining cell.

Figure 6.10. A. Structure of junctional complexes: UO:

Occluding junction; CA: Adhesion belt; DE: Desmosome. B.

Structure of occluding junctions. C. Electron microscopy

image of an occluding junction.

Communicating junctions (gap, nexus or cleft

junctions)

Communicating junctions are also called “nexus

junctions”, cleft junctions” or “gap junctions”. They are

8

macular junctions of varying sizes that communicate

the cytoplasm of neighboring cells, allowing direct

transfer of ions and macromolecules (fig 6-11) . The

basic unit in communicating junctions is the

connexon . Cryo-fracture and negative contrast

studies show that the connexons form a hexagonal

grid. Each connexon is about 6.5 nm in diameter and

its centers lie equidistant by about 9 nm. With

negative contrast we can see that each connexon is

made up of six molecules of connexin (a

transmembrane protein) around a central duct. The

arrangement therefore creates a direct channel of

communication (1.5-2 nm in diameter) between the

cytoplasm of the two adjacent cells. Each nexus-type

junction may contain from a few to hundreds of

connexons, which may form plaques around 0.3 mm

in diameter. These junctions allow movement of

molecules up to 1.2 nm in diameter (such as

inorganic ions, oligosaccharides, amino acids,

nucleotides, vitamins and cAMP) between cells. These

junctions are Ca2+ independent, but the permeability

of the channels is regulated by Ca2+ and pH : it

decreases when pH decreases or when intracellular

Ca2+ concentrations increase. When cells are

damaged, there is a massive and instantaneous Ca2+

influx into the cell, so closure of these channels

prevents any possible damage from being transmitted

to neighboring cells.

This junction is responsible for the electrochemical

coupling between adjacent cells, which is particularly

relevant in the communication between some

neurons and between cardiac muscular cells, where

impulses must be conveyed quickly and

synchronously. As explained in chapter 4, electric

synapses are formed by gap type junctions. Another

important example of gap junctions function is the

ciliated epithelial cells of the respiratory tract. The

synchronous movement of the cilia to remove mucus

from the airways requires rapid communication of

signaling molecules through the cytoplasm of

adjacent cells, which is achieved via gap junctions.

8

[PDF p. 77]

The family of genes coding for the connexins has

many members, designated according to their

molecular weight.

Figure 6.11. A. Structure of gap junctions. B. Structure of a

connexon. C. Electron microscopy image of a gap junction.
