9

[PDF p. 78]

Lecture 4.1. Mechanisms of cell signalling

The coordination between cells and tissues is essential (homeostasis), which occurs through signalling mechanisms which trigger a cell response.

Types of cell communication

A. Throughout secreted molecules

B. Cell-to-cell or cell-to-ECM

C. GAP junctions : direct communication between two cells

Cell signalling characteristics

  1. Response through specific receptors (membrane of cytoplasmic): We find both: hydrophilic ligands which bind membrane receptors, and hydrophobic ligands which bind cytoplasmic receptors.

     Hydrophilic ligands: bind receptor proteins in plasma membrane,

which triggers an intracellular signalling cascade via effector proteins (cytoplasmic proteins and transcription factors) and leads to the cell’s response.

    Hydrophobic ligands: diffuse through the plasma membrane

and bind cytoplasmic receptors. These ligand-receptor complexes then act as transcription factors or cytoplasmic enzymes that activate second messengers. It ultimately leads to the cell’s response.

  1. Intracellular signalling cascade (protein activation and gene transcription)

  2. On/off system: inactivation of signalling cascade

     Receptor mediated endocytosis (pinocytosis)

 Phosphatases (inactivate proteins activated by kinases)

 GTP dissociation to GDP + Pi (GTPase, active when bound to GPT, activating

proteins, GAPs) 4. Integration of simultaneous signals (which leads to signalling output) 5. Amplification of the signalling process: 1 rhodopsin molecule can generate 500 phospho-diesterases, which can in turn generate 10^5 cGMP

A. CELL COMMUNICATION THROUGH SECRETED MOLECULES

Signalling molecules can bind to extracellular or intracellular receptors. There are different types of signalling:

  • Autocrine (local mediator)

  • Paracrine (local mediator)

  • Endocrine (distal mediator, it works with hormones)

  • Neurotransmitters (it involves neurons, and it is close-range)

There are also different types of signalling molecules:

  • Signalling through HYDROPHOBIC LIGANDS (which diffuse through the membrane):

[PDF p. 79]

  • Steroid hormones: these include sex hormones (androgens, estrogens,…) and corticosteroids (glucocorticvoids and mineralocorticoids). These molecules have a lipidic nature, their precursor is cholesterol.

    • Vitamin D, a liposoluble vitamin also derived from cholesterol. Involved in calcium metabolism and bone remodelling.

    Nitric Oxide (NO) . Acts as a smooth muscle relaxant, which is why it’s used to treat hypertension and erectile dysfunction.

    • Eicosanoids : derived from arachidonic acid. Related to inflammation.

Examples of signalling through hydrophobic ligands:

 Example of hormone signalling: steroid hormones and estrogen receptors

Steroid hormones require specific receptors, located in the cytoplasm. When the ligand binds to the receptor, it is going to induce dimerization. The newly formed complex will carry the message to the nucleus by inducing gene transcription. The same molecule acts as receptor and transmission factor. - Example of Gas signalling: NO and smooth muscle NO regulates blood vessel contraction by decreasing muscle tension. There are molecules that can provide an NO group, such as Nitroglycerine or aspirin. NO will bind an intracellular receptor and increase the blood vessel

diameter.

  • Signalling through HYDROPHILIC LIGANDS (bind extracellular receptors: GPCRs, Tyrosine kinases,…):

    • Neurotransmitters : Acetylcholine, dopamine, and adrenaline. We also find others such as histamine, serotonin, glutamate and GABA.

    Peptide hormones : insulin and glucagon, and others such as prolactin, growth hormone (GH), follicle stimulating hormone (FSH),…

    Neuropeptides : natural pain killers such as Enkephalins - Growth factors (ex: EGF, epidermal growth factor)

Examples of signalling through hydrophilic ligands:

 -      Example of G protein coupled receptors

Upon the binding of the corresponding ligand to a 7 transmembrane protein, the GPCR dissociates into its alpha-GTP + beta and gamma subunits, acquiring its active form. This will induce different intracellular responses.

 Example of signalling through Growth factors: Tyrosine kinases

Tyrosine kinases have an extracellular and a cytoplasmic domain, with kinase activity. The ligand binds the extracellular domain, which induces dimerization

[PDF p. 80]

and activates the cytoplasmic domain and its kinase activity. There will be cross-phosphorylation and signal transduction. The EGF-receptor (a tyrosine receptor) can:

➢ Directly activate transcription factors that will travel to the nucleus. ➢ Activate RAS proteins (RAS-RAF-MEK-ERK pathway) (Most cancers

arise from mutations in this family of proteins, leading to incorrect gene expression).

    Example of signalling through Neurotransmitters

Ionic channels can be regulated by the binding of a ligand, such as Ach. Acetylcholine can be released by specific neurons, and increase the concentration of intracellular Na+ by opening sodium channels. This is a chemical synapse (which involves the synaptic space through which a ligand diffuses and then binds). Ach can contribute to the relaxation of cardiac myocytes (as it’s delivered by the PS system), and the contraction of skeletal muscle cells (when delivered by somatic neurons). In the salivary glands, it

contributes to the saliva secretion.

B1. CELL-TO-CELL COMMUNICATION

Contact of membrane proteins of one cell to another cell

Examples of cell-to-cell communication:

  • Activation of T-lymphocytes Through cell-to-cell communication, T cell activation and differentiation occurs. Antigen presenting cells such as dendritic cells and B cells expose the antigen (ligand) bound to an MHC complex on the cell membrane and contact the T lymphocytes which possess the receptor specific to said antigen.

Notch receptor and ligands

B2. CELL-TO-ECM COMMUNICATION

Contact of membrane proteins of one cell to the extracellular matrix

Examples of cell-to-ECM communication:

  • Integrin fibronectin binding The integrin molecules expressed on the cell membrane will bind other proteins in the ECM. This can result in cell differentiation into tissues, migration, proliferation,…

C. COMMUNICATION VIA GAP JUNCTIONS

A protein on one cell membrane binds another protein on the adjacent cell to create gaps (pores) that connect cells (neurons, cardiomyocytes).

Small molecules and ions flow through the pores, allowing a very high signal speed (ex: electrical synapses in neurons, communication between adjacent myocytes,…).

  • Connexons will form through the arrangement of 6 connexin subunits to give rise to these channels

[PDF p. 81]

Lecture 4.2. Cell signalling through GPCRs

Elements of GPCR signalling

  • A 7-pass transmembrane protein receptor.

  • The G protein (alpha, beta and gamma subunits). Alpha and gamma subunits are anchored by covalent

bonds to the membrane.

  • An enzyme/ion channel that produces a 2nd

messenger.

Biological relevance -It’s a family of proteins that is largely represented and encoded for in human DNA. -Regulates multiple signalling processes (Vision, olfaction,…) -They are receptors for many neurotransmitters, such as acetylcholine, serotonin,.. -Many hormones signal through this system -They participate in inflammation processes (histamine)

G-proteins

They are heterotrimeric (alpha,beta and gamma subunits). When G protein is bound to GDP, it’s inactivated, but when it binds to GTP it’s activated, and dissociates into alpha-GTP and beta-gamma complex. It’s only the alpha subunit which binds to gdp/gtp. They regulate enzymes and create ion channels. Alpha subunit separates from beta-gamma complex, and they both become activated.

- Types of G proteins : -Gs (stimulatory): induce cAMP production -Gi (inhibitory): inhibit cAMP synthesis

An example of this would be the effect of adrenaline: it binds to beta-adrenergic receptors in hepatocytes. Once bound, the G-alpha subunit functions as Gs and induces cAMP. Enzymes increase glucose in the blood, preparing the body for fight-flight response. Adrenaline can also bind to alpha-adrenergic receptors in smooth muscle cells. In this case, the G protein acts as an inhibitor (Gi). It inhibits cAMPproduction and induces muscle

relaxation.

EFFECTORS

a) Adenylate cyclase : It’s anchored to the plasma membrane, and when bound to alpha-G-GTP, it’s activated and can convert ATP into cAMP (cyclic adenosine monophosphate) which acts as a second messenger. The elimination of excess cAMP is carried out by cAMP phosphodiesterase.

- cAMP effects:

Fast response: doesn’t require the expression of genes. The opening of ion channels leads to a depolarization of the membrane. For example, the process that gives us our sense of smell: we can detect specific smells because when a certain component binds to a receptor, this G protein pathway is activated, which opens Na+ ion channels. This depolarizes membranes and activates a nerve impulse that is detected in the brain.

[PDF p. 82]

Slower response: it involves the binding and activating of PKA (protein kinaseA): they can target cytoplasmic substances, activate transmission factors (CREB). PKA

can sometimes also involve the activation of ion

channels, but generally it acts as an intermediary that delays the activation or signalling process.

PKA is composed of 4 subunits, 2 homodimers: 2 regulatory subunits, and 2 catalytic subunits which have kinase activity.

Alteration of GPCR in the intestine and respiratory system by Cholera toxin and Bordetella Pertussis (here we can see the importance of shutting off signalling):

  • Cholera toxin is going to activate alphaG subunit, so the protein kinase A will be constantly activated. It binds to a stimulatory G subunit, and induces an activation of the G protein. It stimulates the opening of ion

channels and release of Cl-. Water exits

enterocytes to equilibrate osmotic

concentration and results in diarrhea.

  • The Bordetella toxin will act by inhibiting the inhibition, thus increasing PKA activity.

b) Phospholipase C-beta : also found in the membrane, has to be bound to G-GTP protein to become activated. It results in the production of inositol-3-phosphate and DAG. I3P is a second messenger that induces the release of calcium ; DAG will stimulate the release of PKC and the production of arachidonic acid, a precursor of eicosanoids. Basically, phospholipase liberates secondary messengers that will have different effects on the cell.

[PDF p. 83]

c) Guanylate cyclase : Once activated through G proteins, it generates cyclic GMP, which comes from GTP. This cGMP is activated through NO . In muscles, high cGMP levels induce smooth muscle relaxation. It functions for example in the process of sight, how we distinguish dark from light. Brain detects dark when cGMP is high. The rapid entry of ions generates a depolarization of the plasma membrane which results in a rapid nerve impulse to the brain. This stimulus indicates the absence of light. When the GPCR is activated,

cGMP is converted to GTP and the ion channels are closed. The membrane becomes

hyperpolarized, and the brain senses this stimulus as the presence of light.

Example of GPCR signalling: release of glucagon and insulin ( beta cells=insulin, alpha-cells=glucagon).

➢ Regulation of glycemia:

When there is high glucose in blood, beta cells induce the production of insulin which reduces glucose blood levels. It stimulates the glycogen storage in the liver (can also happen in the muscle by forming saccharide-bound proteins, or in fat by forming lipidic droplets). If blood levels are low, the body releases glucagon and these processes are reverted.

Signal amplification process: one molecule that stimulates receptors can stimulate the activation of thousands of signalling processes.

[PDF p. 84]

Lecture 4.3. Signalling through ion channels

Relevance and main characteristics

Ion channels are present in every cell, but they are particularly important in electrically excitable cells (neurons, muscles,…)

Characteristics:

-Ions move down the concentration gradient (from less to more) -They have a very high rate of transport, no need to interact with receptors. -Two major types of ion channel receptors:

voltage gated: the channel is regulated by changes in membrane potential, it will open or close depending on that. In the resting situation, membrane potential is such that there is a negative charge in the inner membrane. A depolarization involves a positive charge inside the membrane and negative outside. This promotes that the protein channels open and ions

can enter the cell.

               **ligand- gated:** channels open or close depending on the

binding of one specific molecule like acetylcholine. In the case of Ach and Na+: two acetylcholine molecules bind to the channel, which opens and allows sodium to enter the cell.

-Main ions involved: Na+, K+, Cl- and Ca 2+

Acetylcholine and its receptors

There are many receptors for Acetylcholine . It’s a very plastic molecule, it can act through different types of receptors and have a very different outcome, depending on the receptor and the cell. We can differentiate two types of receptors:

  • Nicotinic: Called nicotinic because this channel can also be activated by nicotine. That’s why nicotine is an agonist of Ach. Ach binds to an ion channel receptor.
  • Muscarinic: Ach binds to a G Protein Coupled receptor (GPCR). Called muscarinic because it also binds to muscarine, a toxin from mushrooms.

How acetylcholine acts in neuromuscular junction:

First, there is a depolarization of the membrane. This depolarization causes the release of calcium into the cell ( the presence of Ca2+ in a cell generally promotes exocytosis of vesicles ), which promotes the exocytosis of vesicles with Ach into the synaptic space. It then binds to its receptor in the muscle fiber . The channel opens and Na+ enters .

[PDF p. 85]

This propagates the stimulus in the muscle fiber: the axon potential propagates through the membrane invaginations ( T tubules, because they are in a T shape). These tubules, at some point, contact with the sarcoplasmic reticulum / SER in muscle fibers. The depolarization and the close contact between membrane and reticulum opens Ca2+ channels, promoting its release into the cytosol. This will then cause muscle contraction, as Ca2+ binds to troponin and tropomyosin, leaving the actin’s active site free for myosin

heads to bind to.

To stop contraction, Ca2+ must be removed from the cytosol. For this there are calcium pumps that actively (with energy consumption) move

calcium back into the SER.

To stop the whole process, acetylcholine is either diffused or broken down by an enzyme found in the synaptic cleft ( acetylcholine-esterase ) into acetic acid and choline, that is reabsorbed to

generate more acetylcholine. (Sarcolemma=membrane of a muscle fiber)

Related pathologies. Alteration of the signaling process: MYASTHENIA GRAVIS

It’s an autoimmune disease where

antibodies are produced against acetylcholine receptors. They bind to the receptors and block them, so Ach cannot bind properly to the receptor in the channel. It isn’t a complete blockage, otherwise the patient

would die in a few minutes. There is

some muscle contraction, but it’s impaired . Antibodies bound to the receptors are also then incorporated through endocytosis into the cell, because the antibodies stimulate this process. The channel and the antibody are swallowed and destroyed by lysosomes, so as a result there is a lesser amount of receptors and channels. There is less signalling but also less receptors. The effect of this is muscle weakness (ex: dropped eyelids/ptosis, lacking facial expressions,…), extreme fatigue,…

[PDF p. 86]

The treatment: using immunosuppressors (corticosteroids) to block antibody production, or

the use of AchE inhibitors: it blocks the activity of acetylcholinesterase, and keeps the signalling going for longer. Some AchE inhibitors may be fatal, but used in small doses with this pathology can improve movements.

Toxins that interfere with signalling process

  • Tetanus toxin: produced by clostridium tetani, which causes hyperactivation of signalling. Too much Ach is released, causing muscle rigidity, spasms and paralysis due to hyperactivity.
  • Botulinic toxin: produced by clostridium botulinum . The bacteria blocks the exocitosis process that relesases Ach, and therefore there is no signalling. As a result there is muscle inactivity, which can also be fatal (hypoactivation). Nowadays it can be used for the treatment of migraines, muscle spasms or hyperhidrosis (increased sweating).

[PDF p. 87]

TBL 4.1. Signalling through tyrosine kinases

Cellular communication is possible thanks to signals that stimulate receptors. We find intracellular receptors, and membrane receptors that we will study today.

SIGNALING THROUGH MEMBRANE RECEPTORS

Membrane receptors are amphipathic molecules that collect extracellular signals and transmit them to the interior of the cell, activating a protein cascade that will result in gene transcription and the production of a protein. There are 3 types of membrane proteins:

Ligand gated ion channels

G protein coupled receptors

Enzymatic or catalytic receptors. We will focus on these last ones:

Enzymatic or catalytic membrane receptors are enzymes that once activated, due to ligand bind, enhance the signal transmission through cytoplasm proteins.They have an extracellular domain (where ligand binding occurs), a transmembrane domain and an intracellular domain (which confers the catalytic activity).

This catalytic activity can be a kinase activity: kinases are enzymes that catalyze the transfer of a phosphate group from a donor ATP to a substrate. This process regulates many intracellular signalling cascades.

Tyrosine-kinase receptors (RTK)

Like all membrane receptors, they possess an extracellular domain, cross-membrane domain and intracellular domain. Once activated by a ligand, they enhance the phosphorylation of tyrosine residues, which will lead to a cellular response. Tyrosine-kinase is a dimer. The ligand binds to either one or two subunits. When in monomeric form, they are inactive . After the binding of the signal molecule, the monomers join and form the dimer. In order to get activated, a cross-phosphorylation has to occur by consuming ATP. Active form = phosphorylated dimer.

An example of a tyrosine kinase signalling is the RAS pathway. RAS is a membrane protein that belongs to a small GTPase protein family,

which carries out the

RAS-RAF-MEK-ERK

pathway. At the end of the process, ERK suppresses upstream pathway signalling.

[PDF p. 88]

30% of human cancers are related to a hyperstimulation of this pathway. This hyperstimulation stimulates uncontrolled growth and tumor appearance. Action mechanism: a ligand binds to the receptor, and the second subunit binds to the 1st RTK. Then cross-phosphorylation occurs. Now activated, they can recruit the intracellular proteins. RAS is activated by GEF and stays attached to the membrane. Many RAS proteins are activated = signal amplification. Then RAF is activated, joining RAS and forming an inner structure. Then MEK and ERK are activated. Activated ERK translocates to the nucleus and stimulates many processes that end in gene transcription and a cellular response. The medical relevance of tyrosine-kinase receptors is that many drugs developed to treat cancer act on RTK, either targeting ATP binding sites, interfering with RKT activation or targetting RTK-expressing cells for destruction by the immune system.

Ovarian cancer

Known as a silent killer. It doesn’t show specific symptoms, and these appear at a late stage, when there has already been a metastasis. Therefore it’s difficult to treat and cure. Increased angiogenesis stimulates the formation of new blood vessels around the cell. The tumor enhances angiogenesis by taking advantage of EGF (growth factor). Therefore angiogenesis is targeted to stop tumor growth. Angiogenesis can be stopped by blocking EGF.

RTK-Insulin receptor

Another RTK receptor is the insulin receptor . It’s a receptor that is very abundant in cells of the liver, adipose tissue and skeletal muscles. It’s a dimer made up of two extracellular

subunits and two intracellular subunits. When

insulin binds, intracellular tyrosine domains

become activated and there is a downstream

cascade that leads to the translocation of

GLUT4 transporters, which allows the transport of glucose into the interior of the cell. If glucose in the bloodstream decreases, the GLUT4 transmembrane proteins are

stored in vesicles and removed from the

membrane.

[PDF p. 89]

When insulin receptor does not work properly, it leads to insulin resistance (a stage where cells cannot respond to insulin and therefore can’t take up glucose from the blood. It implies a higher-than-normal insulin concentration to maintain normal glucose levels) and type II diabetes. Overweight and people with obesity, 45 or older, certain ethnicities, high cholesterol levels, are all factors that can lead to this disease. Molecular mechanisms that lead to insulin resistance are still unknown. It can be due to mutations and dysfunctions in proteins, mitochondrial dysfunction,….

What is DIABETES?

It’s a chronic disease where pancreas can’t produce enough insulin or the body cannot use the insulin it produces.

Type I diabetes is an autoimmune disease where the body cannot produce enough insulin because autoantibodies attack beta-cells in the pancreas (those in charge of insulin production).

Type II diabetes is a metabolic disease which prevents the little insulin which the body can produce (it’s produced, although not enough) from working properly. It can’t be taken up by cells, and therefore the body doesn’t recognize high glucose levels in the blood. Factors that can impact type II diabetes: mutations in genes resulting in mutated proteins, environmental factors such as weight and diet,…

[PDF p. 90]

Lecture 5.1. Bioenergetics

Forms of obtaining carbon and energy:

Metabolic pathways are a sequence of reactions that, through the continuous modification of a precursor, transform it into a product. These reactions are catalyzed by enzymes.

Types of chemical reactions in metabolism:

-Transfer of functional groups

-Oxidation and reduction

-Isomerization

-Hydrolysis/condensation -Formation or cleavage of C-C bonds

Metabolic phases

  • Catabolism: degradative phase in which nutrients are degraded into their simpler subunits, to obtain precursors (CO2, H2O, NH3). Two main goals: to obtain energy and to obtain precursors . The main strategy to do this is the oxidation of the carbon atoms, by removing electrons. This produces energy, which is stored as ATP, and the released electrons are collected and stored in molecules called electron carriers. They collect e- from the carbon molecules which are oxidized.

  • Anabolism: It’s a process in which precursors obtained from catabolism are used, through ATP consumption, to generate macromolecules that our body needs to function (lipids, proteins,…).

Types of metabolic pathways

The body’s interest in making the most of its resources is reflected in the metabolic pathways. All nutrients are converged towards a common element (normally Acetyl-CoA ) so the same enzymes can be used at the end of the catabolic pathway. Catabolism is convergent, and anabolism is divergent .

[PDF p. 91]

Catabolic stages

1st stage: big molecules are degraded into small molecules: fats into fatty acids and glycerol, polysaccharides into glucose and other sugars, and proteins into amino acids. This occurs by hydrolysis, which doesn’t require energy.

2nd stage: small molecules are further broken down to finally obtain Acetyl-CoA, which is the main goal of this phase (although some energy is released). It’s a convergent phase.

3rd stage: Acetyl-CoA undergoes a series of reactions (Krebs cycle/citric acid cycle). Most of the ATP is generated in this phase.

Regulatory mechanisms in metabolism

  • Control of enzyme concentration
  • Control of enzyme activity: either by allosteric control or covalent modification (such as phosphorylation)

Compartmentalization of enzymes and substrates (ex: cellular respiration only happens in the mitochondria)

Pathways can be regulated by the own products of the pathway.

ATP

Adenosine triphosphate is broken down to produce ADP or AMP, by hydrolysis, which releases a big amount of energy that is used by our body.

[PDF p. 92]

Lecture 5.2. Electron transport

Gibbs free energy change lets us know about the direction of a reaction. The direction in which the free energy change is negative will be the spontaneous reaction, and therefore, the direction in which the process will take place. The free energy change is not a fixed number, it changes towards equilibrium. Gibbs free energy provides NO info about the reaction rate. That depends on the activation energy and the transition state.

How to calculate the free energy change of a reaction knowing the conditions: (R= gas constant: 8.3 J/mol) )

Example: Glucose 6P to FRuctose 6P

It ‘s positive. How come this process occurs in our cell? Depending on the concentration of the molecules, the free energy rate will be positive or negative in one direction. The direction will therefore be determined by the concentration of molecules. The free energy change changes with the molecule concentration.

What is the ratio in the equilibrium? We equal the equation to 0. This ratio will determine if a reaction is reversible or irreversible . If it’s too high, it’s not possible in physiological conditions, and therefore won’t happen.

In conclusion: all processes that take place in a cell are exergonic

Degradation and synthesis follow different processes, because not all reactions are reversible. This allows the cell to regulate the processes independently, avoiding reactions which would be useless. It allows coordinate and reciprocal regulation.

Coupling reactions allows to establish a negative free energy change, permitting irreversible reactions to take place. This can be done by breaking ATP bonds.

There are two main strategies to obtain ATP: Substrate level phosphorylation (because the element we are using has a high phosphorylation potential) and oxidative phosphorylation.

[PDF p. 93]

Carbon oxidation releases energy

Malate to oxaloacetate = oxidation. Electrons released are collected by NAD+, which becomes NADH (its reduced form). When there is an oxidation, there also has to be a

reduction.

Electron carriers in metabolism: NAD +, NADP +, FAD and FMN. NAD+ and NADP+, made up of a nicotinamide ring which is the active site of the molecule. NADP is more frequently used in anabolism. FAD (flavin adenine diphosphate) and FMN (… mononucleotide). They typically participate in reactions in which double bounds are produced.

[PDF p. 94]

Lecture 5.3 and 5.4. Generation of ATP from glucose and other sugars

Glucose is an excellent fuel. Nervous tissue and erythrocytes preferably use glucose and carbohydrates as their main fuel. -Its oxidation yields -2840 kJ/mol glucose after the full oxidation of backbone carbons. It’s highly reduced and contains a high amount of electrons, which will allow to obtain ATP through oxidative phosphorylation. -It can be efficiently stored in polymeric form ( glycogen ). -Many tissues (neurons, erythrocytes,…) can meet their energy needs on glucose only. -It’s a versatile biochemical precursor, as it can be used to generate all amino-acids, membrane lipids,nucleotides,…

We can use glucose to obtain energy through the oxidation of the full carbon backbone.

GLYCOLYSIS

Glycolysis is a series of enzyme-catalyzed reactions where glucose (6C) is broken down into two pyruvate molecules (3C).The most of the energy released during glycolysis remains in the form of NADH and pyruvate . Not much energy is released during glycolysis. Pyruvate can be further oxidized or used as a precursor for biosynthesis. It takes place in the cytoplasm and doesn’t need O2 : it can be done with or without oxygen. Some tissues in our body carry out glycolysis aways in anaerobic conditions, like erythrocytes. They contain hemoglobin with O2, but they can’t use it as they lack mitochondria and so they can’t carry out cellular respiration. When glycolysis happens without O2, the last step is fermentation. This process also happens in skeletal muscles, when their activity is higher than the mitochondrias possibility to reoxidize NADH. We can differentiate two steps in glycolysis: the preparatory phase (glucose is prepared for breakdown. ATP is consumed) and payoff phase (from which we are able to obtain energy).

- Preparatory phase: in this phase, we need energy, so 2 ATPs are consumed, but this will be compensated for in the next phase. First, glucose undergoes phosphorylation, avoiding the possibility for it to be released out of the cell because of the negative charge present on the phosphate. Also this negative charge allows the substrate to establish many interactions with enzyme active sites.

1. Step 1: Glucose phosphorylation (usually catalyzed by kinases: they transfer one phosphate from ATP). This step is catalyzed by hexokinase,

which can also work on fructose and

mannose.

The liver is a carbohydrate-storage organ. It doesn’t express hexokinase, but a different isozyme, glucokinase . The only difference

[PDF p. 95]

between them is their affinity for glucose ( hexokinase more than glucokinase ). This is so the liver, because it stores glucose, doesn’t consume all the glucose and fill the blood with sugars. If not, we would always be in hypoglycemia. For the liver, the main energy-obtaining pathway is beta-oxidation of fatty acids.

2. Step 2: Glucose-6-phosphate isomerization: it’s isomerized into fructose-6-phosphate by phosphohexose isomerase. This step is

reversible.

3. Step 3: 2nd priming phosphorylation : Phosphofructokinase-1 (PFK-1) phosphorylates carbon 1. It’s a critical step in glucose degradation regulation. It’s regulated by the increase of cAMP and ADP (which indicate low energy). It generates fructose-1,6-bisphosphate.

4. Step 4: Aldol cleavage: fructose-1,6-bisphosphate is broken down by an aldolase between carbon 3 and 4. Carbons1,2,3 become a ketone, dihydroxyacetone-phosphate, and carbons 4,5,6 become glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate (G3P).

5. Step 5: Triose phosphate isomerase then converts dihydroxyacetone-phosphate into glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate, so we obtain 2

molecules of G3P . This isomerization will allow the

G3P molecules to undergo subsequent reactions of glycolysis.

- Payoff phase:

6. Step 6: Oxidation of G3P. An inorganic phosphate is added to G3P by an enzyme, glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate dehydrogenase . Electrons are released and captured by NAD+, which converts to NADH . A high energy molecule is produced, 1,3-bisphosphoglycerate.

7. Step 7: 1st production of ATP: It occurs by substrate level phosphorylation. The phospho group in C1 is transferred to ADP by phosphoglycerate kinase.

[PDF p. 96]

The hydrolysis of this bond releases enough energy to phosphorylate ADP, releasing ATP . 3-phosphoglycerate is produced as a product.

8. Step 8: Migration of the phosphate, which is transferred from C3 to C2. We go from 3-phosphoglycerate to 2-phosphoglycerate, a reaction which is catalyzed by phosphoglycerate mutase.

9. Step 9: There is a dehydration carried out by an enolase, turning it to phosphoenolpyruvate (with a double bond). This is the second high-energy intermediate of glycolysis, which will allow to obtain ATP through substrate level phosphorylation in the next stage. It has a high phosphate transfer potential.

10. Step 10: 2nd production of ATP . Phosphoenolpyruvate loses its phospho group. It’s transferred by a pyruvate kinase to an ADP molecule, which results in the synthesis of ATP . Finally, we obtain Pyruvate .

Glycolysis balance: 2ATPs are consumed in the preparatory phase, but out of the two G3P molecules, each yield 2 ATPs. Therefore, the payoff phase gives us 4 ATP. The final net balance is 2 ATPs, 2 NADH + H+ and 2 pyruvate molecules.

Regulation of glycolysis

Glycolysis is regulated through 3 enzymes: hexokinase, phosphofructokinase-1 and pyruvate kinase.

  • Hexokinase is allosterically inhibited by its product, glucose-6-phosphate.

  • Phosphofructokinase is allosterically activated by low energy markers ( AMP and ADP ) and fructose 2,6-bisphosphate, and inhibited by ATP and citrate, when in high amounts, means there is already enough energy in the mitochondria, indicating there is no need to oxidize high amounts of glucose.

  • Pyruvate-kinase has two isozymes: M (skeletal muscle,…) and L (liver). They are allosterically inhibited by ATP, Acetyl-CoA (indicates enough energy is going to be produced) and alanin (because it’s a source of pyruvate. It can be converted to it, so high amounts of alanin indicate the cell there is no need to produce more pyruvate). In addition to this, the L isozyme can undergo a covalent modification by

phosphorylation and dephosphorylation. The phosphorylated form is inactive and the non-phosphorylated form, active. When blood glucose concentration is low, glucagon is released and binds to receptors in the liver. This will increase intracellular cAMP production and promote the activation of PKA (protein kinase A) which will phosphorylate and inactivate pyruvate kinase L. (As it becomes phosphorylated, it goes into inactive form). This PKA will increase in hypoglycemia. In turn, if blood glucose concentrations are high, the pancreas releases insulin which will trigger a series of reactions that activate a phosphatase which will dephosphorylate pyruvate kinase. That way, it will become activated and will allow the conversion of phosphoenolpyruvate to pyruvate and the continuation of the degradation of glucose.

[PDF p. 97]

Importance of the presence of phosphorylated compounds during glycolysis

The phosphate groups confer the compounds a negative charge, which prevents them from escaping the cell during glycolysis.

  • The energy released from the breakage of anhydride bonds in ATP during the preparatory phase is conserved in the form of phosphoesther bonds, which in the pay off phase will allow the synthesis of ATP.

The interaction between phosphate groups and the active site of the enzymes will decrease the activation energy and increase the specificity of the reaction.

Fates of pyruvate

  • The most part is transported to the mitochondria

and oxidized into CO2.

  • There are also some tissues without

mitochondria (erythrocytes) or some that work in hypoxia, like the skeletal muscle during sport. Therefore, the pyruvate follows a different pathway. Fermentation will be carried out, throughout lactic

fermentation.

Lactic acid fermentation reduces

pyruvate to lactate: NADH needs to be restored, so electrons will be

used to reduce pyruvate into lactate. By this reduction, NADH oxidation yields NAD+ and it’s restored. This way our cells don’t completely waste pyruvate. In skeletal muscle, lactate accumulates in the muscle and is then transported to the liver to be reconverted into glucose. It yields less energy than aerobic respiration, because NADH (each NADH will produce 2.5 ATPs) and 2 pyruvate molecules (which at the end of the cycle would have also produced ATP) are lost, but we can restore NAD+.

Other sugars that can undergo glycolysis

  • Mannose: it’s phosphorylated and then

there is an isomerization. It’s converted

into fructose-6-phosphate which enters the glycolytic pathway.

  • Fructose: it’s phosphorylated to fructose-6-phosphate by an hexokinase, and then enters the glycolytic pathway.

In the liver it’s metabolized in a different

way: the liver doesn’t have hexokinase, but fructokinase, which phosphorylates C1. When, further on, the aldolase breaks the molecule into 2, it results in dihydroxyacetone-phosphate and glyceraldehyde, which is then phosphorylated by a triose kinase and converted to G3P.

[PDF p. 98]

  • Galactose: mainly obtained from the breakdown of lactose. Galactosemias are complicated human diseases that are based on galactose accumulation due to galactose metabolism impairment. They come from the lack of enzymes such as galactokinase, UDP-glucose epimerase, UDP-glucose-galactose-1-phosphate-uridyltransferase,… It’s mainly carried out in the liver. Galactose is phosphorylated into galactose-1-phosphate, which then reacts with a molecule called UDP-glucose (catalyzed by UDP-glucose-galactose-1-phosphate-uridyltransferase, which

catalyzes the attack of the phosphate to the anhydride bonds) and binds to UDP (uracil nucleoside diphosphate). The end products are glucose-1-phosphate and UDP-galactose: there is a sugar exchange/transfer. The G1P can be further oxidized, but the

UDP-galactose is epimerized into glucose, into UDP-glucose. Now this UDP-glucose can further participate in the reaction that converts galactose into glucose-1-phosphate. If galactose accumulates in the liver, it affects its function and also has repercussions on the brain. If this happens, then the patient suffers galactosemia . Clinical symptoms: mental retardation, liver failure,… Metabolic tests are usually performed on babies to detect metabolic illnesses such as galactosemia.

Glycolysis and tumors

Glycolysis occurs at elevated rates in tumors. Initially, tumors are not vascularized (haven’t undergone angiogenesis), so they have to grow in anaerobic conditions. They are characterized by an extreme and big growth, so they need a lot of energy, and one of the quickest ways to do this is fermentation . It gives out less energy but by increasing the rate of fermentation it can be compensated. Knowing that they are highly dependent on glycolysis, we can synthesize drugs that interfere with glycolysis in tumors to treat them. The more glycolytic enzymes, the more opportunities to obtain energy from these pathways: hypoxia triggers the expression of HIF-1 (inducible hypoxia factor) which increases the transcription of glucose transporters (GLUT1 and GLUT3) that allow it to take up more glucose, and of many enzymes such as hexokinases (tumor has more ability to carry out glycolysis). Cancer patients usually present hypoglycemia due to all of this, even before being diagnosed with cancer. CT scans and PET scans allow to differentiate a normal glucose intake from an abnormal one. Glucose is marked and given to the patient, and then its uptake is observed on the

scan.

[PDF p. 99]

From the Molecule to the Cell I Alberto Pérez-Mediavilla

The Citric Acid Cycle (Krebs Cycle)

  • The oxidation of glucose to pyruvate is a necessary step on the way to energy production, but not a sufficient one

  • If you remember that process (glycolysis) you realize that the amount of ATP generated in it is 2 molecules.

  • However both, the pyruvate produced in the process and the NADH that is generated, have a high potential to generate ATP through aerobic oxidative

processes.

  • Now we will see how pyruvate, instead of being reduced to lactate (fermentation), undergoes a total oxidation to CO 2

  • This process, as a whole, leading to the complete oxidation of the organic fuels (glucose, fatty acids, amino acids) is known as cellular respiration

  • Cellular respiration takes place in three stages :

  • First stage : carbon skeletons of organic fuels are oxidized to a common compound called acetyl- coenzyme A. Depending on the starting molecule, the route used to convert the initial carbons into acetate units is different. As we have already seen, in the case of glucose, we will reach pyruvate through glycolysis, and as we will see later, pyruvate will be converted into acetate through an additional oxidative decarboxylation reaction (pyruvate dehydrogenase reaction)

  • Second stage : in this stage, the acetate (acetylCoA) is incorporated into the citric acid cycle (or Krebs cycle) where the two carbons are oxidized to CO 2 . At this stage of cellular respiration, most of the energy released during oxidations is temporarily conserved as NADH and FADH 2 .

  • Third stage : the reduced cofactors (NADH and FADH 2 ) generated both in the first and in the second phase, are oxidized and the released electrons will end up reducing O 2, previously passing through a chain of electron-transport molecules. . In this process, known as oxidative phosphorylation, a large amount of energy is released that allows ATP to be synthesized

  • As seen in the diagram, the Krebs Cycle is the central axis of cellular metabolism

  • It is the gateway to the aerobic metabolism for those molecules that will allow to produce energy

  • It is also a pathway that provides precursors required for biosynthesis of other molecules, Aa, nucleotides, porphyrins, and even glucose (from oxaloacetate)

  • Itself, the Krebs cycle does not generate ATP; its function is to generate electrons in the form of NADH and FADH 2

1

[PDF p. 100]

From the Molecule to the Cell I Alberto Pérez-Mediavilla

  • These reduced cofactors are those that in stage three of cellular respiration drive the synthesis of ATP

Production of Acetyl-coenzyme A

  • As we have said, before entering the citric acid cycle, sugars and fatty acids must be converted to activated acetate units (acetyl-CoA.)

  • Fatty acids, as we will see in another moment, have their own oxidation pathway to acetyl-CoA which is called β-oxidation

  • Glucose, on the other hand, is initially oxidized to pyruvate (glycolysis) so that it is later oxidized and decarboxylated to acetate.

  • The reaction is catalyzed by a multi-enzyme complex called pyruvate dehydrogenase (PDH) that is located in the mitochondria.

  • The global reaction is an oxidative decarboxylation in which pyruvate loses its carboxyl group as CO2 and the two remaining carbons are transformed into an acetyl group attached to Coenzyme A (acetyl-CoA)

  • In the total computation of the process, a pyruvate molecule is decarboxylated and oxidized to acetate bound to CoA and a NAD + molecule is reduced to

NADH

  • Therefore, the energy that is released during the decarboxylation and oxidation of pyruvate is transformed into a thioester bond with CoA and into a reduced electronic transporter.

  • Later we will see how the subsequent oxidation of NADH is able to boost the synthesis of 2.5 ATP

The Pyruvate dehydrogenase complex (PDH)

  • Under the name pyruvate dehydrogenase, three different enzymes and five coenzymes are agglutinated : thiamine pyrophosphate (TPP), FAD, coenzyme A, lipoic acid and NAD [+]

  • It is made up of many copies of three

enzymes: E1: pyruvate dehydrogenase E2: dihydrolipoyl transacetylase E3: dihydrolipoyl dehydrogenase

  • TPP : is the coenzymatic form of vit B1. It participates in decarboxylation reactions. Its deficit causes a disease known as Beri-

Beri that manifests itself with nervous and

cardiac disorders. The absence of TPP prevents glucose from being oxidized, which has serious consequences on the nervous system Exacerbated thiamine deficiency causes Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome, characterized by memory loss, confusion, and paralysis. It is common among alcoholics due to poor intestinal absorption of thiamine

2

[PDF p. 101]

From the Molecule to the Cell I Alberto Pérez-Mediavilla

  • FAD (flavin and adenine dinucleotide) is an electronic transporter formed by the union of two nucleotides, one with adenine and the other with riboflavin. Riboflavin is vitamin B2

  • NAD [+] (nicotinamide and adenine dinucleotide) is an electronic carrier formed by the union of two nucleotides, one with nicotinamide (Vitamin B3) and the other with adenine. Vitamin B3 deficiency causes Pellagra characterized by rough, reddish skin (dermatitis), fatigue and encephalopathies

  • Lipoic acid, derived from octadecanoic acid. It is characterized by two sulfhydryl groups that serve to bind the acetate derived from the decarboxylation of pyruvate and to transport electrons.

  • The toxicity of As (arsenic) is due to the fact that it binds to the SH groups of lipoic acid, blocking PDH and other enzymes that contain this vitamin such as α-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase

  • Coenzyme A is a cofactor acting as a transporter and activator of the acetate derived from the decarboxylation of pyruvate. It is made up of three elements: β-mercaptoethylamine, pantothenic acid and 3’-phosphoadenosine 5’- diphosphate

[PDF p. 102]

From the Molecule to the Cell I Alberto Pérez-Mediavilla

  • The reaction occurs in five stages such that the intermediate substrates never leave contact with the PDH complex, this process is called substrate channeling

    • PDH regulation : there are two regulation mechanisms

      - **allosteric inhibition** by NADH and acetyl-CoA. They both

compete for binding sites on their enzymes and keep E2 in acetylated form, unable to accept the hydroxyethyl group of TPP.

- covalent modification by phosphorylation and dephosphorylation: dihydrolipoyl transacetylase has two enzymes linked:

           - **PDH kinase** : inactivates PDH by phosphorylation in Ser;

this kinase activity is stimulated by NADH and acetyl-CoA

         **PDH phosphatase** : reverses inhibition by

dephosphorylation

Reactions of the Citric Acid Cycle

  • The first difference that can be observed, after studying glycolysis, is that the latter does not regenerate glucose, is a linear sequence of reactions; on the other hand, Krebs cycle is a cyclic sequence of reactions: the first compound of the cycle, oxaloacetate, is regenerated at the end of the cycle

  • At each lap of the cycle there is an entry of one Ac-CoA and the release of 2 CO 2

  • Each turn uses an OA to condense with the Ac-CoA to form citrate.

  • In theory there is no loss of OA, however, some Krebs intermediates serve as biosynthetic precursors, so the OA could be depleted preventing the entry of new Ac-CoA molecules.

  • To avoid the loss of OA, cells have some reactions, called anaplerotic, to allow them to synthesize OA and prevent the cycle from stopping

  • We can say that Krebs cycle is not only a catabolic pathway but it is also an anabolic pathway. These types of pathways are called amphibolic .

  • All reactions take place in the mitochondria; In addition, the enzymes necessary for the final transfer of electrons to O2 coupled with the synthesis of ATP (oxidative phosphorylation) are also in the mitochondria.

5

[PDF p. 104]

From the Molecule to the Cell I Alberto Pérez-Mediavilla

The eight steps of Krebs cycle

1. Formation of citrate

  • The first reaction is the condensation of

Ac-CoA with OA to form citrate

  • It is catalyzed by citrate synthase. The methyl carbon of acetyl binds to the carbonyl of OA

  • During the reaction, an intermediate compound, citroyl-CoA, bound to the active site of the enzyme, is generated. Hydrolysis of the thioester makes the reaction highly exergonic.

  • In this reaction the CoA is recycled

2. Formation of isocitrate

  • Citrate is transformed to its isocitrate

isomer

  • Is a reversible isomerization reaction catalyzed by aconitase (aconitate hydratase)

  • The reaction consists in the addition

and elimination of a molecule of H2O

  • Aconitase has an Fe-S center that

serves to fix the substrate and to add

or eliminate water.

  • Although citrate is a symmetric molecule, aconitase acts only on the two carbons that come from OA.

  • It is inhibited by ATP and NADH so that after a copious meal, there is an accumulation of citrate that will leave the mitochondria and will be used to synthesize fatty acids and therefore fats in the form of TAG

  • Fluoroacetate is an aconitase inhibitor; it becomes fluoroacetyl-CoA which inhibits it.

3. Oxidation to isocitrate to α-ketoglutarate and CO 2 .

  • Isocitrate dehydrogenase catalyzes the oxidative decarboxylation of isocitrate giving rise to α-ketoglutarate and CO 2

  • There are two isozymes of isocitrate dehydrogenase : one of them is mitochondrial and uses NAD [+] as an oxidation-reduction cofactor, the other is both mitochondrial and cytosolic and uses NADP [+] as a cofactor.

6

[PDF p. 105]

From the Molecule to the Cell I Alberto Pérez-Mediavilla

  • The NADP [+] -dependent isozymes main mission is to generate NADPH necessary for biosynthetic reactions

4. Oxidation of α-ketoglutarate to succinyl-CoA and CO 2 .

  • Once again there is an oxidative decarboxylation catalyzed by the multienzymatic complex αketoglutarate dehydrogenase

  • The final electron acceptor is NAD [+]

  • In this reaction, as occurred in PDH, the released energy is conserved as NADH and as a thioester bond in succinyl-CoA

  • Equally to the pyruvate dehydrogenase, α-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase complex is made up of 3 enzymes and 5 coenzymes

  • The enzymes are:

o E1: α-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase o E2: dihydrolipoyl transuccinylase o E3: dihydrolipoyl dehydrogenase

  • The coenzymes are: TPP, lipoic acid, CoA, FAD and NAD [+]

  • The reaction is identical to that of PDH

5. Conversión del succinil-CoA en succinato

  • In the next reaction, the hydrolysis of succinyl-CoA is coupled to the phosphorylation of GDP.

    • The energy released at the hydrolysis of the thioester is used to promote the synthesis of a phosphoanhydride bond (GDP+Pi) and the formation of succinate and GTP

  • The enzyme that catalyzes this reaction is succinyl-CoA synthetase

  • The reaction takes place in 3 stages:

  1. Succinyl-CoA reacts with the Pi and forms succinyl-P and releasing CoA

  2. the P group of succinyl P is transferred to an His residue of the enzyme, releasing succinate

  3. the P group of His is transferred to GDP, forming GTP

    • This type of mechanism in which ATP (or GTP) is synthesized is known as substrate-level phosphorylations

    • The GTP produced in this reaction is easily converted to ATP through the action of nucleoside diphosphate kinase

GTP + ADP ↔ GDP + ATP

7

[PDF p. 106]

From the Molecule to the Cell I Alberto Pérez-Mediavilla

6. Oxiation of succinate to fumarate

  • The succinate formed from succinylCoA is oxidized fo fumarate by the succinate dehydrogenase

  • This enzyme is a flavoprotein (because contains FAD as cofactor); it is strongly attached to the inner mitochondrial membrane .

  • The electrons released during the oxidation of succinate reduce FAD to FADH 2 and subsequently pass to the mitochondrial electron transport chain where they will drive the synthesis of 1.5 ATP molecules

  • This reaction is inhibited by malonate, a structural analog of succinate. The addition of malonate to the mitochondria blocks the Krebs cycle.

7. Hydration of fumarate a malate

  • The reversible hydaration of fumarate to L-malate is catalyzed by fumarase.

  • Fumarase is an stereospecific enzyme and only can use the trans isomer of malate

8. Oxidation of malate to oxaloacetate

  • The last reaction of Krebs cycle consists on the oxidation of malate to oxaloacetate, catalyzed by malate dehydrogenase .

  • Although the equilibrium of this reaction lies very far to the left, under physiological conditions, the continuous consumption of OA by citrate synthase makes the OA concentration low, so the reaction is shifted to the right..

8

[PDF p. 107]

From the Molecule to the Cell I Alberto Pérez-Mediavilla

Energy production

  • Krebs cycle reactions lead to:

  1. oxidation of an acetate group (acetyl-coA) to 2CO 2

  2. 3 NAD [+] are reduced to 3NADH

  3. 1 FAD is reduced to 1FADH 2

  4. 1 ADP is phosphorylated to 1ATP (although in the cycle it is GTP, but we can assimilate it to ATP)

    • Each molecule of NADH will lead the production of 2.5 ATP (oxidative phosphorylation)

    • The molecule of FADH 2 will lead 1.5 ATP (oxidative phosphorylation)

    • Therefore, each turn of the Krebs cycle generates 10 ATP

    • If we consider the energy generated in the complete oxidation of a glucose molecule as a whole the balance will be:

Glucose →2 pyruvate + 2 ATP +2NADH (glycolisis) 2 pyruvate → 2 Ac-CoA + 2 CO 2 +2NADH (Piruvate dehydrogenase complex) 2 Ac-CoA → 4 CO 2 + 20 ATP (Krebs cycle)

  • Making the corresponding conversions of NADH and FADH 2 to ATP (oxidative phosphorylation), we conclude that up to 32 ATP are obtained

Regulation of the Krebs cycle

  • The flow of carbon atoms from pyruvate to the Krebs cycle is subject to regulation at two levels :

- the conversion of pyruvate to acetyl- CoA

- the entry of acetyl-CoA into the cycle

  • In other words, the enzymes that are subject to regulation are fundamentally pyruvate dehydrogenase and citrate synthase.

  • It is also regulated at the level of isocitrate dehydrogenase and α-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase.

  • We have previously seen the regulation of pyruvate dehydrogenase

  • Regarding the reactions of Krebs cycle, there are three factors that govern the flow rate through the cycle:

    • the availability of substrates

    • the inhibition by accumulated products

9

[PDF p. 108]

From the Molecule to the Cell I Alberto Pérez-Mediavilla

 - **the allosteric back-inhibition** of the enzymes that catalyze early

stages of the cycle.

- In general, the overall activity of the cycle depends on the availability of

NAD [+] for the oxidative reactions.

- Actually the availability of NAD [+] depends on the consumption of NADH

by the respiratory chain, which in turn, depends on the rate of consumption of ATP by cells

 - **The accumulation of NADH**, which occurs when ATP consumption is

low, inhibits citrate synthase, α-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase . Under these circumstances, the malate dehydrogenase reaction is also reversed, which causes a decrease in the availability of oxaloacetate, thereby also slowing down the citrate synthase reaction.

 - **The accumulation of**

products (citrate, succinyl CoA and ATP) inhibits the three limiting stages of the pathway: citrate synthase, isocitrate dehydrogenase and α- ketoglutarate dehydrogenase)

 - **In muscle, Ca** **[++]** **,** which acts as a signal for contraction, **activates**

isocitrate dehydrogenase and α-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase, and, as we have seen previously, pyruvate dehydrogenase.

- Citrate acts as an accommodator of the flow of metabolites between

glycolysis and CAC since it inhibits phosphofructokinase I

Anaplerotic reaction to replenish Krebs cycle intermediates

  • As we have already said, CAC provides precursors for many biosynthetic pathways.

  • α-ketoglutarate and OA can serve as precursors of Asp and Glu, and through these compounds as nucleotide precursors.

  • OA is a glucose precursor

  • Succinyl-CoA is an intermediate in the synthesis of porphyrins

  • To avoid a decrease in the concentration of OA as a consequence of the exit of intermediates from the cycle, there are some reactions that allow synthesizing OA or other intermediates; these reactions are called anaplerotic

  • The most important anaplerotic reactions, in liver and kidney, is the reversible carboxylation of pyruvate to form OA, catalyzed by pyruvate carboxylase (liver and kidney)

Pyruvate + HCO 3

- + ATP ↔ OA + ADP + Pi

  • This reaction consumes energy and requires the presence of biotin as a cofactor.

  • Biotin is covalently linked to pyruvate carboxylase.

10

[PDF p. 109]

From the Molecule to the Cell I Alberto Pérez-Mediavilla

  • Another element necessary in the reaction is acetyl-CoA, which acts as an allosteric activator.

  • The accumulation of acetyl-CoA is fundamentally the consequence of a decrease in the concentration of OA.

  • In heart and skeletal muscle there is another reaction in which Phosphoenolpyruvate (PEP) is converted into OA by phosphoenol pyruvate carboxykinase

PEP + CO 2 + GDP ↔ OA + GTP

  • There is a third reaction consisting of the carboxylation of pyruvate to malate, catalyzed by the malic enzyme

Pyruvate + HCO 3

- + NAD(P)H ↔ Malate + NAD(P) +

[PDF p. 110]

Lecture 5.6. Gluconeogenesis and glycogen metabolism

GLYCOGEN

Glycogen is the storage form of glucose in animals . Glycogen is composed of glucose units linked by α1→4 glycosidic bonds, with α1→6 branches . It’s present in the cytosol in the form of granules. The process of glycogen synthesis is especially important in the liver and skeletal muscle. Glycogen is used in the liver as g lucose stores convertible to blood glucose, whereas in the muscle it is used as a source of glucose for energy production. As with most of the biosynthesis and degradation pathways, glycogen synthesis and glycogen degradation follow distinct metabolic pathways, which allows independent control of each route.

Glycogen breakdown / Glycogenolysis

In the first step, glucose residues are removed at the non-reducing ends of the glycogen molecule in a process known as phosphorolysis . Glucose molecules are released as glucose 1-phosphate. This reaction is catalyzed by glycogen phosphorylase. (This enzyme needs pyridoxal phosphate “PLP” (which comes from pyridoxine or vitamin B6) as a cofactor. Glycogen debranching enzyme catalyzes the hydrolysis of α1→6 bonds.) The fact that this reaction is a phosphorolysis is beneficial, since it is not necessary to phosphorylate the released glucose molecule (if it were a hydrolysis, glucose would then need to be phosphorylated, spending one ATP molecule).

Afterwards, glucose 1-phosphate is isomerized to glucose 6-phosphate in a reaction catalyzed by phosphoglucomutase .

- In muscle cells, glucose 6-phosphate is degraded to obtain energy.

- In liver cells, glucose 6-phosphate enters the endoplasmic reticulum where glucose

6-phosphatase catalyzes its hydrolysis to glucose. Glucose is then transferred to the cytosol through a specific membrane transporter, and leaves the hepatocyte via the GLUT2 transporter. Free glucose can go through the blood to other organs, such as the brain or muscle. Therefore, the liver is responsible for maintaining a constant level of glucose in the

blood.

[PDF p. 111]

Glycogen synthesis

The synthesis of glycogen is catalyzed by glycogen synthase, which requires the use of the nucleotide sugar UDP-glucose : the enzyme catalyzes the transfer of the glucose residue from UDP-glucose to the non-reducing end of the glycogen molecule through the formation of a new α1→4 glycosidic bond. The enzyme requires as a primer a chain of

at least eight glucose residues which are generated by the catalytic activity of another enzyme known as glycogenin, which acts as an initiator in the synthesis of glycogen. Glycogen branching enzyme catalyzes the introduction of branches in glycogen. The energy for the formation of the α1→4 glycosidic bond is provided by the hydrolysis of the O-glycosidic bond in the nucleotide sugar.

Coordinated regulation of glycogen synthesis and degradation

Glycogen breakdown and synthesis are reciprocally regulated. This prevents glycogen from being synthesized at the same time that it is being broken down. It’s coordinated through the regulation of the enzyme activities of glycogen phosphorylase and glycogen synthase. Both enzymes are dimers that can be phosphorylated at specific tyrosine residues. Glycogen phosphorylase is activated by phosphorylation, whereas glycogen synthase is inhibited by phosphorylation. In that way, if the cell regulates the phosphorylation of both enzymes using the same mechanisms, when glycogen breakdown is activated, glycogen synthesis is inhibited, and viceversa.

Hormonal regulation of glycogen metabolism

The main hormones involved in the regulation of glycogen metabolism are insulin, epinephrine and glucagon. These hormones are released mostly in response to circulating glucose levels and regulate the activity of glycogen phosphorylase and glycogen synthase . The body maintains fasting blood glucose levels at approximately 80 mg/dL.

                    - **Insulin** is a peptide hormone **produced by β cells in**

the Langerhans islets of the pancreas . Insulin is released in response to high levels of glucose in the blood. The role of this hormone is to reduce glucose levels. Insulin induces the dephosphorylation of glycogen phosphorylase and glycogen synthase . Therefore, insulin inhibits glycogen breakdown and stimulates glycogen synthesis.

[PDF p. 112]

  • Epinephrine (adrenaline) is released in response to stress or low levels of glucose in the blood. The role of the hormone is to prepare the body for a fight-or-flight response to a perceived harmful event. A part of this preparation is to increase blood glucose levels to make it available for muscle cells. Epinephrine induces the phosphorylation of glycogen phosphorylase and glycogen synthase. Therefore, epinephrine stimulates glycogen breakdown and inhibits glycogen synthesis.

    Glucagon is a peptide hormone released by α cells in the Langerhans islets of the pancreas. Glucagon has the opposite effects of insulin. It is secreted in response to low levels of glucose in the blood. Glucagon induces the phosphorylation of glycogen phosphorylase and glycogen synthase. Therefore, glucagon stimulates glycogen breakdown and inhibits glycogen synthesis.

While both epinephrine and glucagon have similar effects, epinephrine acts on skeletal muscle and the liver, while glucagon acts only on the liver. Insulin acts on both

tissues.

Glycogen storage diseases

A number of genetic diseases are known in which genes encoding enzymes involved in glycogen metabolism are mutated. These diseases are known as glycogen storage diseases or glycogenosis . Clinical symptoms range from relatively mild to fatal. Most of these diseases affect the storage of glycogen in organs where glycogen accumulates: liver, muscle and, to a lesser extent, kidney .

One of the first glycogen storage diseases to be described was von Gierke’s disease (type Ia). This disease is associated with mutations in glucose 6-phosphatase and it is one of the most serious, since the liver cannot release

glucose into the blood.

  • Glycogen storage disease type 0 is associated with mutations in glycogen synthase, which impairs liver glycogen synthesis.

In these two diseases, patients are prone to low blood glucose (hypoglycemia).

  • In Pompe disease (glycogen storage disease type II) there is an accumulation of glycogen in the lysosomes due to defects in lysosomal α1→4 glucosidase, affecting multiple tissues, but mainly skeletal and cardiac muscle.
  • In Ardle’s disease (glycogen storage disease type V) there is a deficiency of muscle phosphorylase. Cramps and muscle weakness occur mostly after exercise, but

the liver is not affected.

  • Finally, Lafora disease, a progressive epilepsy, is caused by mutations in genes that regulate the production of glycogen. The disease is characterized by the formation of Lafora bodies in neurons and other tissues (Lafora bodies contain large amounts of poorly branched glycogen).

[PDF p. 113]

GLUCONEOGENESIS

Gluconeogenesis is the synthesis of glucose from noncarbohydrate precursors (lactate, glycerol, amino acids,..)

Gluconeogenesis is an essential process needed to maintain the necessary amounts of glucose in our body. Many tissues (such as the brain, adrenal medulla, erythrocytes, testes, or embryonic tissues) require glucose as the sole source of fuel, and most tissues also require glucose for other functions, such as synthesis of nucleotides or the carbohydrate portion of glycoproteins and glycolipids. After a meal containing carbohydrates, blood glucose levels rise. Some of the glucose from the diet is stored in the liver as glycogen. After 2 or 3 hours of fasting, glycogen begins to be degraded by the process of glycogenolysis, and glucose is released into the blood. As glycogen stores decrease, adipose triacylglycerols are also degraded, providing fatty acids as an alternative fuel and glycerol for the synthesis of glucose by gluconeogenesis. Amino acids are also released from the muscle to serve as gluconeogenic precursors. After approximately 30 hours of fasting, liver glycogen stores are mostly depleted and gluconeogenesis becomes the only source of blood glucose.

In mammals, the main organ where gluconeogenesis takes place is the liver (90% of its total production). It also occurs in the renal cortex, in the cells of the renal tubule, and to a lesser extent in the intestinal epithelium. Gluconeogenesis in the liver is responsible for maintaining blood glucose levels, so the brain and muscle are able to obtain enough glucose

to meet their metabolic demands.

Gluconeogenesis shares with glycolysis the initial and final components: glucose and pyruvate. However, gluconeogenesis is not the reverse pathway to glycolysis. The reason is that the formation of pyruvate from glucose is a highly exergonic process. Therefore, the reverse process is not possible and a different thermodynamically favorable route is required. Seven of the ten reactions are the same in glycolysis and gluconeogenesis, whereas three steps are different.

The differential steps between glycolysis and gluconeogenesis are:

Synthesis of phosphoenolpyruvate from pyruvate

Formation of fructose 6-phosphate from fructose 1,6-bisphosphate

Conversion of glucose 6-phosphate into glucose

[PDF p. 114]

Gluconeogenesis pathway

- Synthesis of phosphoenolpyruvate from pyruvate:

It takes place through reactions in the mitochondria and the cytosol and requires two energy-consuming steps.

Pyruvate can come from the deamination of

alanine or lactate.

                 - Alanine transamination takes place in the

mitochondria.

                 - The conversion of lactate to pyruvate occurs in the

cytosol. In this case, the pyruvate must be transported into the mitochondria.

In the mitochondria, pyruvate is transformed

into oxaloacetate:

Pyruvate carboxylase, the enzyme that catalyzes the process, contains biotin (vitamin H or B7), a prosthetic group covalently attached to the enzyme. This coenzyme is key in many carboxylation reactions. Pyruvate carboxylase is an allosteric enzyme that acts as the first regulator of the gluconeogenic pathway.

Acetyl-CoA is an allosteric activator of pyruvate carboxylase, since high levels of acetyl-CoA indicates the need for oxaloacetate. If there is enough ATP in the cell, oxaloacetate will be consumed in gluconeogenesis. If there is not enough ATP, oxaloacetate will be used for the entry of an acetyl-CoA into the Krebs cycle ( anaplerotic role of pyruvate carboxylase ).

If gluconeogenesis continues, oxaloacetate must be transported to the cytosol, where the rest of the reactions will take place. Oxaloacetate cannot pass the inner

mitochondrial membrane because it does

not have a specific transporter for it. Therefore, oxaloacetate is transformed into malate through the action of mitochondrial malate dehydrogenase. Malate leaves the mitochondria using the malate/α-ketoglutarate transporter. In the cytosol, malate is reoxidized to oxaloacetate in a reaction catalyzed by cytosolic malate dehydrogenase. Cytosolic malate dehydrogenase and mitochondrial malate dehydrogenases are examples of isozymes. They are different enzymes that catalyze the same chemical reaction. Finally, oxaloacetate is decarboxylated and phosphorylated to produce phosphoenolpyruvate in a reaction catalyzed by phosphoenolpyruvate carboxykinase. The CO2 incorporated into pyruvate by pyruvate carboxylase is the same that is now lost.

[PDF p. 115]

This carboxylation-decarboxylation sequence represents a way to “activate” pyruvate so that decarboxylation of oxaloacetate allows the synthesis of phosphoenolpyruvate.

In summary, the steps by which pyruvate is converted into phosphoenolpyruvate are:

  1. Transport of pyruvate from the cytosol to the mitochondria

  2. Conversion of pyruvate to oxaloacetate

  3. Reduction of oxaloacetate to malate

  4. Transport of malate from the mitochondria to the cytosol

  5. Oxidation of malate to oxaloacetate

  6. Conversion of oxaloacetate to phosphoenolpyruvate

The overall balance of the conversion of pyruvate to phosphoenolpyruvate is:

Pyruvate + ATP + GTP + H2O → phosphoenolpyruvate + ADP + GDP + Pi + 2H+ The consumption of two high-energy phosphate groups allows the process to be thermodynamically favorable. Another goal that is achieved in this process is the net transport of NADH from the mitochondria to the cytosol . This is very important, as NADH is required for gluconeogenesis in the step catalyzed by glyceraldehyde 3-phosphate dehydrogenase.

- Synthesis of fructose 1,6-bisphosphate from phosphoenolpyruvate:

The next six reactions in which phosphoenolpyruvate is transformed into fructose 1,6-bisphosphate are the same reversible reactions that are used in glycolysis:

  1. Phosphoenolpyruvate + H2O ⇌ 2-phosphoglycerate (catalyzed by enolase )

  2. 2-Phosphoglycerate ⇌ 3-phosphoglycerate (catalyzed by phosphoglycerate mutase )

  3. 3-Phosphoglycerate + ATP ⇌ 1,3-bisphosphoglycerate + ADP (catalyzed by phosphoglycerate kinase )

  4. 1,3-Bisphosphoglycerate + NADH + H+ ⇌ glyceraldehyde 3-phosphate + NAD+ + Pi (catalyzed by GAPDH )

  5. Glyceraldehyde 3-phosphate ⇌ dihydroxyacetone phosphate (catalyzed by triose phosphate isomerase)

  6. Glyceraldehyde 3-phosphate + dihydroxyacetone ⇌ fructose 1,6-bisphosphate (catalyzed by aldolase )

(Note that all the reactions in gluconeogenesis are to be performed twice up to the reaction catalyzed by GAPDH because two three-carbon precursors are required to make a molecule of glucose)

[PDF p. 116]

- Synthesis of fructose 6-phosphate from fructose 1,6-bisphosphate

This reaction is the second by-pass of the irreversible reaction catalyzed in glycolysis. In gluconeogenesis, fructose 1,6-bisphosphatase-1 (FBPase1) catalyzes the hydrolysis of fructose 1,6-bisphosphate, where inorganic phosphate is released.

- Synthesis of glucose 6-phosphate from fructose 6-phosphate

Fructose 6-phosphate ⇌ glucose 6-phosphate

This isomerization reaction, catalyzed by phosphohexose isomerase, is reversible, and, therefore, it is shared with glycolysis.

- Synthesis of free glucose from glucose 6-phosphate

In the third by-pass step, Pi is hydrolyzed from glucose 6-phosphate in a reaction catalyzed by glucose 6-phosphatase. This enzyme is found on the inside of the endoplasmic reticulum membrane of hepatocytes, but it is not found in the brain or muscle. As a consequence, glucose can be dephosphorylated and released to the bloodstream only by hepatocytes. Glucose 6-phosphate enters the endoplasmic reticulum through a specific translocase and its hydrolysis gives rise to free glucose, which is then transported to the cytosol through another specific membrane transporter, and leaves the hepatocyte via the GLUT2 transporter . Free glucose can go through the blood to other organs, such as the brain or

muscle.

Overall balance of gluconeogenesis

2 Pyruvate + 4 ATP + 2 GTP + 2 NADH + 2 H+ + 4 H2O → glucose + 4 ADP + 2 GDP + 6 Pi

  • 2 NAD+

Six high-energy phosphate bonds are used in the synthesis of glucose from pyruvate. This allows gluconeogenesis to be a thermodynamically favorable pathway.

Gluconeogenic precursors

The three major carbon sources for hepatic gluconeogenesis are lactate, glycerol and amino acids. Kidneys predominantly use amino acids. Animals cannot produce glucose from fatty acids.

- Lactate is produced from pyruvate by erythrocytes and anaerobic exercising muscles.

  • Glycerol is released from adipose stores of triacylglycerols.
  • Amino acids come mainly from muscle, where they are obtained by protein degradation. All amino acids (except for leucine and lysine) are glucogenic because they can be converted to pyruvate or citric acid cycle intermediates. Alanine is the most abundant amino acid that arrives to the liver, since it is produced in the muscle from other amino acids.

[PDF p. 117]

Regulation of gluconeogenesis

Two steps are the main regulatory points in gluconeogenesis:

- Pyruvate to phosphoenolpyruvate (oxaloacetate)

- Fructose 1,6-bisphosphate to fructose 6-phosphate

1. Pyruvate to oxaloacetate/phosphoenolpyruvate Pyruvate is converted to phosphoenolpyruvate when pyruvate carboxylase is allosterically activated by high levels of acetyl-CoA (produced, for example, by oxidation of fatty acids). At the same time, ADP, a surrogate of low cell energy, acts as a negative regulator of phosphoenolpyruvate carboxykinase. Therefore, in a situation in which there is enough energy (high levels of acetyl-CoA and low levels of ADP) the conversion of pyruvate to oxaloacetate is induced.

At the same time, pyruvate kinase, the corresponding glycolytic enzyme, is inhibited by acetyl-CoA and by its phosphorylation induced by glucagon. The activity of pyruvate dehydrogenase, the enzyme responsible for the conversion of pyruvate into acetyl-CoA is also strongly inhibited by acetyl-CoA.

In summary, high levels of acetyl-CoA inhibit glycolysis and promote gluconeogenesis.

2. Fructose 1,6-bisphosphate to fructose 6-phosphate A futile cycle in which both phosphofructokinase-1 (PFK1) and fructose 1,6-bisphosphatase (FBPase1) act simultaneously is prevented by allosteric regulators with opposite effects in these two enzymes. The key regulator of both processes is fructose 2,6-bisphosphate. This regulator strongly induces PFK-1 and inhibits FBPase1. The hormone glucagon induces the degradation of fructose 2,6-bisphosphate. Therefore, glucagon induces gluconeogenesis and inhibits glycolysis. Another allosteric regulator at this level is AMP, which activates PFK-1 and inhibits FBPase1, blocking gluconeogenesis when energy levels are low.

[PDF p. 118]

Lecture 5.7. Oxidative phosphorylation and ATP synthesis

Oxidative phosphorylation is the process that allows the cells to obtain ATP by phosphorylating ADP through the use of the energy released by the transport of electrons. During the traffic of electrons through the respiratory chain, the electron gradient potential will be turned into a proton gradient . The energy coming from those oxido-reductions will be used to pump protons from the matrix into the intermembrane space. Then the ATP synthase will use this gradient to produce

ATP.

The inner mitochondrial membrane is highly impermeable for H+ . The protons can’t go into the intermembrane space if not through specific channels.

Structure of the mitochondria

Outer membrane: permeable to small molecules and ions

Mitochondrial matrix: contains all the necessary enzymes for Krebs cycle, PDH complex, beta-oxidation and the oxidation of amino acids.

Intermembrane space

Inner mitochondrial membrane: contains the complex that forms electron-transport chain and ATPsynthase. It’s highly impermeable, even to H+, which allows the establishment of a proton gradient.

Electron funnelling to the respiratory chain

Carried out by electron carriers such as NADH and FADH2, which accept electrons from dehydrogenases and funnel them to the electron transport chain.

Respiratory electron carriers

The mitochondrial respiratory chain is made up of electronic transporters that act sequentially. Most of them contain a series of electron carrier prosthetic groups that allow the transport of e- (accept and give up electrons). In addition to NADH and Flavoproteins there arre 3 types of electron transport molecules:

  • Coenzyme Q or Ubiquinone: This lipidic molecule can transport 2 electrons (partially reduced when 1 e- is added). It’s liposoluble, found inside the membrane and hydrophobic: it can receive electrons (2) from one complex and transfer to

another one. It’s an intermediate/connector.

  • Iron-sulfur clusters: They are one electron carriers.

Cytochromes: They are proteins with heme prosthetic groups . In mitochondria, there are 3 types: A, B and C, and are strongly bound to proteins. Cytochrome C is

[PDF p. 119]

an exception, since it isn’t located in a specific complex, but bound to a soluble protein which allows it to transfer e- from one complex to another. It’s an intermediate/connector. (2 connectors: ubiquinone and cytochrome C).

How are these transporters arranged?

Because we can isolate them, we know that there are 5 complexes at the inner membrane that belong to the respiratory chain (complex I, II, III, IV and lastly the ATPsynthase).

1. COMPLEX I

It’s the NADH ubiquinone oxidoreductase. As cofactors for electronic transport, it contains FMN and Fe-S centres. It receives electrons from NADH and transports a pair of e- to coenzyme Q, in this way reducing it. During this transport and due to the energy release, there is a proton pump : 4 H+ are transferred to the intermembrane space. It’s at the same time an electron carrier and a proton pump. These two events go hand by hand. In other words: it’s a coupling centre. Subsequently, the reduced ubiquinone transfers the electrons to complex III.

2. COMPLEX II

It’s the succinate dehydrogenase system. Electrons from succinate enter through complex II. It’s the only membrane-bound enzyme of the Krebs cycle. FADH2 transfers the electrons through complex II and at the end reduces coenzyme Q . Complex II couples the oxidation of succinate with the reduction of ubiquinone/coenzyme Q, and finally, Coenzyme Q transfers the electrons to complex III. There is no proton pump . It’s not a coupling centre.

Therefore, electrons stripped from organic molecules enter the respiratory chain through Complex I and Complex II.

There are two additional gates, alternative electron incorporations:

                       **Glycerol-3-phosphate dehydrogenase**

(G3PDH) shuttle, which oxidizes glycerol and incorporates electrons by reducing coenzyme Q.

                       - The **electron transport flavoprotein (ETFP)**,

which receives the electrons from the FADH2 released

at the first oxidation of fatty acids ( beta oxidation ) which go and reduce coenzyme Q.

[PDF p. 120]

3. COMPLEX III

Also known as ubiquinone-cytochrome C oxidoreductase. It’s a coupling gate. During the pass of electrons from ubiquinone to cytochrome C, there is a proton pump . It generates part of the gradient. These electrons reduce cytochrome C, and will then be tansfered to complex IV.

4. COMPLEX IV

Complex IV or Cytochrome oxidase transports electrons from Cytochrome C to O2. Cytochrome C is oxidized, e- pass through complex IV and there is a proton pump (2 H+).

Oxidative phosphorylation is mainly regulated by the availability of ADP that can be phosphorylated to ATP.

Oxidative stress

During electron transport, some electrons can leak from the process and partially reduce oxygen in membrane lipids, proteins,… generating reactive oxygen species (ROS). There are systems that protect against this. The most important system is the one catalyzed by glutathione reductase. Glutathione contains SH groups that can reduce H2O2 (an oxidizing agent) to water, and so glutathione molecules become oxidized by forming disulfide bridges . In this way, they avoid the oxidation

of other structures.

This defense mechanism is highly dependent on the availability of NADPH to reduce glutathione dimers (oxidized in its dimeric form, reduced in the monomeric form) so they can be reused in reducing oxidizing agents

such as H2O2.

During aging, there is more of this stress, because as we get older we express less of these protective enzymes. Antioxidants also help to avoid this oxidative stress.

- Defense mechanisms against ROS during hypoxia

During hypoxia there is an imbalance between the entry of electrons to the respiratory chain and the transfer of electrons to O2 (absent in these conditions). This leads to an increase in the formation of ROS.
