The primase arrives and binds to the helicase, synthesizing the new primer, while the polymerase ends the synthesis of the previous O. fragment. In the meantime, a new Beta-clamp is being added at the point where the new primer has been synthesized. The new clamp moves along the strand and is loaded onto the catalytic core, while the other is discarded, and the synthesis of the new fragment can begin.

Once an Okazaki fragment has been completed, its RNA primer is removed and replaced by DNA. This process is carried out by DNA polymerase I through its exonuclease and polymerase activity, and the remaining nick is sealed by DNA ligase .

3. Termination

[PDF p. 208]

Proteins release the replication complex when the two replication forks of the circular E.

coli chromosome encounter each other.

The two replication forks meet at a terminus region containing copies of a 20 bp sequence known as Ter (for “terminus”). Ter sequences function as binding sites for a protein called Tus (Terminus utilization substance).

These sequences are present in case one replication fork for some reason carries out replication faster than the other. Even though it gets to the end first, it will remain there and not continue replicating due to this 20 bp terminus (Ter).

MECHANISM OF DNA REPLICATION in EUKARYOTES

The essential characteristics of DNA replication are conserved between prokaryotes and eukaryotes. But during replication, eukaryotes present many more replication origins. These are known as “origin of replication complexes” (ORC). Replication also occurs bidirectionally from the multiple ORC, but it’s carried out by eukaryotic DNA polymerases:

Some catalyze DNA synthesis while others are involved in repair.

Polymerase alpha (α) lacks 3’ to 5’ exonuclease activity (proofreading), but it synthesizes the RNA primers.

  • Polymerase delta (δ) is the equivalent to DNA pol III in prokaryotes. Its PCNA subunit is the equivalent to the beta clamp. It mainly performs elongation . This protein can be analyzed in tissues by immunohistochemistry to detect tumors, as it will appear as a marker in highly proliferating cells.

Polymerase epsilon (ϵ) is the other subunit involved in DNA synthesis and elongation, and is thought to play a role in eliminating the primers from Okazaki fragments.

- Polymerase gamma (γ) synthesizes mitochondrial DNA.

[PDF p. 209]

- Lecture 7.6. Protein coding and non coding genes

(video that summarizes the lecture: Javier Novo)

What is a gene?

Genes come in two classes: coding and non-coding genes.

“A gene is a sequence of DNA that is used to make a messenger” This definition was established because non-coding genes also make mRNA, but it’s not translated into proteins. That’s the difference between coding and non-coding genes. Enhancers don’t make messenger RNA, therefore they are part of DNA but not considered genes.

Transcription begins where the gene starts. It’s transcribed into a single stranded RNA molecule, made up of both exons and introns, called primary transcript. The primary transcript is processed through a mechanism called splicing: spliceosomes cut the primary transcript at points which are exactly the boundaries between exons and introns. The remaining exons bind together to create what we know as mRNA, which

contains a CAP at the 5’ end. Maturation of

the primary transcript gives mRNA.

1. PROTEIN CODING GENES

  • There are around 20,000 protein coding genes
  • Exons tend to be very short, whereas introns are very large but yet very very variable. There are genes with one exon only, others with thousands; on average, a human protein coding gene has 7-8 exons.
  • The average size of these exons is 145 nt .
  • The size of the average gene is 20-30 kilo bases.
  • Only 1.5 % of nucleotides in the genome are coding genes. The rest are destined to regulation and many other processes.
  • They have a high G-C content (gene dense), of about 53% - It has at least 4 alternative transcripts, generated by alternative splicing

They appear to have a high expression rate (generate many transcripts)

- Low cell specificity

One gene can make many different transcripts, depending on the exons included. The mechanism of splicing creates different alternatives of mRNA. This is known as alternative splicing. Each of the transcripts will code for slightly different proteins.

[PDF p. 210]

Brown boxes in the picture represent gene sequences before initiation and stop codons. Therefore, these sequences won’t transalte into proteins. They are untranslated regions (UTRs).

  • Initiation codon = AUG (encodes for methionine, proteins always begin with methionine). The previous part to AUG is not translated, and is known as 5’ UTR.

Stop codons = UAA, UAG, UGA, which indicate translation to stop. The region of RNA that comes after this codon isn’t translated, and is

known as 3’ UTR.

2. NON-CODING GENES

  • Around 15,000 genes for long and short non-coding RNAs.

IncRNA genes are shorter than protein-coding genes

  • IncRNA genes have fewer exons, around 2-4 exons.

They are between 10-15 Kilobases long.

  • In the average genome, an IncRNA has around 43% G-C content. They are less rich in G-C content than protein coding genes, and therefore, less gene dense.
  • We find 2 alternative transcripts per non-coding gene

They have a lower expression than protein coding genes (weak regulators)

They have high cell specificity (mostly transcribed in specific cells)

Expression of IncRNAs is very restricted. On average, protein-coding genes tend to be broadly expressed in all cells. But most non-protein coding genes are specific for each cell line, being expressed sometimes in only one cell line.

[PDF p. 211]

3. PSEUDOGENES

Pseudogenes are genes which are inactive and don’t work. There are two types:

- Non-processed, as a result of mutations in duplication.

  • Processed, which have the sequence

of what mRNA would look like

(therefore, made up by exons), and are usually silent because they lack the promoter. These sequences are found away from the promoter because they’ve been relocated through LINE retrotransposition.

HUMAN GENOME GENERAL STATISTICS

[PDF p. 212]

Lecture 7.7. Genetic variation

Mutations in the genome result in alternative forms of DNA known as DNA variants. If these variants in a certain locus are common in the population, the variation is described as a polymorphism. Polymorphic genes are genes that appear in the population in the form of many different alleles. Genomes can have two types of variation:

  • differ in single nucleotides

present larger structural variants.

These variants have profound implications for predisposition to disease.

SINGLE-NUCLEOTIDE POLYMORPHISM (SNP)

In the DNA of our cells, we may find polymorphisms between one individual and the next that consist in variations in one single nucleotide.

How often do we find these

polymorphisms? Are they

common?

SNPs (pronounced “snips”) are found every 500 letters on average. So every 500 letters, each individual will present a polymorphism. Therefore, when we compare two genomes, they will probably differ in 3 million single

nucleotides.

- DNA sequencing

  • Single peaks imply the person is homozygous for that base, in both parental chromosomes.
  • Smaller, double peaks imply there are two different nucleotides in this position in each chromosome (maternal and paternal).

The human genome project

Took 13 years and an estimated cost of 3 billion dollars to sequence the genome of a single individual. But technology has improved a lot, and now we can sequence one’s genome in a single day. This has allowed us to discover more SNPs. Genomes have constant sequences, but about 100 million letters are variants between the different humans on the planet.

[PDF p. 213]

Typical genome vs the reference genome

  • A typical genome, compared to the reference genome, can present 4 to 5 million

variants.

  • About 10,000 (not all nucleotides in DNA are genes) of these SNPs are potentially dangerous, as they change protein sequences.

Furthermore, in general, each of us carry 25-30 SNPs previously associated with disease: it can set a predisposition to suffer a disease or not, depending on other many genetic factors. By knowing the whole genome, we could predict the probability of predisposition.

Lastly, 500,000 overlap regulatory regions . Some promoters are very strong and make many mRNA copies; other promoters are very weak. Protein coding genes usually have stronger promoters than non-protein coding genes. These overlapping genes and SNPs can silence promoters and substantially affect gene regulation and expression.

Distribution of genetic variation

Anywhere in the world, around 4 to 5 million variants are detected when comparing 2 people from anywhere around the world. Anywhere except in Africa, where we find many

more SNPs.

There is more variation inside Africa than outside Africa. Comparing two people from a same African city, they will present more genetic variation than when comparing two genomes from two individuals from different parts of the world.

Why is this? African = “first humans”. From all the people in Africa, only a few of them made it to the rest of the planet and colonized it. Therefore, only a few SNPs were present in the populations that gave place to the many ethnicities around the world today.

[PDF p. 214]

STRUCTURAL VARIANTS and SEGMENTAL DUPLICATIONS

A lot of the genome is covered by segmental duplications. Some of them are polymorphic in the number of times they are repeated. In the population, there are chromosomes with one copy of segment X or many copies of it. If you have a different number of copies for one segment, you will therefore have repeated copies of many genes. These segmental duplications (or structural variants) play a big role in predisposition to disease. A total of 68,000 SV (structural variants) have been described in

humans.

Each of us carry 20 Megabases, and around 2,000 SVs.

[PDF p. 215]

Lecture 7.8. DNA damage

Cells are able to repair the damage that DNA suffers. DNA is constantly being attacked or destroyed by many mechanisms such as DNA replication (even with proofreading, it makes mistakes).

There are several ways in which cells repair DNA damage:

Mismatch repair (MMR), which mostly arise from errors during DNA replication

Base Excision repair (BER), which works by removing the base, not the whole

nucleotide

Lesions that distort the double helix are repaired by Nucleotide-Excision repair (NER)

Double strand breaks (DSBs), a type of damage created by x-rays or ionizing agents, are repaired by a process similar to that of recombination during meiosis.

What does the cell do?

When faced with this situation, the cell tries to repair the damage. Therefore, the cell cycle has to stop and activate cell cycle checkpoints. If the DNA is repaired, the cell continues it’s cycle. If the damage is unrepairable, the cell will commit suicicde and proceed to apoptosis . If these damaged cells don’t die through apoptosis, they will continue to divide and propagate mutations, giving place to tumors and cancer.

[PDF p. 216]

A) Repair of DNA damage or altered sequence on a SINGLE STRAND

1. Repair of mismatches through mismatch repair (MMR)

  • Occurs mainly during DNA replication through the insertion of wrong tautomeric

forms of the nucleobases

  • It can also occur through strand slippage during replication (specifically during the formation of Okazaki fragments). A bulging loop is formed due to the slippage, and at the end of replication there is a strand with a protuberating loop.

Mechanism to repair mismatches: Carried out in eukaryotes by a protein complex, a tetramer: a dimer made up of hMLH1 and hPMS2 can the bind, either to the hMSH2-hMSH6 dimer or to the hMSH2-hMSH3 dimer, forming the tetrameric complex that will repair the mismatch.

Mismatches involving abnormal nucleotides can occur by:

  • Hydrolytic reactions (depurination)

  • Deamination of non-methylated cytosines

  • UV light

  • Alkylating agents

- Alkylating agents Example: o6-methyl-guanine, which binds with thymine instead of cytosine. Alkylating agents

can be found in the diet or in toxic substances.

- Benzopyrene

Molecule that intercalates in the double strand of DNA and creates a distortion. It’s found in

tobacco.

- UV light The incidence of UV light generates pyrimidine dimers (covalent bonds between pyrimidines), which creates bulks and is bad for replication and transcription processes, as it

interferes with them.

[PDF p. 217]

2. Repair of abnormal bases and bulky adducts through BER and NER

  • Base Excision Repair or BER works by removing the incorrect base. Firstly, a DNA glycosylase recognizes the base that needs to be changed and cleaves the sugar-base bond to remove the base, leaving an abasic site. The remaining sugar-phosphate backbone is removed by an endonuclease and a phosphodiesterase, and the gap is filled by a DNA polymerase. Finally, a ligase binds the whole strand together.

  • Nucleotide Excision Repair or NER: allows the repair of bulky, helix-distorting DNA lesions. Proteins recognize the damage ( XPC, together with XPA ). They occupy the region of the lesion and recruit a complex that opens the double helix. RPA binds to one of the strands, and XPF and XPG make an incision and cleave the DNA, generating an oligonucleotide around 30 nucleotides long which contains the damaged site. The strand is removed and correctly filled in by DNA polymerases, using the opposite strand as a template. Finally, it’s all bound together by a ligase .

    • XERODERMA PIGMENTOSUM: Caused by a mutation in genes that codify for XP proteins. The NER process is interfered . If NER can’t work, mutations accumulate and give rise to this disease, Xeroderma Pigmentosum.

[PDF p. 218]

B) Repair of DNA lesions that affect BOTH STRANDS

Double strand breaks occur naturally in B and T lymphocytes for the rearrangement of DNA, to maximize immunoglobulin and T cell receptor diversity. This damage can also happen by accident, caused by external agents (ex: X Rays, which kills cells by making these breaks), endogenous causes,… It consists in the breakage of both strands. Cells respond to DSBs (double strand breaks) in different ways. If DNA repair isn’t successful, the cell undergoes apoptosis. Two repair pathways are mainly employed:

Non-homologous end joining: No template strand is needed in this repair mechanism, because the broken ends are fused together. It rejoins the broken strands without any type of homologous template. It just seals loose ends together again. KU80, KU70 and DNAPKcs, bring the strands together and seal the lession thanks to ligases .

Homologous recombination repair (HRR): this process requires a homologous intact DNA strand to serve as a template. This mechanism uses the same machinery that is used in meiosis for recombination : there are two strands that have to exchange DNA, and everything starts by making a break in one of them. One is used as a template to repair the other.

[PDF p. 219]

TBL 7.1. Apoptosis and senescence

In some occasions, cells need to die. Cell death can occur through:

  • Necrosis, whenever there is an acute injury or cell damage
  • Senescence (aging), when cells lose their ability to proliferate

Apoptosis, programmed cellular death which can occur, for example, when there is DNA damage, pathological infection,…

APOPTOSIS

There has to be a controlled ratio between cell proliferation and cellular death. Apoptosis consists in programmed cell death. It’s a process that’s highly regulated, and can be induced by:

- Genetic / biochemical changes

- Morphological changes

Apoptosis isn’t only important for removing damaged cells, but also for growth and neonatal development. For example, during Syndactyly, the programmed death of cells in the web spaces allows the formation of independent fingers; or in the brain during embryonic development, half of the neurons have to die through apoptosis so there can be plenty of nutrients for neurons and microglia.

Apoptosis can be activated through two pathways, which don’t only occur independently: the intrinsic and extrinsic pathways.

a) Intrinsic pathway

Cell stress, for example caused by DNA damage, induces specific cellular signals that activate p53. This transcription factor activates proteins

involved in mitochondrial membrane

destabilization (BAX and BAK) and the organelle releases Cytochrome C, which will activate the caspase

cascade.

[PDF p. 220]

b) Extrinsic pathway

An external ligand binds to the Death Receptor. This receptor contains a Death Domain (FADD) which is bound to Caspase 8. Upon activation, Caspase 8 will initiate the Caspase cascade, which ends with the activity of Caspase 3.

These two pathways are interconnected through the protein BID. This protein, when activated by Caspase 8, triggers the release of Cytochrome C from the mitochondria through proapoptotic proteins (BAX and BAK).

- BCL-2 family is a family of antiapoptotic proteins.

- BAX and BAK are pro-apoptotic and destabilize the

mitochondrial membrane.

- BH3-only, released in response to chemicals, radiation or growth factors withdrawal, inhibits anti-apoptotic proteins (and is therefore proapoptotic).

Under the microscope

Under electron microscope, apoptotic cells present a dark, electron dense nucleus due to the condensation of chromatin, often presenting “blebs”. Through immunohistochemistry, apoptotic cells can be seen more clearly as dark brown or black due to the compacted nucleus.

p53: the guardian of the genome

p53 is a transcription factor, activated by phosphorylation when DNA damage, hypoxia, activated oncogenes or telomeric erosion are detected. It acts by stopping the cell cycle and allowing the entry of DNA repair proteins, to try and repair the damage. If this isn’t achieved, it triggers apoptosis. This is why 50% of tumors harbor p53 mutations.

The main regulator of p53 is Mdm2. Mdm2 is a p53 inhibitor, which:

Inhibits p53 gene regulation activity

Exports p53 out of the nucleus

Induces p53 degradation

[PDF p. 221]

How do T-lymphocytes induce apoptosis?

A Cytotoxic T lymphocyte binds its T cell receptor to and infected cell by:

  • Binding to the antigen presented to the T-lymphocyte by the cell

  • Binding to the Major Histocompatibility Complex I (MHC-I)

It then induces apoptosis by activating the extrinsic and intrinsic pathways.

                        **Extrinsic pathway:** Once bound, the Cytotoxic

T-cell expresses the FAS ligand, which induces apoptosis by binding to receptors in other neighbouring cells.

Intrinsic pathway: the Cytotoxic T-cell releases perforin, which creates a perforin-channel in the infected cell. Subsequently, it releases granzyme, an enzyme which enters the infected cell through the perforin-channel and activates the Caspase cascade, thus inducing apoptosis.

SENESCENCE

Senescence occurs when a cell loses its proliferation capacity and is permanently in a G1 phase. It can be induced by:

- Oxidative stress and external damage

  • Genetic factors, such as telomere shortening: after each cell division, the end of chromosomes (telomeres) shortens a little bit every time. As telomeres shorten, the cell enters into telomere crisis after too many replications: this is a sign that the cell is very old and has possibly accumulated mutations, so it goes into senescence. In cancer cells and stem cells, telomerase is expressed, which allows them to elongate telomeres and evade senescence. This enzyme is also expressed in oocytes and spermatozoa, which are going to give rise to a new organism. This allows the baby to be born with the telomere counter at 0.

[PDF p. 222]

PROGERIA

Progeria, also known as Hutchinson-Gilford syndrome, is caused by a mutation in Lamin-A. It has quite a low incidence (less than 200 cases diagnosed), and conferes a life expectancy of 13-14 years.

Lamin-A is part of the nuclear lamina : it forms an intracellular-scaffolding for the nuclear envelope, and is in charge of organizing nucleosomes. In a normal LMNA gene, the sequence is transcribed and once the primary transcript is produced, the exons are removed and matured, forming mRNA. Exon 11 contains a sequence which encodes for the cleavage site. This mRNA is traduced into a protein (Lamin-A) which becomes farnesylated to allow it’s transport into the nucleus and consequent attachment to the inner part of the nuclear membrane. A fragment of the protein is removed by proteolysis at the cleavage site to eliminate the farnesyl group, which is necessary for the normal function of the protein. In patients with Progeria, a mutation in Exon 11 changes the way this exon is spliced. The mutation generates a new splicing site, producing the loss of 50 amino acids. Within these 50 amino acids, the cleavage site is lost, so the farnesyl group won’t be able to be removed, and the farnesylated protein will form aggregates and become harmful for the cell. Chromatin suffers conformational stress and DNA becomes damaged, which activates p53 and increases cellular senescence, thus accelerating aging. This is why patients, at very young ages, resemble a person older than 100 years old. Patients with this syndrome usually present tricuspid valve reflux. Lonafarnib can be used for the treatment of Progeria, as it’s a farnesyltransferase inhibitor. But this implies that LMNA isn’t correctly transferred into the nucleus, which can also have

side effects.

NORMAL LMNA GENE MUTATED LMNA GENE

[PDF p. 223]

TBL 7.2. Forensic DNA analysis

STRs (microsatellites) can be used to identify individuals. There are many different alleles in the population for short tandem repeats (polymorphic). Different people have different combinations of alleles; that’s why it’s a useful method to identify suspects. The more microsatellites you analyze, the better.

How to analyze microsatellites?

Modern DNA profiles analyze short tandem repeats (STRs), located in non-coding regions of chromosomes. Currently, the FBI analyzes 13 STRs, plus a sex marker. This way, they reduce the possibility of random matches.

STRs are highly polymorphic, having nine or more different alleles

that can be detected.

To detect if a person is homozygous or heterozygous for a certain STR, we use PCR technique, which amplifies specific regions of DNA. PCR primers are designed to bracket the STR. In this type of PCR, one primer of the pair is fluorescently labeled.

The same DNA region is amplified many times, although the different alleles amplify as different sizes. The amplified alleles fluoresce, as each has incorporated a fluorescent primer.

Out of the 14 STRs which are analyzed, 3 or 4 different polymorphisms are labeled with each of four fluorescent dyes. The amplified DNA fragments are loaded onto a gel, where the smaller pieces migrate faster than the larger ones.

Each fragment is detected when it passes by a laser when it’s near the bottom of the gel. The laser reads all four colors and displays an output of the PCR according to the sizes and fluorescent colors of the amplified species.

[PDF p. 224]

Each locus appears as a single peak (homozygous) or a pair of peaks (heterozygous).

A complete DNA profile of 13 STRs+sex marker comes with a random match probability (probability that a specific STR profile matches that of another individual in a population). There are ways to calculate these random match probabilities, by using the frequencies at which different STR alleles occur in a population.

- In homozygous individuals: (frequency of A )^2

- In heterozygous individuals: (frequency of A) x (frequency of B) x 2

[PDF p. 225]

Lecture 8.1 and 8.2. DNA transcription I and II

Transcription will give rise to different types of RNA.

Messenger RNA (mRNA)

  • Transfer RNA (tRNA)
  • Ribosomal RNA (rRNA), essential structural and functional components of

ribosomes.

Non-coding RNA (ncRNA)

The transcriptome in a tissue is the collection of all RNAs that are being produced in said

tissue cells.

Coding and non-coding RNAs in the human genome

Nowadays, we know that less than 3% of the genome are coding genes, which are transcribed into mRNA and then into proteins; 80% corresponds to non-coding genes, which are only transcribed into RNA but not translated to produce proteins. These non-coding RNAs correspond to tRNA, rRNA,… but also to Small ncRNA and Long ncRNA, which are still being investigated but have been found to play a key role in the control of gene expression (through regulating specific interactions with chromatin, degradation,…)

It’s also been observed that the

expression of these lncRNA and sncRNA is varied during disease. In relation to this, they can be targeted to affect their expression levels, so they are starting to be used as new pharmacological targets.

RNA SYNTHESIS

RNA synthesis is a polymerization reaction catalyzed by RNA polymerases , a set of enzymes which synthesize RNA from a DNA template. It’s a reaction in which nucleotides are incorporated to form a single stranded chain, a process during which a PPi will be released. Also it’s a directional reaction: DNA is read from 3’ to 5’ by RNA polymerase, and the RNA strand is synthesized in 5’ to 3’ direction.

From an energetic point of view, it’s a favourable reaction: it’s going to release energy from a breakage of an anhydride bond. Pyrophosphatases will degrade the pyrophosphate, driving the reaction in forward manner (shifts the equilibrium) to favour the continuation of the synthesis.

[PDF p. 226]

Catalytic mechanism of RNA polymerases

From the 3’ end of the DNA chain, RNA polymerase starts to synthesize a complementary

strand.

In the catalytic core of RNA polymerase, there are 2 Mg2+ ions:

One helps in the nucleophilic attack and the breakage of the phospho bond

The other helps in the removal of the PPi

A. TRANSCRIPTION IN PROKARYOTES

Prokaryotic RNA polymerase

  1. It identifies specific regions in the genome where transcription begins
  2. Unwinds the DNA double helix to expose the template ( helicase activity)
  3. Carries out the synthesis (phosphodiester bond) from 5’ end to 3’ end.
  4. They also identify termination sequences in the genome that mark the end of transcription.
  5. It interacts with activating and suppressing factors, which can modulate its activity.

Different subunits form the prokaryotic RNA polymerase.

  • Alpha subunits, which have regulatory roles and interact with regulatory proteins
  • Beta subunits, endowed with catalytic activity and interact with the template. They carry out the elongation of RNA.

Sigma subunits, which play a key role in the recognition of the promoter.

Mechanism of transcription in prokaryotes

[PDF p. 227]

1. Initiation step

It involves the interaction and identification of promoter regions. First, the RNA polymerase complex binds DNA at specific regions in the genome. These regions are known as promoter regions, and are recognized by the sigma subunit. It binds and forms a complex (closed complex) which undergoes structural changes that facilitate the separation of the two strands (open complex) . From here, transcription can start.

Promoter recognition : The promoter is referred to as the upstream/5’ region of the genome. The termination sequence is known as downstream/3’ region. The promoter is a part of the gene which is recognized by RNA polymerase, marking the initiation of the transcription process. When comparing one bacterial gene with another, we may come across consensus sequences, which are conserved between different genes. These consensus sequences are many promoters with a characteristic sequence. Another sequence greatly conserved is the -35 box. If we continue to move upstream, we may also find similarities in the

UP element.

This consensus sequence is the one which will be recognized by RNA polymerase, allowing it to initiate the process and carry it out in the proper orientation. These sequences are conserved in order to help position RNA polymerase in the right orientation.

2. Elongation

As it begins this step, the sigma subunit dissociates from the complex. The RNA complex leaves the promoter and proceeds with transcription, forming what is known as the transcription

bubble.

This process is accompanied by changes in the coiling of

DNA.

[PDF p. 228]

3. Termination

Two types of termination mechanisms end the process:

                   - **“rho” independent mechanisms:** some **sequences**

in the DNA template mark the end of the process. First, sequences rich in G and C give place to the formation of hairpin structures, which destabilize the complex between RNA polymerase and the template. After the G and C rich regions, there are Adenine rich regions: because the energy required for the pairing between As and Us is lesser than that of G and Cs, these A rich

regions will destabilize the complex. In this mechanism, structural features will put an end to transcription.

                   - **“rho” dependent mechanisms:** the **rho factor** is a

multimer with ATPase activity which recognizes and binds RNA. The ATPase activity allows it to move along the RNA strand until it comes in contact with RNA polymerase. Once in contact, it separates and destabilizes the transcription complex, inducing termination.

B. TRANSCRIPTION IN EUKARYOTES

Fundamental differences with prokaryotic transcription

  • In eukaryotes, transcription and translation take place in separate compartments . RNAs need to travel from the nucleus to the cytoplasm to be translated, and prior to its translocation, a maturation process needs to occur in the nucleus. This maturation process allows the existence of many additional regulatory processes. In prokaryotes, due to the lack of nucleus, transcription and translation can take place almost simultaneously.

In eukaryotes, DNA is tightly associated with proteins, forming a tertiary structure ( chromatin ), which contains histones and hundreds of other proteins which aid in DNA packaging.

  • In prokaryotes, there are no post transcriptional processes for RNA. The primary transcript is directly translated.

  • In eukaryotes, genes are transcribed by 3 different RNA polymerases, not only

one.

Eukaryotic polymerases do not come in direct contact with DNA. They need the help of what are known as transcription factors.

[PDF p. 229]

Eukaryotic RNA polymerases

RNA polymerase I carries out the transcription of ribosomal RNA (rRNA)

- RNA polymerase II carries out transcription of mRNAs and most sncRNAs

- RNA polymerase III carries out transcription of tRNAs and 5’-rRNA

RNA Polymerase II

It needs general transcription factors that are common to all genes to carry out transcription. There are also gene-specific transcription factors:

activators and coactivators.

In many genes we can find a region in the -30 position known as the TATA box (although it isn’t present in all genes). If we move upstream from this TATA box, we find that there are various regulatory sequences.

During transcription, there are also specific transcription factors which come into contact with RNA polymerase II (thanks to cofactors) and regulatory sites (thanks to activators), which signal the process to begin. Not all genes have the same regulatory regions and transcription factors; therefore, their expression will depend on hormones and signalling processes that activate different types of factors. Chromatin needs to be relaxed and decondensed for transcription, which is also mediated by the binding of specific transcription factors.

[PDF p. 230]

Mechanism of transcription in eukaryotes

1. Transcription initiation phase

It’s initiated by sequential binding of general transcription factors (GTFs) and RNA polymerase II to a promoter. During initiation, we find many types of transcription factors which for the preinitiation complex (PIC):

  • TFIID interacts with + and - regulatory proteins
  • TFIIF binds to RNA pol II (and TFIIB)
  • TFIIB binds to TBP (TATA box binding protein) and recruits the RNA polII-TFIIF complex.
  • TFIIE binds and exerts a helicase activity, separating

the DNA strands

  • TFIIH also presents a helicase activity, as well as kinase activity. It endowes this kinase activity on RNA polymerase II CTD (carboxyl terminal domain): the phosphorylation of CTD causes a conformational change that activates the polymerase activity.

This initiation phase is the most regulated phase in the process. It’s the most important control point.

2. Elongation phase

3. Termination phase

RNA PROCESSING

In both prokaryotes and eukaryotes, RNAs must be chemically modified to

attain their functional forms. The

reactions involved in RNA processing

include:

  • Nucleotide elimination

  • Modification of nucleobases

  • Nucleotide addition

Splicing reactions

[PDF p. 231]

mRNA processing in eukaryotes

mRNA is processed in eukaryotes, which allows additional regulatory points. We find:

1. Firstly, 5’ CAP synthesis, which stabilizes the RNA strand. The CAP synthesizing complex carries out this CAP binding to mRNA.

About this CAP structure:

    - mRNA capping **protects** the strand **from degradation** (stability)

    Participates in **mRNA transporting** from the nucleus to the cytoplasm.

2. Secondly, intron splicing takes place. Both introns and exons are transcribed, but introns are eliminated from the primary transcript and exons bind to form mature mRNA. This implies a significant change in size and the existence of many regulatory mechanisms and splicing

reactions.

This splicing is carried out by spliceosomes, sophisticated machinery which are small nuclear ribonucleoproteins. They perform the chemical reaction that breaks phosphodiester bonds and forms new ones between

exons.

In most genes, there are variations in these splicing reactions, known as alternative splicing. As a result, different variants coming from one same primary transcript appear. This mechanism expands the information contained in the genome (as it creates protein variants; for a single gene, we will be left with many mRNAs) and implies the existence of additional regulatory possibilities.

What will happen to the introns / the exons which are eliminated through alternative splicing? The excised portions of RNA will participate in the regulation of RNA expression by forming circular RNA structures. They’ll do so by binding and sequestering miRNA (seen in next lecture).

3. Thirdly, a 3’ poly-AA chain is added on the 3’ end of RNA. It is thought to protect RNA from degradation starting in the 3’ end.

[PDF p. 232]

Lecture 8.3. Regulation of gene expression

Genes are switched on by core promoters, which are usually found upstream from the transcription start site (TSS). But in order to regulate when that promoter is active or not, we need further signals.

Core promoters are modulated by millions of switches in our genome, known as enhancers (regulatory elements/developmental enhancers), in a process mediated by changes in chromatin conformation. The level of genome packaging will allow genes to come into contact with these enhancers/regulatory elements. Chromatin is usually repressive, as it is packed. All these functional regions have to be opened up and chromatin relaxed, creating regions of open chromatin known as DNAse I Hypersensitive Sites. These regions are opened by histone acetylases, therefore leaving promoters and enhancers free for binding.

Initiation factor’s components and assembly

Core promoter (TATA box) + transcription start site (TSS). TBP (TATA box binding protein) binds to TATA box; SP1 binds to GC boxes;

When all these factors assemble, RNA polymerase is phosphorylated, binds and initiates transcription.

What determines the assembly of this complex?

Once chromatin is opened through specific chemical reactions, regulation can start:

100 Kb upstream from the core promoters, we find the enhancers . Activators will bind to enhancers, creating enhanceosomes which will trigger DNA bending, by looping. These active enhancers (enhanceosomes) interact with coactivators (such as HATs) to stimulate chromatin remodeling and histone acetylation. This creates open sites. Enhancers will then become in contact with the promoter through the Mediator protein, triggering the assembly of RNA polymerase and general transcription factors at the promoter site.

[PDF p. 233]

Example: Inside each TAD, there are more probabilities of interaction. Specific TAD genes contact each other rather frequently. But flanked TADs will not come in contact with each

other.

If there is a CTCF binding disruption, for example due to mutation, TADs aren’t correctly flanked and this will give place to aberrant interactions. We are now left with a large TAD which allows enhancers to activate additional genes. There are specific enhancers for each tissue, to regulate specific gene expressions. If for example an enhancer of one TAD in the brain can become in contact with a gene from another TAD which should only be expressed in the liver, this will have physiological

consequences.

DHS (DNAse I Hypersensitive sites)

They are open chromatin sites and allow the activation/inactivation of genes.

  • There have been 3,000,000 DHS found in the past years.

  • DHS are very tissue specific: 50% are active in just one cell line; Around 1% (4,000) are

found in all lines.

  • Each of them is 100-200 Mb, so around 18.7% (⅕ of the genome) of the genome is comprised to regulation.

[PDF p. 234]

Histone acetylation

Histone acetyl-transferases (HATs) add acetyl groups on the tails which protuberate from histones. They can be acetylated or deacetylated. For example, when some lysines (+), which have a positive charge and are therefore attracted to DNA (-), are acetylated, the positive charge disappears and nucleosomes become more relaxed, with less interactions.

Histone deacetylases (HDACs) carry out the opposite reaction.

This acetylation creates open sites which will allow the binding of transcription factors, and transcription to begin.

Degradation of mRNAs

If we don’t regulate mRNA degradation, they are overtranslated and produce excessive proteins. Therefore, gene expression can also be regulated by the half life of its respective RNA.

- Through miRNAs

MicroRNAs (miRNAs) are genes which are transcribed and once transcribed, adopt a secondary structure in the form of a hairpin. This hairpin structure is processed through cleavage by dicer, and we end up with a miRNA duplex molecule. The duplex structure dissociates, so only one of the strands

is active.

The function of these miRNA is to find

the complementary sequence within the strand of target mRNA. Once bound, the mRNA is degraded and doesn’t translate into any more proteins.

RISC = “RNA-induced-silencing-complex” RISC targets miRNA once it has found its complementary sequence in a mRNA strand, to form the complex which performs the mRNA silencing.

[PDF p. 235]

- Through long non-coding RNAs (lncRNAs)

LncRNA acts as a “sponge” for miRNA (sequesters the miRNA), therefore activating and enhancing protein translation and production.

- Through Pseudogenes

Pseudogenes imitate the active DNA sequence, and therefore also act as a sponge and sequester miRNA. The available miRNA strands have to spread out to sequester both active and pseudogene mRNAs. Less miRNA will be inhibiting the active mRNA.

- Through circular RNA

Circular RNAs, which are differentially generated by alternative splicing of exons or from introns, also act as sponges for miRNA.

Introns are coded by parts of the genome which can overlap with the protein-coding regions that give rise to exons. Therefore, they can share common sequences with these genes. If they match the complementary miRNA, this allows them to sequester it, leaving less available for the mRNA which gives rise to proteins.

The exons excised by alternative splicing may also possess complementary sequences to that of the miRNA.

[PDF p. 236]

Lecture 8.4. Epigenetics: DNA methylation and histone modifications

DNA methylation is a form of epigenetic regulation of gene expression. Genes can also be regulated by specific histone modifications (acetylation or methylation). Different combinations of chromatin marks define the functional state of chromatin regions.

Promoters and enhancers have distinctive histone modifications.

DNA METHYLATION

DNA is methylated at cytosine bases (C), but only when the C is followed by a G base (C followed by G in the same strand / CpG dinucleotide). Methylation occurs at the 5’ carbon. DNA methylases are the enzymes which add this methyl group to Cytosines.

CpG dinucleotides are more or less distributed randomly through the genome. But when we look at which nucleotides are methylated or not, we notice that:

Unmethylated cytosines are concentrated around the promoter of most genes (they themselves act as the promoter), forming “Unmethylated CpG islands” . (Not all genes are regulated by CpG islands).

Passive and active demethylation

When methylation occurs, both strands are methylated.

  • Passive demethylation: When the cell divides, it does it semiconservatively, and we are left with hemimethylated DNA strands. Methylation has to be maintained, or else we would be left with unmethylated CpGs after many subsequent cell divisions. There would be a delusion of methylated molecules (passive demethylation).

Active demethylation: A few years ago, an active demethylation pathway was also discovered. Scientists discovered that it occurred through a combination of enzymatic complexes (TET) and BER. TET modifies methyl-cytosine, it hydroxylates it in several steps (5’ hydroxymethyl-cytosine), and continues to do so until it creates a 5’-carboxy carbamoyl methyl cytosine. BER (base excision repair) removes all of these modifications from the DNA by removing the modified Cytosine, and replaces it with a normal, demethylated C nucleotide. Therefore, demethylation is carried out by modifying methylated Cytosine until it becomes so different from an unmethylated C, that the cell has no choice but to replace the base through BER. There is no active demethylase , but there is an active demethylation pathway.

[PDF p. 237]

Active methylation

There are, however, methylases which carry out an active methylation pathway. It’s achieved through DNA methyl-transferase 1 (DMT-1), which is in charge of keeping DNA strands methylated even after cell division. There are also DNA-methylases-de novo, which methylate cytosines of CpGs which weren’t methylated

before.

They are both required to maintain correct DNA methylation levels.

Degree of methylation

DNA methylation and cancer

One of the ways in which cells become tumor cells, aside from mutations, can be due to changes in methylations in CpG dinucleotides from CpG

islands.

There can be a global hypermethylation of the promoters of CpG islands, disrupting their activity. Tumor suppressor genes (TSG) can become inactive due to hypermethylation, giving rise to

tumors.

[PDF p. 238]

HISTONE MODIFICATION

Nucleosomes are globular proteins with tails which protrude from the structure. They can be modified through acetylation and methylation.

N-terminal and Histone 3 (H3)

Lysine 4, lysine 9, lysine 27 and lysine 36 are important in the amino acid sequence of H3. Modifications in these groups will determine if a gene is active or not.

L9, L27 and L4 in inactive genes: Lysine 4 remains unmethylated, while Lysine 27 becomes trimethylated and Lysine 9 can be found di- or trimethylated. Nucleosomes around the promoter acquire these conformations.

Active genes: We find trimethylated Lysine 4 (L4) and trimethylated Lysine 36 (L36), while Lysine 9 and Lysine 27 become acetylated. When the promoter becomes activated, and genes are being actively transcribed, nucleosomes adopt this conformation.

ENHANCERS vs PROMOTERS

Enhancers also present a series of epigenetic modifications, but they are not promoters, so epigenetic modifications will be carried out in a different manner.

As a similarity, nucleosomes corresponding to enhancers and to promoters appear acetylated. There is also a nucleosome depletion in both cases. There are no nucleosomes at the centre of promoters or enhancers because transcription factors are going to bind to this active site, and need to be free for this binding.

The difference resides in the methylation of lysine 4 from enhancer and promoter nucleosomes: promoters present high levels of trimethylation, and low levels of di and mono methylation. With enhancers, the opposite occurs: higher levels of monomethylation and lesser di and trimethylation.

We can know if a promoter or an enhancer is being activated just by looking at the methylation ratio.

[PDF p. 239]

Lecture 8.5. The genetic code

History of the genetic code

Cracking the genetic code was one of the greatest scientific developments of the 20th century. This was thanks to the work of many people, such as Severo Ochoa, Robert Holley, Marshall Nirenberg. They received a Nobel Prize for their contributions. While cracking the genetic code, they reasoned that to codify 20 different amino acids, whilst having 4 nucleotides, each amino acid must be encoded by 3 nucleotides. The first codon identified was UUU (codifying for phenylalanine) in 1961.

Crick’s adaptor hypothesis

He suggested that in order for the cell to make proteins from the genome sequence, we would need something to transfer and adapt this information (what we now know as tRNA ).

Characteristics of the genetic code

  • There are 64 possible combinations of codons, formed by

the association of 3 different nucleotides.

  • The genetic code is universal (all living beings use the same genetic code).

  • The information is translated in the form of codons .

  • The sequences are read in a continuous way: 3 letter codons are read without spaces or overlapping. The reading frame, once established, is followed in this

fashion.

The genetic code is unambiguous : there is only 1 amino acid assigned to a particular codon.

  • The genetic code is redundant: we have 64 possible combinations but only 20 amino acids. Therefore, many combinations can codify for the same amino acid. Redundancy is usually found in the third letter. Why is this redundancy found in this 3rd nucleotide? We know that the codon and anticodon are complementary. The alignment between the both is done in an antiparallel way. The specificity of the anticodon for the first two nucleotides is very high, it will determine the specificity of the amino acid. The third is not so important: therefore, different genes which start with the same first two codons will be able to codify for the same amino acid.

[PDF p. 240]

Chemically similar amino acids have similar codons (polar, non-polar, positively charged,…)

  • The initiation codon is always AUG . This codon codifies methionine . Therefore all of our proteins begin with a methionine residue. It’s helpful to find the initiation sequence of a gene within the genome.

  • The termination codons are UAA, UAG, UGA, which are “nonsense” codons: they don’t codify for any amino acid, only signal the end of transcription.

Types of mutations and their impact on the polypeptide chain

a) Missense mutations: they change the sense/meaning of the codon, and therefore

cause one amino acid to change into another. The repercussions depend on the difference between the previously encoded amino acid and the new one.

b) Synonymous (silent) mutations: no impact on the genome (in terms of the protein

sequence), thanks to the redundancy of the genetic code. Called silent because the sequence of proteins remains the same, so it’s undetectable.

c) Nonsense mutations: there is a mutation which causes the codon to become a

nonsense codon (symbols termination). Generally, these mutations have a big impact because it signals the process of transcription to stop, and we are left with an incomplete protein.

d) Frameshift mutation: It is an insertion or deletion type of mutation, which displaces

the whole reading frame: from the point of the mutation onwards, the reading frame will be changed. The impact is generally pretty high.

Mutations in hemoglobin gene

- Sickle cell anemia

Disease caused by a point mutation (substitution). Similarly to what happens with mutations in glucose-6-dehydrogenase (G6DH), this

mutation is associated with resistance to malaria because it creates a

hostile environment for plasmodium falciparum in red blood cells. There is a mutation in position 6 in the beta chain, meaning a glutamine

becomes a valine.

Glutamine is negatively charged and valine is hydrophobic, so valines start to stick together and form fibers, modifying the structure of hemoglobin.

[PDF p. 241]

- Hb McKees-Rock

Mutation at the very end of the protein which causes hemoglobin’s chains to be of different

sizes.

- Hemoglobin Constant spring There is a mutation in the stop codon, which is changed by glutamine, and therefore the protein synthesis is continued until it encounters the next stop codon. We are left with a hemoglobin protein which is longer than a normal hemoglobin.

Curiosities

1. Why does the genetic code seem to be unaffected by evolution? If we change the genetic code, we change all our proteins. That would be uneventful for any organism, as it would have such a high impact, it would not be compatible

with life.

2. Why is redundancy associated with the third nucleotide in the codon? It avoids the impact of many mutations: a mutation in the third codon might not affect

the final amino acid if it remains with it’s first two nucleotides intact.

3. Why do chemically similar amino acids have similar codons? It reduces the impact of mutations: a mutation could imply a change in an amino acid, but if it has similar chemical properties, the interactions won’t be as perjudicial.

[PDF p. 242]

Lecture 8.6. Protein synthesis

Protein synthesis is a very complex and energy-expensive process. Around 90% of the energy produced is used for protein synthesis. It’s possibly the most complex biosynthetic process, and it’s carried out by:

messenger RNA (mRNA)

transport RNA (tRNA)

ribosomal RNA (rRNA)

ribosomal proteins

  • enzymes

protein factors

Messenger RNA (mRNA)

It’s a single strand of nucleotides with a 5’-CAP on the 5’ end and a 3’poly-AAA tail. Within the messenger RNA chain we can distinguish:

- Initiation codon

- Stop codon - Between them, the coding sequence / open reading frame (ORF), which has the information that will code for the respective protein. - UTRs (untranslated regions), as not all of the RNA chain is translated into a proteins

Ribosomes

They are the site of protein synthesis. Ribosomes are supramolecular structures, made up of 2 subunits: large and small. In between them, there is a channel through which mRNA advances while being translated. Both subunits are composed of rRNA (65%) and ribosomal proteins (35%); They are riboproteins : the large subunit contains up to 45 different proteins, whilst the small subunit has approximately 30 proteins, and less RNA.

Ribosomes can be free / associated forming polyribosomes in the cytosol, or found

bound to membranes such as the

membrane of the RER (because the proteins they synthesize will enter the lumen to suffer post traduction modifications).

[PDF p. 243]

Transfer RNA (tRNA)

The most important arms of tRNA are the amino acid attachment site (on the 3’ end) and the anticodon (which will recognize the codon). All tRNAs end in CCA, which is the sequence to which amino

acids will bind on the 3’ end.

PROTEIN SYNTHESIS

Activation of the amino acids

It consists in the binding of the aminoacid to the tRNA. Also known as the stage of synthesis of the aminoacyl-tRNA. We produce this aminoacyl-tRNA molecule through the formation of a covalent bond, carried out by a synthetase, in a process by which ATP will be

consumed and form AMP (2 ATP high energy bonds consumption). The goal here is to activate the amino acid by creating a high energy bond, and to create aminoacyl-tRNAs which are correctly assigned

to each codon in mRNA.

Errors in translation occur here, because the

synthetase introduces the wrong amino acid corresponding to the necessary anticodon.

From here, translation may begin, a polymerization process in which information from the genome is transferred to proteins through RNAs.

Translation

1. Initiation

The initiation process consists in the formation of the complex which will start the synthesis, by binding to AUG. Methionine will be the first protein in the protein chain, although in post-traductional modifications it can be removed. In mitochondria, as they are prokaryotes, the first amino acid is not methionine, but N-formyl-methionine (this confirms the theory that mitochondria used to be prokaryotic cells).

Components required for initiation include: a template, many initiation factors (eIFs), large and small ribosomal subunits, GTP and the activated methionine-tRNA complex.

[PDF p. 244]

Within the ribosome, we differentiate different sites:

  • The “A” (aminoacyl) site, through which the next complementary amino acid will

arrive.

  • The “P” (peptide) site, where the tRNA bound to methionine is found at the beginning of the process. In this site, the peptidyl - transferase will form the peptidic

bond between amino acids.

  • The “E” (exit) site. Not a very clear site in eukaryotes, but clearly distinguished in prokaryotes.

To carry out initiation, circularization of the mRNA occurs: The 3’-poly-A tail is the site where the polyA binding protein will attach. PABP also interacts with other initiation factors.

The small subunit of the ribosome is going to bind mRNA on the 5’ end, and from there it will move trying to find the AUG sequence. Once found, it stops and binds to the initiation codon, while the large subunit comes to the site and

binds too.

2. Elongation

We need eEFs (eukaryotic elongation factors), energy, many aminoacyl-tRNAs,…

            - The binding of the next incoming aminoacyl-tRNA occurs

            - Consecutively, the formation of the peptide bond takes

place within the P site, carried out by peptidyl-transferase. When people started to investigate this enzyme, they didn’t find it as it is not a protein: it’s a ribozyme, a non proteic molecule with enzymatic activity.

3. Termination

The protein continues to elongate until it reaches the termination codon, and subsequently a termination factor (eTF) binds, separating the complexes.

[PDF p. 245]

Polyribosomes

Polyribosomes are ribosomes found associated together, forming ribosome clusters, which carry out protein synthesis at the same time, using only one thread of mRNA.

“christmas trees of translation”

Inhibition of translation

There are many compounds which inhibit translation.

- Antibiotics: they inhibit translation in prokaryotes. Therefore, we use them as a treatment for disease, as they don’t have an effect on human translation. Examples are:

    **Tetracyclines**, which block the A site of ribosomes

    **Streptomycin**, which causes misreading and inhibits initiation

    **Cycloheximide**, which binds to the 50S subunit and blocks translocation

    **Puromycin**, which we don’t use as an antibiotic, as it’s a competitive inhibitor

of tyrosine-tRNA and can disrupt translation

- Toxins: they also block translation

- Diphtheria toxin - Ricin: It’s a molecule that is isolated from seeds. It’s a protein with 2 different subunits: the B chain is a lectin (recognizes carbohydrates) and introduces the molecule inside the cell; the A chain is an rRNA N-glycosylase enzyme which catalyzes the hydrolysis of an N-glycosidic formed by an adenine within

the 28S rRNA.

Barely any ricin is necessary to kill a person, as it’s not a usual inhibitor which acts on an enzyme. Ricin IS the enzyme, and therefore one ricin molecule it can carry out many thousands of hydrolysis reactions (murder of Georgi Markov, through a ricin pellet).

[PDF p. 246]

Lecture 8.7. Protein maturation, folding and targeting

Protein folding

During protein translation, protein folding begins. The protein folds sequentially and cooperatively, meaning that as some regions acquire their secondary conformations, it is easier for other regions to do so as well. Depending on the amino acid sequences, they will fold into alpha-helices or beta sheets. Sometimes, proteins aren’t able to fully form by themselves. They need the help of other cofactors and proteins. These other proteins which induce the correct conformation are known as chaperones.

During protein folding, there are several intermediate folding states, which are generally risky, as some hydrophobic regions become exposed and tend to cluster together and form aggregates by establishing hydrophobic interactions. Chaperones are involved in:

Avoiding the risk of hydrophobic regions being exposed long enough to form aggregates

They bind to the hydrophobic regions, unfold them and allow the aberrant proteins to become correctly folded

Lastly, some are involved in protein targeting and transport within the cell

Chaperones

HSP (heat shock proteins) (Proteins which were discovered to be present in the cell after a heat shock, as many other proteins had been denatured) and Chaperonins.

  • HSP interacts with proteins during their folding process. They can also bind poorly folded hydrophobic regions and by hydrolyzing ATP, favour their denaturation so they can fold correctly.

                           **Chaperonins** are multiprotein

complexes, which are involved in the folding of proteins that do not fold spontaneously. One of the best known chaperonins in eukaryotes is HSP60. They are made up of several rings that form a chamber, inside which the correct protein conformation is induced.

[PDF p. 247]

Protein’s final destinations

Most proteins are synthesized at RER. But, how do the ribosomes know that they have to be

bound to the RER membrane?

This is achieved due to specific signals contained within the protein, known as signalling peptides, which c ause the protein to translocate to the RER. These signals are found in the N (amino) terminal and are generally similar. There are some consensus sequences for these signalling peptides.

Features:

  • At the C-terminal of the signal peptide (NOT of the protein), there is a short sequence of polar amino acids with small side chains. This is the point where the protein will cleave the signal peptide (there is an amino acid that constitutes the point of proteolysis).

In the N terminal, we generally find positively charged amino acids.

  • The signal peptide core is rich in hydrophobic amino acids.

Directing proteins to the ER

The amino (N) terminal region contains the signal peptide. The protein begins to form and once the N terminal emerges from the ribosome, the sequence is recognized by a protein, the signal recognition particle (SRP) ribonucleoprotein.

It attaches to the ribosome and this

stops translation, which allows the

ribosome to bind to the RER

membrane, and for the amino

terminal to enter inside the reticulum.

Once inside, the SRP hydrolyzes GTP. This is the signal which disrupts the interaction between SRP and the signal peptide, causing them to dissociate. From here, translation is continued within the RER lumen, until the protein is synthesized and a peptidase proteolyzes the signal peptide.

[PDF p. 248]

Glycosylation of proteins

Many proteins undergo chemical modifications such as glycosylation, which consists in the addition of oligosaccharides to the protein side chains. It’s an essential step in its

maturation towards the final conformation.

N-linked glycosylation begins in the RER while the protein is still being translated. The amino acids to which oligosaccharides will bind are those with H groups (such as asparagine). O-linked glycosylation generally occurs further on, in the Golgi apparatus.

Although the oligosaccharides which bind differ from protein to protein, they have a common core which is synthesized inside the reticulum, through many transferases . Once formed, specific transferases transfer this core group to the protein, so it can bind.

Incorrect glycosylation is the cause of many proteinopathies . The disruption of the process generates stress inside the reticulum and triggers the UPR (unfolded protein response), either to reactivate glycosylation or promote apoptosis.

Protein targeting for their transport into the nucleus

Proteins for the nucleus have a nuclear localization sequence (NLS). They are very short and rich in hydrophobic regions, which are recognized by nuclear transport systems. Targeting of nuclear proteins is mediated by importins and the

GTPase Ran.

Importins (participate as heterodimers, with 2 subunits) bind to the specific sequences and transport the proteins close to the nuclear pore complexes. Through the nuclear pore, proteins translocate into the nucleus. Once in the nucleus, 2 additional proteins (RAN, a GTPase, a small G protein; and CAS, Cellular-apoptosis susceptibility protein) participate: Ran binds to the importin beta subunit and separates it from the protein. Protein-alpha importin complex binds to Ran and CAS, causing the release of the nuclear protein.

There is a Ran reactivation process which involves the hydrolysis of a GTP molecule.

[PDF p. 249]

Lysosomal targeting

Another well-known transport system of proteins within the cell is that of lysosomal hydrolases. The presence of one or more mannose 6-phosphate (M6P) residues is the structural signal that drives these enzymes to the lysosomes. Impairments in this transport cause the accumulation of waste products inside lysosomes, that will lead to cell dysfunction and serious diseases known as “Lysosomal storage diseases” (sphingolipidoses, glycogenosis or mucopolysaccharidosis).

Protein degradation

2 ways to degrade proteins:

- In the lysosomes

  • Through proteasome. The information for the half life of a protein is contained within said protein. In eukaryotes, proteins are linked to the ubiquitin protein, through which their half life will be mediated. The more ubiquitin present, the more likely for them to be degraded by proteasome. Cyclins contain a sequence that makes them more likely to be ubiquitinated, and therefore have a shorter lifespan.

[PDF p. 250]

Lecture 8.8. Protein degradation: lysosomes

Discovery of lysosomes

They can be isolated by applying ultracentrifugation to a suspension of broken cells containing subcellular components. After the 1st centrifugation, a pellet at the bottom of the tube is observed, containing the cell’s nuclei. This pellet is known as the nuclei sediment. On the top layer rests the supernatant. If we centrifuge the supernatant at a higher speed, for 15 minutes, 2 new factions are formed. The bottom layer corresponds to a mitochondria, peroxisomes and lysosomes sediment. If we continued to centrifuge, we would ultimately obtain ribosomes.

Structure and components of lysosomes

The lysosomal membrane is made up of a bilipid layer that contains many elements:

    - We find **carrier proteins,** as many of the components which are hydrolyzed

in the lysosome are going to be exported out of it for their metabolic use. For example, LDL. The lysosomal enzymes are going to degrade LDL, and free cholesterol will be carried out of the lysosome, into the required area of the cell, by these carrier proteins. - The inner side of the lysosomal membrane is highly glycosylated. The purpose is thought to be that glycosylation protects the lysosomes from being digested by the enzymes on the inside.

    Lastly, the presence of **proton pumps** keeps the inner meidum at a low pH.

There is an ATPase enzyme which will import H+ into the lysosome to lower the pH, down to 5. It’s lowered so enzymes can work at their required pH. The lysosome contains at least 40 types of acid hydrolases (lipases, nucleases,..), which can digest molecules. But to carry out their function they need to be

found in an acid medium.

Not every single lysosome contains every hydrolase (each lysosome carries out the digestion of specific molecules), but all of them posses a hydrolase that is common to all, ACID PHOSPHATASE, which is a universal marker for the recognition of lysosomes (as it is found in all).

How material is incorporated for digestion

                 **Primary lysosomes:** in the vesicles coming from the

Golgi apparatus, there is not such a low pH (pH=7) so enzymes are inactive. These vesicles which aren’t active for digestion are known as primary lysosomes.

[PDF p. 251]

Secondary lysosomes: when primary lysosomes fuse with late endosomes, they form secondary lysosomes, in which pH=5 and will carry out digestion.

Types of digestion

A) Intracellular digestion

1. Heterophagy: cells digest molecules coming from the outside of the cell (pinocytosis and phagocytosis)

2. Autophagy: by this process cells are able to digest their own components for different purposes. What’s the use of autophagy?

    **Under normal conditions,** autophagy degrades **damaged**

components in the cell.

          - When there is a **lack of nutrients,** fasting

starves the cells and promotes them to digest some of the cell components to obtain energy (ex: autophagy in muscles, in prolonged starvation, causes muscle atrophy).

    **When organelles have undergone hypertrophy,**

for example due to intoxication with lipid-soluble drugs (degraded in the SER through P450) like phenobarbital, which will give place to an overload of the organelle. Once it has undergone this hypertrophy, it will be degraded.

B) Extracellular

2 types of cells able to carry out extracellular digestion.

- Sperm: they have special lysosomes known as acrosomes. Acrosomes are found in the heads of the sperm, and once it contacts the oocyte, the acrosome fuses with the plasma membrane and all the enzymes are released outside the cell to degrade the membrane of oocyte, for fertilization.

[PDF p. 252]

  • Osteoclasts: bones undergo constant formation and destruction. Two cells are in charge of this process: osteoblasts (in charge of bone formation) and osteoclasts (responsible for bone degradation). They have specific lysosomes with enzymes able to digest the components

of mineral bone.

Extracellular digestion is not a common process, and is highly specialized.

Lysosomal enzymes tagging and transport

The lysosomal enzymes are tagged by Mannose-6-phosphate (M6P)

Firstly, the lysosomal enzyme is synthesized in the Rough Endoplasmic Reticulum (RER), where a carbohydrate (Mannose) is also added through N-glycosylation.

Next, the molecule travels to the Golgi Apparatus, where Mannose is phosphorylated by the sequential activity of two enzymes.

Within the Golgi Apparatus, Mannose-6-phosphate binds to a receptor. This induces the release of the enzyme, packaged in a transport vesicle.

  • The vesicles fuse with late endosomes to form

secondary lysosomes, where low pH causes the dissociation between the enzyme and its receptor.

The receptor is recycled and returns to the Golgi Apparatus.

LYSOSOMAL STORAGE DISEASES (LSDs)

This family of diseases is characterized by:

- Abnormal storage within lysosomes

  • Lack of digestion of substrates

The presence of lysosomal enzymes in the extracellular space

All of this causes cellular stress and results in cell malfunctioning. Most are autosomal recessive disorders, such as Inclusion-cell disease/mucolipidosis type II and Gaucher’s disease; others are X chromosome linked, like Fabry’s disease.

They are rare, but there is a high incidence of LSDs in endogamic populations. This is one of the reasons why there are no drugs or treatments for these disorders.

[PDF p. 253]

a) INCLUSION (I) - CELL DISEASE

I-cell disease is an autosomal recessive condition, a type of LSD caused by a mutation in the enzyme which carries out tagging, phosphotransferase . ( Molecules generally need tagging to remain within the cell or in a specific organelle, a sort of barcode. If there is no tag, the enzymes/proteins denature and are released outside of the cell). As a result, endosomes are left full of undigested material. Having endosomes full of undigested material is very stressful for the cells, as it blocks their normal functioning, and therefore there is cell malfunctioning. The presence of enzymes outside the cell is another problem: they won’t be fully active due to the pH, but they do present some activity, and are therefore able to digest some extracellular components, giving rise to defects and mental retardation.

Patients with this disease will suffer from many defects in development (as cells can’t use nutrients), dwarfism,… and have a very low life expectancy.

b) GAUCHER DISEASE

Gaucher disease is caused by a glucocerebrosidase deficiency (Ga-Glu). Therefore, in the cells there are depositions of glucosylceramide. It accumulates in cells, mainly affecting macrophages.

Macrophages are mainly found in the bone, liver, spleen,… which is why many of the clinical manifestations affect bone, liver and spleen. There are skeletal and neurological defects (as glucocerebrosides are also found in neurons), loss of mineral bones and also an enlargement of liver and spleen.

It can be treated by:

Symptomatic treatment

Bone marrow transplant, by first getting rid of all the defectuous stem cells and replacing them with transplanted healthy ones

Enzyme replacement therapy: many bacteria are covered in glycans, which are what macrophages use to recognize and bind to them during infection. This property can be used in enzymes, by covering glucocerebrosidase with mannose to favour its endocitosis, tricking the macrophage into thinking it’s a pathogen

Gene therapy, which has the same goal: to replace the efficient enzyme

[PDF p. 254]

TBL 8.1. Epigenetic-related diseases

Usually, when looking at a specific gene, both alleles should be transcribed, but some regions in the genome contain genes which are expressed only from one of the two chromosomes. This is achieved through IMPRINTING, a mark which tells the chromosomes which allele is the one that will be expressed. It’s an epigenetic mark. Imprinting is regulated by lncRNAs, antisense genes, epigenetic markers… When there are disruptions in these imprinting mechanisms, diseases arise. Only around 150 genes throughout the genome are imprinted .

Experiments done whilst investigating imprinting genes

During the experiments, a normal fertilized egg was formed when the pronuclei from the mother and father fused together.

But when two sets of maternal information were taken

and fused together, this gave place to a gynogenetic embryo ( an embryo formed from eggs without genetic contribution from penetrating sperm), which resulted in a cyst. When the information all proceeded from the father, an androgenetic embryo was formed.

The embryo, to be viable, needed one set of chromosomes from the mother and another from the father. Therefore, parental genomes are NOT equivalent: there are regions of differential expression, which will depend on the parental origin (IMPRINTING). In these regions of imprinted genes, it is all done in a coordinated manner so only maternal or only paternal genes are expressed, depending on the region in question. Each cluster is regulated in a specific manner. This regulation is carried out by strands of lncRNA, CTCF binding, methylation, transcription of antisense genes and many other factors.

Example of CTCF binding

CTCF only binds to CpG islands when they are unmethylated. When the CpG islands are unmethylated, CTCF binds and creates a TAD, acting as an insulator . Therefore, Igf2 (insulator growth factor 2) becomes inactivated .

When the island is methylated, CTCF doesn’t bind and H19 doesn’t transcribe as its promoter

is blocked. But this allows the enhancers to act

on Igf2, permitting its transcription, as the two regions are no longer flanked by CTCF.

[PDF p. 255]

The SNRPN domain (PWS and AS)

The CpG island is the promoter for a long ncRNA gene. When this island promoter is methylated (in the maternal allele), the lncRNA won’t be transcribed, allowing the expression of Ube3a but not of the other antisense genes found on the left, which will remain untranscribed . When the promoter is unmethylated (in the paternal allele), lncRNA will be expressed. This lncRNA strand will overlap and cover the gene for Ube3a, making it silent, as RNA polymerase II won’t be able to reach the promoter. In this case, the antisense genes on the left will therefore be able to be transcribed, as Ube3a has been silenced.

Imprinting genes must be reset during gametogenesis

During gametogenesis, imprented regions must be reset and re-established depending on the sex of the individual who is going to pass on the genes: if it is a male, both his maternal and paternal chromosomes will have their imprented regions erased and then remethylated according to the paternal methylation pattern. In the case of a female, the opposite will happen, as she is going to pass on her genes as maternal. In conclusion, after active demethylation during gametogenesis (although there are no active demethylases, there is an active demethylation pathway), the marks corresponding to oogenesis or spermatogenesis have to be established.

Prader-Willis and Angelman’s syndrome

- PWS: mutations and lack of genes in the

P aternal chromosome

- AS: mutations and lack of genes in the

M aternal chromosome

[PDF p. 256]

Causes of Prader-Willis syndrome (PWS) and Angelman’s syndrome (AS)

a) Microdeletion

Microdeletion, either in the paternal chromosome ( PWS, prevents genes from the paternal chromosome from being expressed) or maternal chromosome (AS) can occur, preventing some genes from being expressed and therefore giving rise to one syndrome or the other (depending on which genes aren’t being expressed). Microdeletion generally occurs because of unequal recombination during segmental duplication (this implies that non-equivalent regions are exchanged between homologous chromosomes during meiosis, which will lead to microdeletion). Microdeletion can also be the result of strand slippage during replication .

Prader-Willis Angelman’s

b) UPD (uniparental disomy) affecting the SNRPN domain

During UPD, instead of having 2 chromosomes 15 marked as maternal and paternal, there are 2 chromosomes marked both as maternal or both as paternal. If they are both paternal, there will be an over-expression of all the antisense genes (which isn’t really a problem), but no Ube3a; if they are both maternal, there will be Ube3a expression, but no expression of the antisense genes (seen in red).

How does this UPD happen? It occurs by non-disjunction in meiosis I: during the first meiotic phase, instead of each chromosome migrating to different poles of the cell, they both go to the same gametocyte. Therefore, at the end of meiosis, this will result in cells with 2 copies of chromosome 15 (they are not haploid cells, it’s a sort of mitosis), whereas others

will have none.

When “gametocytes” fuse during fertilization, we will find trisomic zygotes on one hand , and monosomic zygotes on the other. Both zygotes are incompatible with life. Therefore, the forming embryo will take different courses of action:

[PDF p. 257]

In the case of trisomics, the embryo excludes one chromosome randomly. Depending on which one is excluded, the embryo may end up with chromosomes coming from the same parent, giving place to

UPD.

In the monosomic case, the embryo duplicates the only existing chromosome, giving place once again to UPD.

Prader-Willi’s syndrome is caused by maternal uniparental disomy (mUPD). Angelman’s syndrome is caused by paternal uniparental disomy (pUPD).

c) Mutations in Ube3a

Mutations in Ube3a in the maternal

chromosome (only chromosome in which it is active) can cause Angelman’s syndrome. But a mutation in Ube3a in the paternal chromosome won’t be a cause of Prader-Willi’s syndrome, as this gene is already silenced in the said chromosome through imprinting mechanisms.

d) Mutations in the imprinting coding region (ICR)

It accounts for less than 5% of the causes for each syndrome. As a consequence, there cannot be demethylation and reestablishment of the corresponding imprinting patterns.

Diagnosis of each syndrome in the laboratory

The most common cause for both syndromes is microdeletion, when there is unequal recombination due to segmental duplicates. Once microdeletion has been discarded as the possible cause, other tests can be performed.

PCR is performed for the diagnosis of any of the two diseases. PCR testing will recognize if there are both methylated and unmethylated regions. Specific primers are used, one which amplifies the methylated regions only, and the other which binds and amplifies the unmethylated only.

[PDF p. 258]

In a person without the disease, methylated and unmethylated corresponding bands should appear. But in case of disease, we would see only the methylated or the unmethylated band, depending on the syndrome.

- Diagnosing Prader-Willis Syndrome

For PWS, if the results of the PCR are normal, we can discard the possibility of disease as it is only caused by maternal UPD (uniparental disomy) or microdeletion in the paternal chromosome. In the case that the person does indeed have PWS, we would only see a methylated band on the PCR results (due to microdeletion or to UPD). Consequently, the next test to be performed would be a FISH assay, to determine if the cause is microdeletion or

UPD.

If there is microdeletion, it’s detected through this FISH

assay. If there is no microdeletion, UPD has to be confirmed by analyzing microsatellites, to tell if the homologous chromosomes are identical (UPD) or different.

- Diagnosing Angelman’s syndrome

For Angelmans syndrome, if the PCR result is normal, there may be another possible cause: a mutation in the Ube3a gene, which will not appear on the PCR and has to be detected throughout further testing. PWS is not caused by mutations in the Ube3a gene, as a mutation in the paternal Ube3a gene would have no visible effect since it is silenced in said paternal chromosome. In the paternal chromosome, what is expressed in the SNRPN region (where Ube3a is found) are the antisense genes.

On the PCR results, in case of not being normal, we would only see an unmethylated band corresponding to the paternal imprinted region. Once again, this result is

either due to microdeletion in the maternal

chromosome or due to paternal UPD. If the bands are abnormal, we would again carry out a FISH assay, analyze microsatellites in case of suspecting paternal UPD,…

[PDF p. 259]

[PDF p. 260]

Lecture 9.1. General principles of clinical genome analysis

Chromosome structure and types

Chromosomes have a “p” and a “q” arm (short and long), each with distinct bands. Light and dark bands can be distinguished thanks to Giemsa staining (G bands). After replication, the sister chromatids are bound by a centromere. At the terminus regions of chromosomes, we find protective ends known as telomeres .

There are different chromosome types, depending on the location of the centromere:

- Metacentric

- Submetacentric

- Acrocentric

- Telocentric

Human chromosome nomenclature

          - Numbers from 1-22 are assigned + X and Y chromosomes

(sex chromosomes)

           - Short arm = p; Long arm = q

           - Arms are divided into many bands and sub-bands.

Subdivision is done according to centromere proximity of said

band

How to do a Karyotype?
