A Karyotype is the ordered representation of all human chromosomes according to size and banding pattern. Every chromosome can be recognized.

A substance known as Giemsa is used to stain the

chromosomes when they are in their most compacted forms (during metaphase). Giemsa will produce G bands: darker G bands correspond to more gene dense regions, whereas lighter ones correspond to regions where genes are less compact.

These bands reflect the active TADs: clear bands

tend to have more active genes, and darker bands correspond to compacted and repressed genes.

Additionally, an ideogram represents the map of a single chromosome.

[PDF p. 261]

The Karyotype is a very specific array of all the chromosomes. They are organized from the largest to the smallest (exception in chromosomes 21 and 22. Initially, chromosome 22 was thought to be smaller), and present different centromere locations.

The typical Karyotype for a female is 46, XX The typical Karyotype for a male is 46, XY

FISH assay

When the changes in chromosomes are small and can’t be observed on the karyotype, FISH technique is employed (Fluorescence in Situ Hybridization). Double DNA strands, when separated (denatured) by heat, bind complementarily when they are left to renature (anheyl). This process of complementary binding is known as hybridization. In FISH assay, fluorescently tagged probes are introduced in the sample, and once DNA and the probe are denatured, the probe anheyls to its corresponding sequence.

If we see the probe fluoresce, the region is present. If the probe does not fluoresce, it means it hasn’t bound due to a microdeletion in the specific region it was sent to bind to.

It’s also very useful to detect breakpoints in translocation, as well as fusion points.

(Example: FISH assay on chromosome 15. We can observe an aberration in one of the copies of chromosome 15).

CHROMOSOME ABNORMALITIES

Classification of chromosomal abnormalities

- According to which cells are affected

- Constitutional: they are present in all nucleated cells since early development.

- Somatic/Acquired: present in certain cells or tissues, and not in all cells.

- According to how the disease arises

- Structural abnormalities: such as duplications, inversions, deletions,… - Numerical abnormalities: changes in the number of chromosomes in a specific set (aneuploidy) or changes in the ploidy of the individual (polyploidy), caused by errors in chromosomal segregation.

[PDF p. 262]

A) Structural abnormalities

These abnormalities are caused by unrepaired chromosome breaks or defects in the recombination process (unequal crossover), due to the presence of scattered repeats.

  • Inversions (inv): they occur when a segment

of the chromosome is cut and inverted when

it’s rejoined. The inversion can be paracentric (when they don’t include the centromere) or pericentric (when the breakpoints are on either side of the centromere, and include it. There is a breakpoint in each arm). Example: 46, XY, inv(11)(p11;p15)

  • Duplications (dup): occur when specific segments are doubled. They will have to be very large to be detectable on the Karyotype. Example: dup(7)(q21;q23)

  • Ring chromosomes (r): consists in a fusion

between telomeres. The free ends fuse

together, and we end up with a ring structure. Example: 46, XY, r(7)(p22;q36)

● Isochromosomes (i): an arm is lost, and the remaining arm is duplicated. We are left with two identical arms. The total number of chromosomes is still 46, but there is a serious modification in the genes. Example: 46, X, i(Xq)

  • Deletions (del): we are left with a reduced number of genes.

    - Terminal deletions: Example: 46, XY,

del(7)(p22;pter) - Interstitial deletions: Example: 46, XX, del(5)(q13;q33)

  • Translocations (t): they consist in rearrangements in which two non-homologous exchange segments. In this way, derivative chromosomes are formed. Translocations are easily detected through FISH assay. The translocation can be reciprocal or non-reciprocal.

    Reciprocal translocation: both non-homologous chromosomes exchange

segments of their genome. It can either be balanced (if it’s an even exchange) or unbalanced (if it’s an unequal exchange which results in extra or missing genes). - Non-reciprocal translocation: they are one-way translocations

Example: 46, XX, t(2;5) (q31;p21). By convention, the lower number goes first. q and p numbers symbol the bands involved.

[PDF p. 263]

B) Numerical abnormalities

They involve the loss or gain of complete chromosomes, and can affect only a set or the whole ploidy of the individual.

1. Polyploidy

- Triploidy, which is mostly caused by dispermy (2 cells which fertilize a single egg), or by the presence of a diploid oocyte. A full triploid organism is not compatible with life, whereas a diploid/triploid mosaic can survive. Mosaic = thought to occur by the fusion of the 2nd polar body with a normal diploid cell in the zygote. Gives rise to a “Mixoploidy”

- Tetraploidy, a situation which is always lethal for the zygote. There is a failure to complete the first zygotic division.

2. Aneuploidy (affects not the whole ploidy, but only a specific set)

- Nullisomy, where 0 chromosomes are present in a specific set.

- Trisomy, where there is 1 extra copy of a particular chromosome. DOWN’S SYNDROME consists of a trisomy in chromosome 21, which is a trisomy compatible with life due to the reduced size of said chromosome. KLINEFELTER’S SYNDROME is another trisomic exception as it consists of a trisomy in the sex chromosomes (XXY). 47, XYY syndrome can also occur.

- Monosomy, where there is 1 missing copy of a particular chromosome set. TURNER SYNDROME is characterized by a monosomy in the X chromosome, making it the only viable monosomy which is compatible with life. Monosomies generally have more catastrophic consequences than

trisomies.

[PDF p. 264]

Karyotype examples

Example of a trisomy: 47, XY, 8+ (trisomy 8) (it’s a cancer leukemia cell) Example of a translocation: 46, XY, t(9;22) (there is a translocation. By FISH, we can actually identify the breakpoints in each chromosome) Example of Down’s syndrome trisomy: 47, XX, 21+ (trisomy 21. Causes Down’s Syndrome). It’s the only trisomy which is compatible with life, as well as trisomies in sex chromosome. Sometimes, we can also find the karyotype 46, XX, +21 / 47, XX, +21: This is a situation in which not all cells in the body possess this trisomy = mosaicism.

Effect of maternal age and Nondisjunction

The risk of having a child with Down’s syndrome increases with the age of the mother. This is because these abnormalities are caused by nondisjunction during meiosis: instead of each homologous chromosome migrating to different poles of the cell, they migrate together, and therefore nullisomic and disomic gametes arise. When these disomic gametes fuse with sperm during fertilization, they form trisomic zygotes for chromosome 21.

Spontaneous abortions and stillborns can occur due

to chromosomal aberrations.

Until week 7, there is a 20% prevalence of

chromosomal aberrations

From weeks 7 to 20, the prevalence is of 35%

From weeks 20 to 40, there is a 4% prevalence

Robertstonian translocation or Centric fusion

It involves acrocentric chromosomes. This type of translocation involves only chromosomes 13, 14, 15, 21 and 22, because the ends of their short arms have similar repetitive DNA sequences that predispose to their fusion. After the double strand break and consequent translocation, we are left with:

  • 1 dicentric chromosome, which is stable in mitosis. The 2 centromeres where fusion has taken place acts as 1 big centromere.
  • 1 acentric chromosome, which is unstable and lost during mitosis without

consequence. Acrocentric chromosomes fuse together, joining two “long arms” of DNA into one.

If this translocation occurs in a potential gamete, this person becomes a carrier of a Robertstonian translocation. There’s a possibility of passing missing or extra DNA on to your children, resulting in

monosomies or trisomies.

[PDF p. 265]

Lecture 9.2. Patterns of single gene inheritance

The Mendelian rules determined the laws of gene inheritance. They give a pattern for transmission of genetic disease (depending on if an allele is dominant, recessive, X linked,…). Mitochondrial inheritance follows different patterns of inheritance.

Pedigree/Genealogy

X-Chromosome inactivation characteristics

There can only be one X active X chromosome. Therefore, whenever there are 2 (XX, female) or more, all except 1 will form highly condensed chromosomes: Barr bodies.

  • Under normal circumstances, inactivation is random . It takes place during the 8-cell stage, meaning some cells will inactivate the maternal X and others, the paternal X. As a consequence, heterozygote XX individuals will be mosaics for X-linked recessive diseases. Affecting cell lines which didn’t inactivate the carrier X.

The inactivated X however, isn’t completely inactivated, only 60% of it. The other

40% remains active.

  • XIST gene is expressed in the X chromosome that will be inactivated

TYPES OF INHERITANCE

A) Autosomal: related to autosomes (non-sexual chromosomes). Autosomal inheritance can be dominant or recessive, depending on which will be expressed in the phenotype

over the other.

- Autosomal recessive inheritance

In case of recessive alleles, the individual must possess two copies of it for the character to be expressed (be homozygous for said character). Therefore, a person can be healthy while carrying the recessive allele for a specific disease. These people are known as carriers. In order to work out the risks for someone to suffer from a disease, we use Punnet’s squares, a method to calculate the outcome of different matings. A feature of many recessive disorders, especially rare conditions, is that affected individuals often have two identical mutant alleles because the parents are close relatives. They are said to be consanguineous.

[PDF p. 266]

Autosomal recessive diseases

 - **CYSTIC FIBROSIS:** there are changes in the viscosity of

the mucus, so cilia cannot work properly and suffers from recurrent infections. Also affects the pancreas. People affected are homozygous for the same mutation.

              - **SICKLE CELL ANEMIA:** a mutation in

the beta-globin chain from hemoglobin causes it to form rigid fibers, giving the red blood cells a sickle-like shape and impairing their function. The sickle cells have a shorter life span that leads to anemia, and can block small blood vessels, causing hypoxic tissue damage.

Although carriers of an autosomal recessive disorders are considered asymptomatic, they may nevertheless express some disease-related trait that can distinguish them from the normal population. Take sickle-cell disease: affected individuals are homozygous for a β-globin mutation and produce an abnormal hemoglobin. Carriers of the sickle-cell mutation are not quite asymptomatic. The sickle-cell allele produces a mutant β-globin that is co-dominantly expressed with the normal β-globin, and heterozygotes can have mild anemia (sickle-cell trait) which can worsen under intense, stressful conditions such as exhaustion, hypoxia (at high altitudes), and/or severe infection.

- Autosomal dominant inheritance

Dominant alleles, when present, will always be expressed. They do not need the individual to be homozygous. It’s typical to find most of the offspring affected (at least ½ or ⅓ ). Additionally, an heterozygous individual affected by a dominant allele could have the possibility of not passing it on to their offspring.

Autosomal dominant diseases

- FAMILIAL

HYPERCHOLESTEROLEMIA:

mutation which conferres a

resistance to cholesterol uptake (for example, due to mutation in APOB 100 gene, the ligand present in

LDL-cholesterol which binds to the

LDL receptors in cells), causing it to

accumulate in the bloodstream and

supposing a risk for atherogenesis.

[PDF p. 267]

Penetration and expressivity

Incomplete penetration: A curiosity about autosomal dominant inheritance is that sometimes, for autosomal dominant diseases, the dominance isn’t completely penetrant and only 80% of the people with the dominant mutation present it in the phenotype (present the disease). The molecular basis for incomplete penetration isn’t very well known. It is thought that there are additional genetic variants, in the promoter for example, which change the dominance and expression of the dominant allele.

Expressivity: way in which the disease is expressed. Many times, people with the same disease may not present the same symptoms. This is because the same mutation isn’t expressed in the same manner. Each manifestation panel is represented on the pedigree by only colouring-in specific segments of the affected individuals.

B) X Linked mutations

  • X linked recessive inheritance

It is specially prejudicial for males, as they only count with one X chromosome: therefore, even if the disease is recessive, males will show the disease when in possession of the mutated X chromosome. These males will only pass on the disease to daughters, which will express the recessive disease only if the mother also passes on the recessive X mutated chromosome. Therefore it’s in very rare cases when women

X linked recessive diseases

    - **DUCHENNE’S MUSCULAR DYSTROPHY:** disease

caused by a mutation in the gene coding for dystrophin, a protein found in association with the

inner side of the membrane of skeletal and cardiac

muscle cells. The muscles become damaged, and it can eventually lead to death by muscle dysfunction (the weakened diaphragm causes respiratory failure, and cardiomyopathy can lead to heart failure).

[PDF p. 268]

- X linked dominant inheritance

X linked dominant diseases

- HYPOPHOSPHATEMIA: X linked hypophosphatemia is a disorder related to low levels of phosphate in the blood. Phosphate is a mineral that is essential for the normal formation of bones and teeth. It can give rise to X-linked

rickets.

MITOCHONDRIAL GENOME INHERITANCE

Diseases due to mutations in the mitochondrial genome only come from the mother. Males NEVER pass the disease, as they don’t contribute mitochondria during fertilization. There can be lots of incomplete penetrance, depending on the proportion of mitochondria which present the mutation. They can even not show the disease if the amount of mutated mitochondria is very low. Mitochondrial inherited diseases are very variable.

[PDF p. 269]

Lecture 9.3. Polygenic inheritance

Multifactorial diseases have both genetic and environmental factors (cholesterol, quality of the air, smoking, weight, diet,..). In general, most traits are the result of the interaction between many genes and the environment. In addition to being multifactorial, multiple genes contribute to the risk of suffering a certain disease (polygenic inheritance) such as cancer, asthma, diabetes, heart failure,…

The genetic risk is less easy to quantify and identify than environmental factors. We all have some sort of risk, but only when this risk goes beyond a certain thresh-hold will a person manifest the disease. Many methods can be used to estimate the heritability of

multifactorial diseases.

How can we quantify genetic risk?

A) Firstly, we can quantify the risk by looking at the family history of the disease. There

is a small proportion of people in the general population with certain diseases, called sporadic cases, versus familial cases, which are prevalent in successive generations of a family tree.

    Looking at the prevalence of a disease in the general population gives a

certain probability (generally environmental factors)

    When looking at the prevalence of a selected disease in a family/group of

relatives, we obtain another probability (which involves the genetic factors which establish predisposition to said disease) .

By calculating λ (relative ratio or relative risk) = the division of both probabilities,

we get an idea on the general risk to develop a disease (heritability), taking into account the prevalence of disease in the general population and genetic factors in your specific family tree.

B) Another way to quantify heritability is by using studies with twins.

Many pairs of twins are used, in which at least one has the disease being studied, to identify the heritability of diseases. Once we have selected the many pairs of twins, we study the concordance rate (frequency in which, when one has the disease, the other also presents it. If they both have the disease, they are concordant; if only one of them has it, they are discordant). If out of 100 diseased individuals, 80 of the twins from the pair also present the disease, there is a concordance rate of 80%.

h = 2 x (Cmc - Cdc)

[PDF p. 270]

Heritability will only be high when the concordance rate presented by monozygotic twins is higher than that of dizygotic twins (Cmc>>Cdc). Monozygotics share many more genes in common: if the concordance rate is only high in monozygotic twins, it means the disease is mainly caused by genetic factors, excluding the additional environmental ones (more or less exclusion depending on the concordance rate presented by dizygotic twins).

ALZHEIMER’S DISEASE

Alzheimer’s disease (AD) is caused by multiple pathogenicity, meaning that many different disruptions lead to the same disease, they converge into the same final result.

Two main problems lead to the disease: the accumulation of beta-amyloid (which is prone to aggregation. It forms fibrils that clump up together and accumulate outside the neurons, forming amyloid plaques) and TAU protein (Associated to microtubules. If it gets phosphorylated, it dissociates and microtubules stop working within the neurons, forming neurofibrillary tangles inside them).

Apart from beta amyloid and TAU, there are other

factors which confer risk for Alzheimer’s. One of the

most well known risk variants is APO-E4, which

increases the risk and lowers the age at which early onset takes place. APO-E lipoprotein is a gene which has multiple allelic variants in the population. Depending on your genotype for APO-E, your risk of developing early onset Alzheimer’s varies: there are risk APO-E variants and protective APO-E

variants.

APO-E2 is a protective variant and decreases the risk and early onset.

APO-E4 increases the risk.

-early onset, lower than 65 years -late onset, over 65 years old

[PDF p. 271]

Molecular characteristics of Alzheimer’s

The disease can arise by many ways:

    - The **overproduction of beta amyloid** due to **defects in cleaving enzymes**

(overly active β -secretase )

- Faulty processing of β -amyloid

1. Accumulation of β -amyloid: In the bilipid membrane, there is a protein known as Amyloid-precursor-protein (APP). The APP processing reaction is carried out by secretases ( α, β 𝑎𝑛𝑑 γ), which cleave APP at certain points. The catalytic subunit of γ -secretase is a presenilin protein ( presenilin 1 or 2).

When the first cut is done by beta-secretase, it will later on trigger a higher the release of a 42 amino acid isoform. If it’s done by alpha-secretase, the cut is done in specific places which won’t produce the beta-amyloid fragment which conferres the disease.

γ-Secretase carries out the second cut and cleaves at alternative single locations to generate a series of Aβ isoforms of different lengths. The Aβ42 isoform (42 residues long) is thought to be the greatest contributor to pathogenesis, as it is prone to form amyloid aggregates. But under normal conditions , it’s not produced in large quantities, unlike the predominant Aβ40 isoform.

    - The most causative **APP mutations** which give rise to Alzheimer’s are

heterozygous missense mutations distributed close to APP cleavage sites, as they increase the Aβ42/Aβ40 ratio (especially γ-secretase cleavage sites, suggesting that γ-cleavage events or their regulation are critical for development of disease). - The causative mutations in PSEN1 and PSEN2 ( which act as cofactors for beta-secretase) are also heterozygous missense mutations; they, too, seem to have the effect of increasing the Aβ42/Aβ40 ratio.

[PDF p. 272]

2. Processing of beta-amyloid: On top of the effect of APP cleavage sites, there is the

influence of APO-E. Each of the alleles of APO-E is a combination of two different

SNPs. The “rs” number makes reference to the specific SNP in each allele which will change the specific amino acid (amino acids 112 and 158).

  • The combination of T and T gives the allele epsilon 2, a protective variant against

Alzheimer’s

  • The presence of T and C in each of the SNPs gives epsilon 3 (normal allele in the population,doesn’t confere risk nor protection)
  • C and C give place to epsilon 4 allele, which conferres a risk for Alzheimer’s

disease.

Just having 1 epsilon-4 allele increases the risk of disease 3-5 times, whereas having 2 epsilon-4 alleles increases the risk up to 15 times.

APO-E is involved in the clearance and cellular uptake of amyloid-beta. If it’s not removed properly, amyloid-beta oligomers accumulate and cluster to form their characteristic plaques . If the clearing pathway is functional and works well, even if you produce much beta-amyloid, the risk is highly reduced and you won’t necessarily present the disease.

There are also environmental factors involved in Alzheimer’s disease, such as exercise. But

ultimately, there is a strong genetic component associated with it.

[PDF p. 273]

Lecture 9.4. Cancer genetics

Concept of oncogenes and tumor suppressor genes

Oncogenes are abnormal versions of protooncogenes (genes coding for cell cycle promoting proteins such as cyclins and EGF, which stimulate the cell cycle when carrying out their normal function). When there is a gain of function in these genes due to alterations, it makes proteins overstimulate the cell cycle, and the cells tend to become malignant/cancerous as they proliferate too much

and accumulate mutations.

Oncogenes are known as dominant genes, because a point mutation in only one oncogene allele results in an overstimulation of cell proliferation.

Tumor suppressor genes code for proteins involved in stopping or slowing down the cell cycle (p53, Rb, p21,…). A decreased function in these genes, for example due to mutations, makes cells divide constantly and uncontrollably, giving place to cancer.

- p21 is a TSG which blocks the activity of cdk/cyclin complexes

- Rb is found bound to E2F, and when phosphorylated, it separates and liberates E2F, which will translocate to the nucleus as a transcription factor.

These tumor suppressor genes are known as recessive genes, as they need the presence of 2 mutated copies to function abnormally, giving place to cancer.

How are oncogenes generated from protooncogenes?

1. Deletions or point mutations in the coding sequence. For example, what occurs with the EGFR gene during cancer: there is an activating mutation, which produces a hyperactive protein that over-stimulates the cell (whereas normally, mutations are inactivating and cause inactivation of proteins). It acquires more functionality than normal, so there is increased signalling.

2. Gene amplification This happens in the case of HER-2 receptors during breast cancer: there is no mutation, so it’s a normal version of the gene, but too many copies of the protein are produced due to hyperstimulation of said gene. Therefore during cancer we find more copies of the gene than normal, resulting in overly expressed proteins on the cell membrane which promote the cell cycle.

[PDF p. 274]

3. Chromosome rearrangement

    It can occur through **fusion,** resulting in the **hyperactivation of a protein.**

    Through the **relocation of a promoter region,** causing it to be near the

gene. The nearby regulatory sequence causes a normal protein to be overproduced (this can occur, for example, when a virus infects and introduces its genome into the host’s DNA. The infecting virus can alter the promoter, causing it to overproduce the protein it regulates) .

How are tumor suppressor genes inactivated?

They can be inactivated by a series of changes affecting both alleles (we usually need dysfunctionality in both alleles for TSGs to be inactivated, although having one dysfunctional allele makes the other more prone to suffer mutations and silencing).

We need combinations of different events which result in the inactivation of both alleles.

1. Inactivating point mutations

2. Chromosome deletion

3. Chromosome loss (of a whole chromosome) 4. Silencing through DNA methylation: if the promoter region is methylated, the gene won’t be produced. Tumors use this system to shut down tumor suppressor genes, preventing their expression.

All these combinations, affecting both alleles, will result in the inactivation of tumor suppressor genes (TSGs).

[PDF p. 275]

EGFR mutations (epidermal growth factor receptor)

The oncogenic version of EGFR is very important in lung cancer. These EGFR hyperactivating mutations are present in 10-15% cases of lung cancers ***** (drugs ending in -ib are chemical compounds; ending in -ab are therapeutic antibodies).

There are chemical compound drugs which can be used to treat this sort of lung cancer caused by EGFR hyperactivation.

- Gefitinib

- Osimertinib

They are used as targeted therapy, directed to a specific protein/gene and not all cells in proliferation (as occurs with chemotherapy).

Hyperactivating mutations cause an excessive activity of the EGFR in the membrane. This produces enhanced signalling and a promotion of the cell cycle.

  • Gefitinib can be injected to block kinase activity of tyrosine kinase. This will inhibit the signalling process. This is why these drugs are generally known as tyrosine-kinase inhibitors (TKI): they prevent kinase from binding and phosphorylating, stop signalling and stop the cells from dividing.

At the beginning, most patients respond to the drug. But ultimately, up to 60% develop a resistance mutation (T790M) in the kinase domain. Due to this mutation, the drug can’t be active as it can’t bind to the kinase domain. The tumor develops resistance and learns how to survive in the presence of the drug. Pharmaceutical companies therefore developed Osimertinib.

  • Osimertinib is able to bind to the normal and mutated kinase

domain. This drug is also able to reach the brain (unlike chemotherapy, it can pass the blood brain barrier, enter the brain and attack metastatic sites), making it more efficient.

[PDF p. 276]

HER-2 and breast cancer

Today (29 oct) is world breast cancer day! 25-30% of patients with breast cancer overexpress HER-2.

- Trastuzumab

- Pertuzumab

HER-2 (human epidermal growth factor receptor-2) is overexpressed in primary tumors and metastatic sites in many types of cancers, mainly breast cancer.

  • Trastuzumab is a recombinant monoclonal antibody, which binds to the outer domain 4 of HER-2. Many mechanisms have been attributed to it, but the binding to the HER-2 receptor and subsequent inhibition of the signal transduction is thought to

be the main mechanism of action.

A toxin, known as TDM1, can be combined with trastuzumab to increase the potency of this treatment. The trastuzumab-TDM1 complex binds and blocks HER-2. This binding induces endocytosis, and the cell internalizes the HER-2 receptor + antibody-toxin complex. It’s all degraded in lysosomes, from where TDM1 is released inside the cell and acts by disrupting the correct functioning of microtubules. It causes cell cycle arrest and apoptosis by the inhibition of microtubule assembly.

  • Pertuzumab is a humanized monoclonal

antibody that acts by binding to domain 2 of HER-2 receptor, which is involved in the homodimerization process needed for downstream signalling (mechanism carried out by all tyrosine kinases.First, ligand binding. Then dimerization. Lastly, it becomes active by reciprocal phosphorylation).

A combination of these two antibodies + chemotherapy gives out better results when fighting

cancer.

➢ FISH assay

It can be used to detect the amplification of

HER-2 in breast cancer.

A green centromere probe is used to label

chromosome 17 in which there is an HER-2

gene copy, and a red probe is used to detect the HER-2 gene. What we usually see in cancerous cells is one green dot, followed by many red ones, which would correspond to abnormal cells in which there has been gene amplification.

[PDF p. 277]

TBL 9.1. Polymerase Chain Reaction (PCR)

The process used in PCR is replication, from which we produce DNA from DNA.

PCR is necessary because searching for a certain sequence on a single DNA strand is like looking for a needle in a haystack. Before PCR, scientists had to proceed to search through the whole genome. In 1980, an idea arose to put an end to this problem.

PCR technique basics

To know if the sequence we are looking for is present, we need a process which copies and amplifies that specific DNA sequence. It’s possible to copy a fragment repeatedly, through replication, by using a DNA polymerase enzyme. For replication, each strand will be used as a template.

PCR is therefore carried out to obtain millions of

copies of a specific DNA fragment.

To carry out the process in a test tube:

1. Denaturation: firstly, the double stranded DNA has to become denatured and separate. This can be done by establishing high temperatures, at around 90 degrees celsius.

2. Primer annealing: DNA polymerases need the presence of a primer to begin replication (a small DNA sequence which binds to the 5’ end). For it to anneal, the temperature has to be lowered (50-70 degrees). This step is critical for specificity: we want very specific primer fragments, which will only bind to the sequence of interest. If it’s not specific enough, there will be replication of other fragments and contamination of the sample. 3. Addition of DNA polymerase: The polymerase will copy and carry out the extension on the DNA strand. For extension, a temperature of 72 degrees celsius is required.

By repeating this process in many cycles, we increase the number of copies of the sequence in interest.

[PDF p. 278]

By the end of the second cycle, we manage to flank a small sequence by using 5’ and 3’ primers, which corresponds to the fragment of interest. These small sequences are the ones which will be repeatedly increased after each cycle.

This is a simple procedure, but due to the different temperatures required for each cycle, we are presented with a problem: when restarting each cycle, the DNA polymerase

enzyme will be denatured at the high temperatures required for the denaturing of the strand and primer annealing (90 degrees, 50-70 degrees). Thermus-aquaticus polymerases (TAQ polymerases) are used to solve this problem, as they are extremophilic enzymes which can work optimally at temperatures even higher than 90 degrees celsius.

What do we need to carry out a PCR?

  • We need to know the sequence for the ends of the fragment, in order to synthesize the primers. - Within the tube, we need: the DNA sample, DNA primers, free available nucleotides, TAQ polymerase enzyme, and a buffer solution (with a particular pH and ions). This is known as the Master Mix. - Equipment needed: a machine known as the thermal cycler, which carries out the required fast changes in temperature.

From here, Denaturation, Primer annealing and Extension can occur in many cycles. Typically, around 35-40 cycles are carried out, which is enough, as half a million fragments are produced at the end.

Visualization of the result

For visualization, many techniques can be employed:

                             **Fluorescent dyes which are able to**

bind DNA: if they bind, they fluoresce, and can be observed under UV light. The fluorescence is present when the fragment has been replicated. If the specific sequence which was being searched for isn’t present, the sample won’t fluoresce.

[PDF p. 279]

Quantitative PCR: more quantitative methods to visualize PCR results can be used. Quantitative PCR uses a fluorescent probe, which binds to the DNA strand. The Reporter is a fluorescent molecule, but in the presence of the Quencher, the light is sequestered and there is no light emission. Once PCR begins, TAQ polymerase copies the DNA strand. When it reaches and encounters the probe, in order to continue replication, it hydrolyzes it. The Reporter and the Quencher are separated.

Now, the liberated Reporter is capable of emitting light.

With this technique, fluorescence can be detected in real time. We can measure the light according to each cycle, making it a more quantitative method. To visualize quantitative PCR/real time PCR, we use a

machine able to detect fluorescence.

PCR applications

a) Molecular identification

- Gene expression analysis: different cells express a different set of genes. Additionally, the amount of expression will be different depending on the tissue. This gene expression, which leads to proteins, can be assessed through immunohistochemistry, gene electrophoresis (for detecting the protein) or ELISA (antibodies/antigens). But this type of detection system has limitations:

    An antibody is required

    The sensibility of the technique is typically low, so if there is a low protein

expression, it cannot be detected. PCR serves as an additional tool for gene expression assessment: instead of directly measuring proteins, we amplify the RNA sequences expressed in each cell type to detect the presence of said proteins. Problem: TAQ polymerase only works on DNA. Therefore, we will need retrotranscription for this detection system, by taking viral machinery which carries out retrotranscription. mRNA is retro-transcribed into cDNA ( complementary DNA) by using an additional primer. To measure how much DNA is present in the solution after retrotranscription, we run a PCR, which will give different graphs depending on the initial amount of DNA. This whole process is known as RT-PCR (retrotranscription-PCR)

[PDF p. 280]

  • Pathogens: pathogen identification through RT-PCR can be carried out by introducing primers specific for the pathogenic genome.

- Forensic medicine

- Disease-risk identification

- Disease-treatment customization

b) Genetic engineering

(to obtain recombinant insulin for example, by carrying out PCR to obtain many copies of insulin genes)

Detection of SARS-CoV2

( Document on ADI)

[PDF p. 281]

TBL 9.2. Diseases caused by expansion of trinucleotide repeats

There are a series of diseases caused by the expansion of trinucleotide repeats. They are usually repaired by MMR (mismatch repair), but some escape, giving place to

disease.

There are two main groups of diseases caused by the expansion of trinucleotide repeats:

Caused by the expansion of CAG trinucleotide (Huntington’s disease)

Caused by expansion of other, non-CAG trinucleotides (Fragile X and myotonic dystrophy). These diseases are usually neuropathies, and they all show anticipation. (Dynamic mutations, meaning they expand in subsequent generations)

1. CAG trinucleotide expansion diseases

These CAG expansions have an autosomal dominant inheritance. They are characterized by expansions of the CAG trinucleotide, which is located in coding regions of the mutated gene. This CAG trinucleotide codes for glutamine, so there will be an expansion of glutamines in the polypeptide chain. This expansion seems to cause a gain of function (the protein gains a new function and sticks together with other molecules), with toxic effects, generally causing neuropathies.

Neuropathies caused by CAG trinucleotide repeats have a series of common features:

Anticipation: they all present anticipation, meaning that the disease appears earlier after each subsequent generation (first generation, age 45. Second generation, age 39,… after each generation, the age of onset is earlier). This occurs because, after each generation, the size of the expansion becomes larger. The more repeats, the more severe the pathology, therefore symptoms appear earlier.

The expanding repeat is in a coding sequence of the gene (CAG = codes for glutamine).

The expansion is more frequent in the paternal line. These expansions take place mainly in spermatogenesis : during spermatogenesis, there are many more cell

[PDF p. 282]

divisions which involve replication (where trinucleotide expansion takes place). When a father is the one who transmits the repeat, it will expand into a larger mutation in the offspring due to spermatogenesis which occurred in the father.

The expansion results in the gain of function, not a loss of it (which would cause the gene to be silent).

Strand slippage during replication which isn’t repaired through MMR gives place to these expansions.

A) SPINAL CEREBRAL ATAXIA (SCA12)

SCA stands for Spinal cerebral ataxia Ataxia is a problem with the coordination of walking, due to problems in the cerebellum. The death of neurons in the cerebellum and spinal cord leads to ataxia. SCA12 is the only CAG expansion disease which isn’t found in a coding region, but in 5’UTR.

B) HUNTINGTON’S DISEASE

It’s a dominant autosomal disease. It’s caused by a mutation in the huntingtin gene: the expansion of the CAG trinucleotide in the gene leads to a protein with additional glutamines. This has several repercussions:

  • The polyglutamine mutation alters the function of huntingtin protein: there is a lack of interaction between the domains of the protein it codes for, making it l ose its function and making it prone to form aggregates and toxic deposits.

The expansion can also affect the expression of some genes which are very important for neuronal function, such as Dopamine. Abnormal huntingtin protein will prevent the interaction of transcription factors with the promoters of these genes. Another important disruption is the decrease in Beclin, altering autophagy.

(Beclin is one of the molecules which regulates autophagy, and in order to regulate the process, it has to be un-ubiquitinated by ataxins. If ataxins are expanded due to the presence of mutated huntingtin, they don’t function correctly. Beclin remains ubiquitinated, and is therefore a target for proteasomes. Beclin levels in the cell decrease (they are degraded), so autophagy isn’t correctly regulated, leading to the death of neurons).

[PDF p. 283]

2. NON-CAG trinucleotide expansion diseases

The expansion occurs in non-coding regions of the gene. These mutations affect by changing the expression of a coding gene (ex: expansion in promoters), and not the amino acid sequence.

  • There is no gain in function, but a loss of function. There is a lack of protein expression due to effects in regulation and promoter regions.

  • There is anticipation in these diseases, except in Friedreich’s disease. When passing on the expansion mutation to children, it continues to grow (anticipation).

  • Additionally, the expansion is caused by hundreds of thousands of repeats which involve a premutation state. Premutation state: It’s a range number of premutated alleles between healthy and pathogenic, which imply predisposition or high risk of developing full, pathogenic mutations. Only premutation alleles will transition to mutation, it’s a transition state that alleles go through.

In these non-CAG expansions, the expansion is more frequent in the maternal

line.

This is because the mechanism of expansion in these diseases isn’t strand slippage during replication. It’s thought to be due to unequal crossing over, as they are explosive expansions. Because this unequal recombination occurs in oogenesis and not spermatogenesis, there is maternal transmission.

A) FRIEDREICH’S ATAXIA

It is the only of these non-CAG trinucleotide expansion diseases which doesn’t present anticipation.

[PDF p. 284]

B) FRAGILE X SYNDROME

It affects the gene FMR1, which encodes for the protein FMRP (fragile mental retardation protein). This syndrome is caused by an expansion of CGG trinucleotides in the 5’-UTR.

The expansion shuts down the expression of the gene due to epigenetic factors: in full mutations,

the FMR1 mRNA strand can interact with its own

DNA due to the many CGG repeats. This leads to the formation of an RNA-DNA loop, which attracts epigenetic repressors. As a result, there is decreased FMRP (the protein coded by FMR1). The absence of the protein alters the function of neurons, as its function is to move/shuttle mRNAs (which have structures known as TG-quartets) that play important roles in neuronal functions.

Just one X chromosome with the expansion will lead to Fragile X syndrome, which causes mental

retardation.

X-linked dominant inheritance. Sherman paradox

(DOMINANT inheritance, although with incomplete penetrance , as there are premutated alleles). Black numbers depict the number of repeats; blue percentages depict the probability of having offspring with the disease. The pre mutated alleles expand until reaching >90 in the third generation. Sherman came up with the theory that as we move down, the probability of women having offspring

with the disease increases.

We can find normal transmitting males. They transmitted the disease, but didn’t show incomplete penetrance, as they had a premutated allele. The allele expands in the maternal transmission, not in the paternal transmission. A premutated male will pass-on those premutation repeats, and not an expanded allele. He is technically a carrier, but doesn’t suppose an expansion in repeats. These males are healthy, but it has been seen they can develop neurological symptoms at a very advanced age (ex: tremors). In conclusion: The probability of passing from premutation to full mutation increases as the size of the repeat increases as well, and expansion only occurs in the maternal

line.

[PDF p. 285]

- TBL 9.3. Dysregulation of the cell cycle in cancer

CANCER is a genetic disease which consists in the alteration of oncogenes and tumor suppressor genes . Inherited mutations, genetic and epigenetic changes produced by carcinogens, viral infections or chronic inflammation cause normal cells to transform into tumor cells, which don’t respond to regulation and start dividing uncontrollably.

There are many risk factors, such as diet, age, gender, and lifestyle choices like alcohol and tobacco consumption.

➢ Tobacco smoke and cancer:

Tobacco components are carcinogenic . Some of these compounds bind DNA irreversibly, causing DNA damage and producing mutations which can affect oncogenes or tumor suppressor genes; tobacco smoke also causes chronic inflammation: due to prolonged consumption, acute inflammation arises. A chronic state of inflammation causes inflammatory cells to damage surrounding

tissues.

    A mechanism used by macrophages and other leukocytes in this

inflammatory process is the production of ROS, which normally kills pathogens but in chronic inflammation, will reach our own cells and damage

them.

    Additionally, many **cytokines** are produced, to recruit more cells. Tumors can

make use of these cytokines, by expressing receptors for them, to grow and proliferate. These receptors are in general found on immune cell’s surfaces, but tumors can also express them. Therefore, inflammation will enhance their growth.

Its common effects are lung and bladder cancer.

Comparison: healthy lung and smoker’s lung under the microscope. Macrophages become full of carcinogenic

residues.

Characteristics of tumor cells

- High proliferation potential (altered expression of GFs and continuous activation of intracellular pathways maintain autonomous and uncontrolled growth).

- Unlimited replication capacity (the presence of telomerase allows the tumor to acquire unlimited replication and evade senescence).

- Blockage of apoptosis (tumors increase the expression of antiapoptotic proteins such as BCL-2 and block proapoptotic genes, like BAX and BAK)

- Metastasizing capacity (by decreasing the adhesion between neighbouring cells)

- Induction of angiogenesis (formation of new blood vessels; otherwise, the tumor will undergo necrosis)

- Evasion of the immune system

[PDF p. 286]

We can find different types of tumors:

Benign tumors (slow growth rate, highly differentiated, don’t metastasize). Benign tumors coming from epithelial cells are known as papillomas (stratified epithelium) or polyps (cylindrical epithelium).

Malignant tumors (high growth rate, undifferentiated, metastasize). Malignant epithelial tumors are known as carcinomas and non-epithelial malignant tumors are called

sarcomas.

INDUCTION OF CANCER

1. Carcinogens They produce acquired genetic alterations. Two main types:

a) Physical carcinogens

- Ionizing radiation, such as X-rays. These radiations produce DNA oxidation and strand breaks. Leukemia is a malignancy that develops frequently in the first years after exposure to ionizing radiation.

- Non-ionizing radiation, such as ultraviolet rays. UVA rays cause indirect damage to DNA by generating free radicals that can oxidize, while UVB rays cause direct damage through the production of pyrimidine dimers. These dimers, if not repaired through NER mechanism, can generate mutations in genes involved in the cell cycle. Melanoma is associated with the presence of TT dimers in tumor cells.

b) Chemical carcinogens

Tobacco is an example of a chemical carcinogen. Some of its compounds damage DNA and alter critical genes, and their continuous inhalation causes inflammation which can become chronic, inducing the production of ROS and cytokines which stimulate the proliferation of tumor cells.

2. Microorganisms

- Helicobacter pylori : bacterium which possesses carcinogenic potential. Its presence in the gastric mucosa causes chronic gastritis (inflammation) leading inflammatory cells to produce free radicals which can damage DNA.

- Hepatitis C virus (HCV): its oncogenic capacity is associated to chronic hepatocyte infection. The development of a hepatocarcinoma is related to the virus’ capacity of surviving in the liver.

[PDF p. 287]

Papilloma virus (HPV): it’s a type of oncogenic virus. It introduces its viral genome into host cells’ and there is latent transcription and translation of viral proteins E6

and E7. These cause the inactivation of tumor

suppressor genes such as p53 (which is mainly in charge of stopping cell proliferation when damage is detected and, if not repaired, carrying out apoptosis) and Rb (becomes permanently phosphorylated, therefore E2F constantly promotes replication and cell division).

3. Genetic factors

a) Alterations in DNA repair mechanisms: damage to DNA has to be repaired to

maintain the integrity of the genome, through repair mechanisms such as NER. The alteration of NER causes a disorder with recessive autosomal origin known as Xeroderma Pigmentosum (XP), where children present erythema and in later stages, cutaneous atrophy and telangiectasia.

b) Oncogenes: they are the altered version of protooncogenes. When mutations take

place in those genes, there is abnormal cell proliferation as protooncogenes are in charge of stimulating cellular growth. Oncogenes are dominant genes (mutation in one of the alleles is enough to produce an abnormal proliferative effect). There are different types of oncogenes:

- Growth factors, who’s overexpression causes hyperstimulation of cell proliferation.

- Growth factor receptors, such as EGFR and HER-2 which can become overexpressed on cell membranes and overactivate signalling cascades due to constitutive activations or hyperactivation of the regulatory region.

- Genes of intracellular signalling pathways. KRAS, B-RAF mutations can alter the MAPK pathway.

Some tyrosine kinase-type proteins implicated in intracellular signalling can

also be altered. This occurs in CHRONIC

MYELOGENOUS LEUKEMIA, where the translocation of protooncogene Abl from

chromosome 9 to chromosome 22

produces an abnormal chromosome, called Philadelphia chromosome. This results in a fusion protein (Bcr-Abl) with a constant tyrosine kinase activity that promotes uncontrolled proliferation.

[PDF p. 288]

Genes related to apoptosis (mutations in anti-apoptotic genes). This type of genetic modification is produced in FOLLICULAR LYMPHOMA. The translocation of proteins 14 and 18 causes overexpression of BCL-2.

c) Tumor suppressor genes: they code for proteins which inhibit the cell cycle. The

loss of functionality in these genes (in both alleles) results in uncontrolled proliferation. TSGs are recessive genes (require mutation in both alleles to affect). There are different types of TSGs:

    - **Retinoblastoma (Rb),** which is found bound to **E2F** transcription factor to

inactivate transcription. E2F becomes active when Rb is phosphorylated. The inactivation of Rb in both alleles makes it unable to perform its function.

- p53, which under stressful conditions halts the cell cycle in order to repair DNA damage. It induces the expression of p21, an inhibitor of Cyclin D/Cdk4, allowing to stop the progression of the cell cycle. p53 is found mutated in

50% of human cancers. Individuals who inherit an altered

copy of p53 suffer from LI-FRAUMENI SYNDROME and present a high risk of developing cancer if the other allele

becomes mutated.

d) Epigenetic alterations

- DNA methylation, which can result in the silencing in the expression of a

gene.

- Histone modifications. In tumors, patterns of histone methylation and acetylation are found altered, which may lead to the silencing of TSGs such as p21.

Families of antitumoral drugs

- Alkylating agents, such as platinum derivatives, which react with DNA generating DNA breaks and induce apoptosis.

- Antimetabolites, structural analogues of purines and pyrimidines which generate altered DNA sequences and result in ruptures (ex: antipyrimidine 5-fluoracyl ).

  • Mitotic inhibitors, such as Vinca alkaloids and taxanes (ex: taxol), which interfere with microtubules during mitosis and cause cell death.

- Topoisomerase inhibitors

- Antitumoral antibiotics (ex: anthracyclines )

[PDF p. 289]

Classification of inhibitors

  • EGFR inhibitors: Gefitinib and Osimertinib (block ATP binding site).

  • HER-2 inhibitors: Trastuzumab and Pertuzumab (antibodies which bind and block different HER-2 ligand binding domains)

  • VEGF and VEGFR inhibitors: Bevacizumab (antibody against VEGF, inhibits endothelial cell proliferation and angiogenesis)

Tumor angiogenesis and metastasis

Tumors requiere vascularization to survive. If not vascularized, they undergo necrosis . The situation of hypoxia and hypoglycemia in which the tumor is initially found promotes angiogenesis by stimulating expression of VEGF (HIF-1). Angiogenesis facilitates the entry of tumor cells into circulation, resulting in metastasis . This is also favoured by the destruction of E cadherin and of connections with neighbouring cells and the ECM.

Vascularization of normal cells vs vascularization of

tumors: in tumor vascularization, there is no hierarchy (veins, arterioles, capillaries,…) because blood vessels in tumors grow very fast and in a disorganized way. There is a morphological and biochemical difference, which can be used to target specific vasculature of the tumor and not that of normal cells, by using anti-VEGF drugs, such as Bevacizumab, which prevents the binding of VEGF to its receptor.

Evading the immune system

Cancer cells produce tumor antigens that are not expressed on the rest of the cells. They present some foreign antigens (proteins) which result from mutated protooncogenes/TSGs. These antigens, together with MHC-I, can be recognized by CD8 T-lymphocytes. They will carry out phagocytosis and present tumor antigens to T-CD4 lymphocytes. But this mechanism isn’t always achieved, as tumors manage to evade the immune system by decreasing the expression of MHC-I or tumor antigens. Therefore autoantigens aren’t presented, and T cells cannot carry out surveillance.

They also express TGF-beta, a growth factor which in normal tissues stimulates proliferation of fibroblasts, but also has a potent anti-immune effect, deactivating the killer function of T cells. In patients without a functional immune system/immunosuppressed, tumors are expressed at a higher frequency, as there is completely no tumor recognition.

[PDF p. 290]

Immunotherapy

The tumor types that best respond to immunotherapies are lung cancer and melanomas. This is because they have a high load of mutations which translate into new aberrant proteins, meaning there are more antigens for T lymphocytes to recognize.

BREAST CANCER

Incidence is very high and increasing, mainly due to exposure of new carcinogens and new detection systems. 1 out of 8 women in Spain will develop breast cancer over the course of their lives. Survival rate is quite high as the result of development of new treatments and early detection methods. If detected early, there is an almost 100% chance of survival.

- Normal breast

  • Ductal carcinoma in situ: In DCIS, glands are enlarged, with lots of cells invading the duct. Tumor is still only found within the gland, although enlarged.

  • Invasive carcinoma: In invasive carcinoma, glands and ducts can no longer be observed, as they are overpopulated by tumors.

Different tumors express or repress different types of proteins. Not all tumors express estrogen receptors; some learn how to survive without estrogens or progesterone, making them estrogen-progesterone negative. Tamoxifen can only be used to treat estrogen-receptors expressing tumors.

Pertuzumab is used in combination with trastuzumab, as not all tumors respond well to each drug. They target different domains of HER-2 receptors (domains 2 and 4), making it

more effective treatment.

[PDF p. 291]

TBL 9.5. Gene mutations as therapeutic targets in cancer

CLINICAL CASE

NGS techniques can test in a very small amount of sample, thousands of genes (high sensitivity)

A patient presents the following type of stage IV lung cancer:

The initial lung tumor was treated with Genitifib after tumor DNA sequencing (NGS) detected a mutation in L858R, which affects the cytoplasmic domain of EGFR making it overly active. The tumor showed significant regression until May 2015, when the patient presented tumor growth and pleural effusion. She had developed resistance to Gefitinib (acquired resistance).

Alterations in the EGFR gene

  • In 40% of cases of cancers caused by point mutations, the mutation is L858R in Exon 21 from the EGFR gene. Hotspots are regions of oncogenes where mutations are seen frequently. They correspond to activating mutations, very specific alterations which are going to result in a hyperactivity of the gene.

  • We can also find in-frame deletions.

Either by point mutation or deletion, there is a very subtle change in the protein associated with gain of activity.

Typically, these kinds of mutations are associated with a specific profile: adenocarcinomas, non-smokers, asian and young females.

[PDF p. 292]

Endothelial growth factor receptors (EGFRs)

EGFR is a member of the transmembrane receptor family. It’s part of the HER-1 family, a type of tyrosine kinase which becomes activated by the binding of EGF. L858R mutation affects the cytosolic domain, specifically an area close to the ATP binding site. Therefore, this will affect the activity of the protein. With the mutation, Km goes up, but Kcat increases even more, causing efficacy to increase for ATP and the peptide.

This causes an overactivation of the receptor. It’s so strong, it doesn’t even need the presence of a ligand (EGF) for dimerization and activation. The process is no longer regulated, and the cell proliferation becomes independent. It results in the activation of the MAPK pathway, which promotes cell proliferation and angiogenesis.

Oncogenic addiction

Once the tumor gets this mutation, it becomes addicted to it. It d epends on this pathway for proliferation and survival. In normal cells, there are alternative pathways, but tumors become highly dependent on this enhanced pathway. This is known as oncogenic

addiction.

Medically, oncogenic addiction is very interesting, as blocking this proliferation pathway can have a high effect on the proliferation of the tumor. By using TKI (tyrosine kinase inhibitors), such as Getifinib (which blocks the ATP binding site), we block the EGFR activity.

Primary resistance vs acquired resistance

- Primary resistance: clinical situation in which a malignant tumor does not respond to a specific drug. It can be caused by mutations in KRAS, Mdm2,… KRAS mutations are mainly associated with primary resistance, as a mutation in KRAS implies that the pathway is always active, independently from EGFR.

The activation is continuous even if the tyrosine kinase has been inhibited. A patient with a

KRAS mutation can’t be treated with EGFR

TKIs, as it won’t have any effect in suppressing proliferation.

[PDF p. 293]

Secondary resistance: the most important type of mutation which causes EGFR drugs secondary/acquired resistance is the mutation in T790M, located in the tyrosine-kinase domain. It’s a missense somatic mutation, resulting in threonine being interchanged by methionine. It inhibits Getinifib binding, as it doesn’t allow the drug to enter the binding site.

Osimertinib can be prescribed in the presence of this mutation, as it can block the ATP binding site even when the patient presents this mutation. To prescribe osimertinib, we must first sequence the tumor DNA to detect the specific mutation/alteration. T790M mutations account for 60% of secondary resistance mutations. Additionally, osimertinib can cross the blood-brain barrier

and reach metastasis sites in the brain.

Patients can also develop resistance to osimertinib, so we would carry out another tumor sequencing to detect any additional mutations.

In the case of our patient, an additional activating mutation in RAF was detected. This is a way the tumor has to evade the blockage of EGFR, as it activates the MAPK pathway without the need of the EGFR tyrosine kinase. The patient was treated with dabrafenib (B-RAF inhibitor) and trametinib (MEK inhibitor), plus osimertinib, to block the pathway by attacking many mutation points. The tumor

showed increased reduction.

The patient sadly died, but if there is regression, the tumor could have also continued to develop mutations and activating pathways which we would have had to block. All this is known as targeted therapy, a type of personalized/precision medicine to treat particular types of cancers in particular types of patients.
